section

Chapter 0
Abstract

Reasoning about weak higher categorical structures constitutes a challenging task, even to the experts. One principal reason is that the language of set theory is not invariant under the weaker notions of equivalence at play, such as homotopy equivalence. From this point of view, it is natural to ask for a different foundational setting which more natively supports these notions.

A possible approach along these lines has been given by Riehl–Shulman in 2017 where they have developed a theory of synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categories. For this purpose they have introduced an extension of
homotopy type theory/univalent foundations (HoTT/UF)
. Pioneered by Voevodsky, this logical system is designed to develop homotopy theory in a
synthetic
way, meaning that its basic entities can essentially be understood as topological spaces, or more precisely
homotopy types
. As per Voevodsky’s
Univalence Axiom
, homotopy equivalence between types coincides with logical equivalence. As a consequence, a lot of the conceptual ideas from classical homotopy theory can be imported to reason more intrinsically about homotopical structures.

In fact, in 2019 Shulman achieved to prove the long-standing conjecture that any higher topos (in the sense of Grothendieck–Rezk–Lurie) gives rise to a model of homotopy type theory. This gives a precise technical sense in which HoTT can be regarded as a kind of internal language of
(\infty,1)
-toposes
\mathscr{E}
. By analogy, Riehl–Shulman’s extension, called
simplicial homotopy type theory (sHoTT)
can be interpreted in diagram
(\infty,1)
-toposes of the form
\mathscr{E}^{{\text{\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{\includegraphics[height]{simplex}}}}^{\Op}}
. Syntactic additions make it possible to reason type-theoretically about internal
(\infty,1)
-categories implemented as (complete) Segal objects.

Based on Riehl–Shulman’s work about synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categories and discrete covariant fibrations, we present a theory of co-/cartesian fibrations including sliced and two-sided versions. The study is informed by Riehl–Verity’s work on model-independent
\infty
-category theory, and transfers results of their
\infty
-cosmoses to the type-theoretic setting. We prove characterization theorems for cocartesian fibrations and their generalizations, given as existence conditions of certain adjoint functors (
Chevalley criteria
). Extending the author’s previous joint work with Buchholtz, we prove several closure properties of two-sided cartesian fibrations as well as a two-sided Yoneda Lemma. Furthermore, we discuss so-called Beck–Chevalley (bi)fibrations and prove a synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categorical version of Moens’ Theorem, after Streicher.

Finally, we show how to interpret Riehl–Shulman’s strict extension types in the intended higher topos model, in such a way that makes them strictly stable under substitution. This uses a method, originally due to Voevodsky, which has previously been employed in the works of e.g. Kapulkin, Lumsdaine, Warren, Awodey, Streicher, and Shulman.

Our work takes up on suggestions in the original article by Riehl–Shulman to further develop synthetic
(\infty,1)
-category theory in simplicial HoTT, including in particular the study of cocartesian fibrations. Together with a collection of analytic results, notably due to Riehl–Verity and Rasekh, it follows that our type-theoretic account constitutes a synthetic theory of fibrations of internal
(\infty,1)
-categories.

Chapter 1
Zusammenfassung

Der Umgang mit schwachen unendlichdimensionalen Kategorien stellt selbst für Expert*innen eine Herausforderung dar. Dies begründet sich schon auf grundsätzlicher Ebene damit, dass die Sprache der Mengenlehre nicht invariant unter den Äquivalenzbegriffen dieser schwachen, höherdimensionalen Strukturen ist, wie z.B. Homotopieäquivalenz. Unter diesem Gesichtspunkt ist es naheliegend, nach einer alternativen Grundlagentheorie zu suchen, die mit den homotopietheoretischen Begriffen besser kompatibel ist.

Ein möglicher Ansatz wurde 2017 von Riehl–Shulman in Form einer synthetischen Theorie von
(\infty,1)
-Kategorien vorgeschlagen. Zu diesem Zweck haben sie eine Erweiterung der sogenannten
Homotopie-Typentheorie
bzw.
univalenten Grundlagen
(engl.
homotopy type theory/univalent foundations (HoTT/UF)
) eingeführt. Hierbei handelt es sich um ein logisches System, entscheidend geprägt durch Wojewodski, das eine
synthetische
Grundlage für Homotopietheorie bieten soll: Die grundlegenden Objekte tragen bereits eine topologische Struktur. Genauer gesagt, handelt es sich um
Homotopietypen
. Wojewodskis
Univalenz-Axiom
bewirkt, dass in diesem System Homotopieäquivalenz mit logischer Äquivalenz übereinstimmt. Dies hat zur Folge, dass viele konzeptionelle Ideen aus der klassischen Homotopietheorie benutzt werden können, um eine synthetische Theorie aufzubauen.

Ein entscheidender Beitrag wurde 2019 von Shulman geleistet, der bewies, dass jeder höherdimensionale Topos (im Sinne von Grothendieck–Rezk–Lurie) Anlass zu einem Modell von Homotopie-Typentheorie gibt. Diese Vermutung war lange Zeit offen. Nach diesem Resultat kann man HoTT in einem genauen technischen Sinne als eine Art interne Sprache beliebiger
(\infty,1)
-Topoi
\mathscr{E}
verstehen. Analog dazu lässt sich Riehl–Shulmans Erweiterung, die sog.
simpliziale Homotopietypentheorie (sHoTT)
, in Diagramm-
(\infty,1)
-Topoi der Form
\mathscr{E}^{{\text{\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{\includegraphics[height]{simplex}}}}^{\Op}}
interpretieren. Vermöge syntaktischer Erweiterungen erlaubt die Theorie die Behandlung interner
(\infty,1)
-Kategorien, modelliert durch sogenannte (vollständige) Segal-Objekte.

Ausgehend von Riehl–Shulmans Arbeit über synthetische
(\infty,1)
-Kategorien und diskrete kovariante Fibrationen entwickeln wir eine Theorie ko-/kartesischer Fibrationen, die auch gefaserte und zweiseitige Verallgemeinerungen erfasst. Dabei dienen Riehl–Veritys Arbeiten über modellunabhängige
(\infty,1)
-Kategorientheorie als wichtige Grundlage, deren Resultate für
\infty
-Kosmoi wir auf den typentheoretischen Kontext übertragen. Wir geben Charakterisierungssätze für kokartesische Fibrationen und ihre Verallgemeinerungen an, die als Existenzsätze bestimmter adjungierter Funktoren formuliert sind
(Chevalley-Kriterien)
. Gemeinsame Vorarbeiten mit Buchholtz verallgemeinernd zeigen wir eine Auswahl von Abschlusseigenschaften zweiseitiger kartesischer Fibrationen sowie ein zweiseitiges Yoneda-Lemma. Desweiteren diskutieren wir sogenannte Beck–Chevalley-(Bi-)Fibrationen und beweisen eine synthetische
(\infty,1)
-kategorielle Version des Satzes von Moens, basierend auf einem Beweis von Streicher.

Abschließend führen wir eine Kohärenzkonstruktion für Riehl–Shulmans strikte Erweiterungstypen durch, sodass diese auch in den homotopietheoretischen Topos-Modellen strikt substitutionsstabil interpretiert werden können. Diese Methode geht auf Wojewodski zurück und fand Anwendung in Arbeiten von z.B. Kapulkin, Lumsdaine, Warren, Awodey, Streicher und Shulman.

Unsere Arbeit folgt Vorschlägen aus dem ursprünglichen Artikel von Riehl–Shulman, die synthetische
(\infty,1)
-Kategorientheorie in simplizialer Homotopie-Typentheorie weiterzuentwickeln, insbesondere bezüglich kokartesischer Fibrationen. Zusammen mit einer Reihe analytischer Resultate, v.a. von Riehl–Verity und Rasekh, folgt aus den genannten Betrachtungen, dass unser typentheoretischer Zugang eine synthetische Theorie von Fibrationen interner
(\infty,1)
-Kategorien darstellt.

Chapter 3
Summary and overview

1
Summary of the results

The
synthetic perspective on
(\infty,1)
-categories
alluded to in the title is not new to this thesis, but has been established previously in
[
RS17
]
—with the central idea of using as a model the
(\infty,1)
-topos of simplicial spaces, in order to later in the type theory carve out the (complete) Segal types independently suggested by Joyal as well. The thesis presents further developments along these lines, relying strongly on aspects of the highly expansive foundational work in
[
RV22
]
, transferring it to the type-theoretic setting.

For the most part, this dissertation concerns the synthetic development of
fibered
(\infty,1)
-category theory
. Despite the discussion of the actual semantics making up only a small portion of the text, we still have chosen to mention the
semantics
in the title, to emphasize the immediate semantical analogies. Unlike other possibly more syntactically motivated approaches to synthetic higher category theory, simplicial HoTT is clearly semantically motivated. With the right ambient logical and “analytic” theory preestablished, one readily sees that the (complete) Rezk types are clearly interpreted as (complete) Segal objects. Particularly, using the
\infty
-cosmological, formal higher catgeorical perspective, more intricate notions such as adjunctions, cartesian fibrations
etc
. also translate to their correct semantic counterparts.

In the following, we summarize the results of each thesis chapter. For the general narrative we refer to the non-technical introduction
Chapter

4
.

1
Co-/cartesian families

In
[
RS17
]
, Riehl and Shulman have developed a synthetic account to left fibrations of
(\infty,1)
-categories. A left fibration
E\twoheadrightarrow B
presents a (covariant) presheaf
B\to{\mathrm{Space}}
, valued in the
(\infty,1)
-category of (small)
\infty
-groupoids (
aka
spaces)—the
\infty
-categorical analogue of the category of sets. We introduce a notion of
cocartesian families
,
i.e.
covariant presheaves
B\to{\mathrm{Cat}}
valued in the
(\infty,1)
-category of (small)
(\infty,1)
-categories.
1
1

1

Note that this is to be read
cum grano salis
: At that point, we do not have a satisfactory internal treatment of the classifiers
{\mathrm{Space}}
and
{\mathrm{Cat}}
yet. This means, a type-theoretic account of these categorical universes is still under way. But these objects exist externally as is well known from “analytic” higher-category theory, cf.
[
Ras18
]
specifically for the case of (complete) Segal spaces. In fact, for the “naive” universes defined as
\Sigma
-types such as
\sum_{X:\mathcal{U}}\isRezk(X)
or
\sum_{X:\mathcal{U}}\lx@nobreakspace\isDisc(X)
it is
not derivable
that they be (complete) Segal. This has been observed in simplicial spaces initially by Shulman, and later verified for a few low-dimensional special cases in unpublished work by Buchholtz and the author
[
BW18
]
. Already for discrete types in simplicial reflexive graphs, the naive universe contains “too many arrows” to be Segal.
This is based on previous joint work of the author together with Buchholtz
[
BW21
]
, which contains some parts that also occur in this thesis. However, the proofs presented here have been established by the author of the thesis. To provide an essentially self-contained treatise, we also have included an expository section from
[
BW21
, Section 2]
in the thesis at hand as well as, occasionally, some other expository paragraphs from
loc. cit.
These have originally been written by the thesis author as well.

Generalizing classical results, and paralleling the study of
[
RV22
, Chapter 5]
, cocartesian type families are defined as type families where one can lift arrows in the base type to dependent cocartesian arrows in the family (or, equivalently in the total type). We prove that this, as expected, can be equivalently expressed as a criterion involving the existence of a certain left adjoint right inverse (abbrev.
LARI
),
aka

Chevalley criterion
, and a different condition postulating the existence of a fibered left adjoint. These results are extended to cocartesian functors between cocartesian fibrations. These characterizations have immediate important consequences, since they entail various closure properties of cocartesian families and functors, as detailed in
[
BW21
]
. In particular, these are a type-theoretic version of the closure properties of cocartesian fibrations in
\infty
-cosmos theory.

Moreover, compared to
[
BW21
]
, we present an alternative account to
cocartesian arrows
, more along the lines of
[
RV22
, Section 5.1]
. Namely, as recently discovered by Riehl and Verity, cocartesian
arrows
can also be characterized in terms of a Chevalley criterion in terms of
relative adjunctions
à la Ulmer (which are briefly treated in the appendix). This yields a more formal proof of the closure properties of cocartesian arrows, and ties in with the discussion of “LARI fibrations” as formal generalizations of cocartesian fibrations.

This treatment of cocartesian arrows has been suggested to us by Riehl.

Note that analogous results for cartesian fibrations,
i.e.
contravariant
{\mathrm{Cat}}
-valued
\infty
-presheaves, follow by (manual) dualization. Since at present, the type theory at hand lacks a type former for the opposite category, contravariant maps over
B
still have to be encoded as families over
B
, with contravariance produced by the dual Chevalley criterion. In particular, this gives rise to a notion of cartesian and simultaneously cocartesian
aka

bi-cartesian
family.

2
Beck–Chevalley families and Moens’ Theorem

As an application of our theory of synthetic cocartesian families, we provide an account of Beck–Chevalley fibrations and Moens’ Theorem by adapting Streicher’s methods
[
Str21
, Section 15]
from the
1
-categorical case to our type-theoretic setting. A
Beck–Chevalley fibration
is a bicartesian fibration which moreover satisfies a fibrational version of the traditional Beck–Chevalley condition, crucial e.g. in categorical logic. If, moreover a Beck–Chevalley fibration is lex and satisfies some further conditions, generalizing the so-called
extensivity property
for categories, then it is called a
lextensive
or
Moens fibration
. By Moens’ Theorem, lextensive fibrations over a given base
B
are classified by lex functors
from

B
to some lex category.

Although the Beck–Chevalley condition has had some appearance in
(\infty,1)
-category theory
[
HL13
]
, this, to our knowledge, has not been the case for Moens’ Theorem so far. In the classical theory, Moens’ Theorem plays an important role for the so-called
fibered view of geometric morphisms

[
Str97
,
Str21
]
. Our generalization to the case of Rezk types externalizes to a version of Moens’ Theorem for internal
\infty
-categories in an arbitrary
\infty
-topos.
2
2

2

in the sense of Grothendieck–Rezk–Lurie
We hope that this yields a starting point for further investigations along these lines in higher topos theory—be they synthetic or analytic.

3
Two-sided cartesian families

We generalize our study of co-/cartesian families
E\twoheadrightarrow A
to the
2
-variable case of spans
A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\xi}}{{\twoheadleftarrow}}E\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\twoheadrightarrow}}B
with
mixed variance
. This constitutes a theory of
two-sided cartesian fibrations
, where one leg of such a span
\xi:E\twoheadrightarrow A
is cocartesian,
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
is cartesian, and some further compatibility conditions between the lifts are required. Morally,
3
3

3

Recall, that we do not currently have an “
\mathrm{op}
” in our theory.
such a two-sided fibration encodes a functor
B^{\Op}\times A\to{\mathrm{Cat}}
. This also has a discrete version, called
distributor
or
(bi-)module
,
4
4

4

Common synonyms also include
profunctor
or
relator
, since they can be seen as relations between categories, cf.
[
Bén00
]
.
where the legs are
discrete
co-/cartesian fibrations, resp. Accordingly, a distributor can be presented as a functor
B^{\Op}\times A\to{\mathrm{Space}}
or as a
presheaf of copresheaves

B^{\Op}\to A\to{\mathrm{Space}}
. The prime example of a distributor is given by the hom-bifunctor
\hom_{A}:A^{\Op}\times A\to{\mathrm{Space}}
.

Analogously to our study of cocartesian families, cf. also
[
BW21
]
, we develop the theory of two-sided cartesian families and their discrete versions in simplicial type theory, after Riehl–Verity’s installment for
\infty
-cosmoses
[
RV22
, Chapter 7]
. To achieve a similar analysis, we have to give some rather explicit discussions of fibered or
sliced
notions of synthetic co-/cartesian fibrations.
5
5

5

This is particularly owed to the fact that we currently do not have the appropriate categorical universes at hand, so we cannot reason about fibrations as literally being objects of some
\infty
-category, which is in contrast to
\infty
-comos theory.
The payoff will be a systematic analysis of the notions of two-sided fibrations and functors à la
[
RV22
]
, encompassing namely Chevalley-like characterization theorems and closure properties. Furthermore, we generalize the synthetic Yoneda Lemmas from
[
RS17
,
BW21
]
from the non-/discrete, one-sided case to a synthetic version of the two-sided case, as given in
[
RV22
, Section 7.3]
.

This involves some results about fibered (LARI) fibrations between Rezk types, generalizing
[
RS17
, Section 11]
and
[
BW21
, Appendix B]
which are developed in the appendix.

In our setting, we allude to internal versions of the operations of this (externally present) double-category.

4
Semantics: Strict stability of extension type formers

By the results of Riehl–Shulman
[
RS17
, Appendix A]
, simplicial type theory can be modeled in suitable model structures equipped with an appropriate shape theory. However, their study technically does not include universe types. On the other hand, building on previous work, Shulman
[
Shu19
]
established positively the long-standing conjecture that any Grothendieck
(\infty,1)
-topos gives rise to a model of homotopy type theory with universes strictly à la Tarski.

Let
\mathscr{E}
be an
(\infty,1)
-topos. Then it follows that the
(\infty,1)
-topos
\mathrm{s}\mathscr{E}:=\mathscr{E}^{{{\text{\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{\includegraphics[height]{simplex}}}}}^{\Op}}
of simplicial objects in
\mathscr{E}

almost
gives rise to a model of simplicial homotopy type theory. In light of the previous work, what’s missing
6
6

6

setting aside questions about initiality of syntax
is a construction interpreting the extension types à la Riehl–Shulman in such a way that they are strictly stable under substitution.

We provide such a construction generalizing a splitting method originally due to Voevodsky
[
Voe09
,
Voe17
]
, and later well investigated for HoTT by
[
KL21
,
LW15
,
Awo18
]
.

Since in the models
\mathrm{s}\mathscr{E}
, all the shapes are fibrant, we simplify our setting so as to work completely inside the model structure presenting
\mathrm{s}\mathscr{E}
. The advantage is that we can work inside an ordinary comprehension category, rather than deal with extra fibered, possibly non-fibrant structure, and separate classifiers for the “cofibrations”. This should make the construction as transparent as possible, and also ready for adaptation to technically more intricate settings.

We conclude by discussing how our synthetic type-theoretic notions translate in the models
\mathrm{s}\mathscr{E}
. In particular, any
\infty
-topos
\mathscr{E}
gives rise to an
\infty
-cosmos of
Rezk objects
internal to
\mathscr{E}
, which can be understood as the
internal
(\infty,1)
-categories in
\mathscr{E}
.
7
7

7

cf.
[
RS17
, Example A.14]
Thus, simplicial HoTT captures (some of) the synthetic theory of internal
(\infty,1)
-categories in an
\infty
-topos, similarly to how standard HoTT captures (some of) the synthetic theory of homotopy types in an
\infty
-topos.

2
Structure of the thesis

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Ch.~1}}}

Structure of the thesis

In
Figure

1
, we suggest a few paths to take reading this thesis. A “complete tour” is suggested by the regular arrows “
\rightarrow
”. One can take detours over the appendices as indicated via “
\dashrightarrow
”, or simply consult them as needed. It is also possible to do straight “jumps” via the squiggly arrows “
\rightsquigarrow
”, depending on individual interest. However, in any case we do recommend reading
Chapter

4
(and
Chapter

5
) to set the stage.
Chapters

7
and
8
are not dependent on each other, but they both rely on
Chapter

6
where co-/cartesian fibrations are introduced.
Chapter

9
is the part primarily concerned with semantics. As such it can be read on its own right. A conclusion and an outlook can be found in
Chapter

10
. The appendices contain mainly technical results. Namely,
Chapter

11
provides a treatise of a version of relative adjunctions after Ulmer
[
Ulm68
]
. This is also interesting from a conceptual point of view because it gives a more general perspective on cocartesian arrows. Finally, in
Chapter

12
we collect several results on fibered (LARI) adjunctions and equivalences, as well as some sliced constructions that will be used often in the main text.

4
Related work

Apart from the original paper by Riehl–Shulman
[
RS17
]
there is follow-up work on directed univalence in simplicial HoTT by Cavallo–Riehl–Sattler
[
RCS18
]
. Directed Univalence in a bi-
cubical
type theory has been studied by Weaver–Licata
[
WL20
]
where they prove directed univalence for the classifier of covariant discrete families, with ongoing work towards directed HITs
[
Wea21
]
.

Inspired by Voevodsky’s
homotopy type system (HTS)
, higher categories have been studied type-theoretically in
2-level type theory (2LTT)

[
ACKS19
,
Cap17
]
. In this approach, one considers simultaneously a layer for HoTT and a layer for extensional type theory validating UIP, together with a cocercion function from the former to the latter.

Directed versions of (homotopy) type theory have been studied by several people
[
War13
,
LH11
,
Lic11
,
Nuy15
,
Nuy19
,
Nor19
,
SA19
]
. Kavvos discusses ideas towards directed type theory capturing two-sided fibrations in
[
Kav19
]
. On the semantic side, there are ongoing investigations by Cisinski and Nguyen
[
Cis20
,
Ngu19a
,
Ngu19
]
about directed univalence. There is also ongoing joint work by North, van den Berg, and McCloskey about directed (algebraic) weak factorization systems
[
Nor21
]
.

As an overarching theory, model-independent foundations of higher category theory have been established in Riehl–Verity’s
\infty
-cosmos theory
[
RV22
]
.

Preceding work to this thesis has been done by Buchholtz in joint work with the thesis author
[
BW21
]
, parts of which make up
Chapter

6
of this thesis.

Furthermore, a proof assistant for simplicial type theory is being developed by Kudasov
[
Kud21
]
.

Chapter 4
Introduction

1
Higher structures in mathematics

Higher homotopical structures have been gaining relevance in modern-day mathematics. For example, in classical algebraic topology, the fundamental group of a pointed space is a very important invariant. But this construction loses information about the higher homotopies. Furthermore, it can be argued that its construction is not particularly natural: it relies on choosing a basepoint, parametrizations of loops, and involves taking a quotient. An improvement over this is given by the
fundamental
\infty
-groupoid
or
singular Kan complex
of a space, whose
n
-simplices are given by the
n
-dimensional paths in the space simply by definition.

The increasing complexity of mathematical objects studied also adds to the complexity of the structures formed by these objects. This is already visible at the level of bare sets: the collection of (small) sets naturally forms a (large) groupoid rather than a (large) set again, since the preferred notion of identity is
isomorphism
rather than plain equality on the nose. This is true even more so for more complicated objects. Many objects in areas such as topology, algebraic geometry, differential geometry or even theoretical computer science naturally form
weak higher-dimensional category
. In this case, contrasting ordinary
1
-dimensional categories, composition is defined in some weaker sense than as an operation on sets, and the respective laws do not hold equationally but up to coherence as witnessed by some higher-dimensional morphisms—possibly in arbitrarily high dimensions.

But the problem when reasoning about weak structures of this kind in traditional set-theoretic mathematics is that the definitions tend to become very involved. Since at the fundamental level set-theory is dealing with honest equality, these weak higher-dimensional structures have to be built out of rigid ones, often producing very involved combinatorics and the necessity to keep track of higher coherences in an explicit way. In addition, for most of these higher structures of interest there do exist various different mathematical implementations which are equivalent in some precise technical sense, but hard to compare ‘‘directly’’---not least because they do live in different categories. A remarkable amount of traditional lower-dimensional category and topos theory has been successfully generalized to weak higher-dimensional categories, producing new ramifications not present in the lower-dimensional case. But because of the different ‘‘competing’’ models
1
1

1

in the sense of homotopy theory, not logic/model theory
it has always been a challenge to develop the theory in a “uniform” manner: indeed, it is often the case that those arguments involve switching back and forth between different models—or, despite coming from traditional
1
-categorical ideas initially, one gets caught up in the challenging technicalities of the chosen model when making things fully precise.

2
Model-independent
\infty
-category theory

On the one hand, this situation does have the advantage that one can deliberately exploit the different practical advantages of one model over the other. On the other hand, for both technical and philosophical reasons, it seems desirable to aim for unification in the theory of higher categories and homotopical structures, at least to the extent of making the parallels to the classical theory as transparent as possible. One approach to this has been the long-standing program of Riehl and Verity, who have developed a vastly comprehensive theory of higher categories via
\infty
-cosmoses
. The idea is to work
synthetically
: instead of trying to axiomatize the definition of a higher category, they do so for the supposed
structures
the higher categories should live an. An
\infty
-category
, of whatever flavor,
2
2

2

e.g.
(\infty,n)
-categories for
0\leq n\leq\infty
is then simply an object of the respective
\infty
-cosmos. E.g. for the very prominent case of
(\infty,1)
-categories this provides a unified, model-independent treatment encompassing simultaneously their different incarnations as quasi-categories, complete Segal spaces, Segal categories, and
1
-complicial sets. A crucial aspect of this theory is that a lot of the arguments (sometimes with extra care) can even be carried out in the
homotopy
2
-category
of the
\infty
-cosmos—a strict
2
-categorical quotient, comparable by analogy to the homotopy (
1
-)category of a model category. For instance, remarkably the data of a
2
-adjunction in the homotopy
2
-category lifts to define a fully homotopy coherent adjunctions in the
\infty
-cosmos
[
RV16
]
. Furthermore, there is a notion of cosmological functor and cosmological biequivalence between
\infty
-cosmoses which allows to transfer constructions and theorems across different models. The slogan is that equivalent
\infty
-cosmoses have equivalent
\infty
-category theories (
Model Independence Theorem
,
[
RV22
, Theorem 11.1.6]
). Again, this is analogous to how model categories encode the same homotopy theories in case they are Quillen equivalent.

An
\infty
-cosmos is defined as an
(\infty,2)
-category with certain
(\infty,2)
-categorical limits.
3
3

3

In technical terms, it is presented as a (sufficiently complete) fibration category, enriched over the Joyal-model structure of quasi-categories.
They have succesfully been used to develop a synthetic
\infty
-category theory, however, using structures that are based on set-theoretic mathematics and previously known “analytic” results about concrete models of
(\infty,1)
-categories. This development has evolved quite far, but one can still ask: is there an approach to
\infty
-category theory
within
a “homotopy-invariant” theory? An approach in this direction is
homotopy type theory (HoTT)
, a logical system, which has developed in parallel to the rapidly expanding study of higher categories in the early 2000s.

3
Homotopy type theory and univalent foundations

Going back to Bertrand Russell, type theory was devised in the early 1900s as a logical system to avoid logical paradoxes that at shook the foundations of mathematics at the time. The basic entities in a type theory are called
types

A,B,C,\ldots
and they can have
elements
(also:
inhabitants
or
terms
). Basic judgments consist e.g. of declarations of the form

\lx@nobreakspace x:A

saying that
x
is an element of type
A
. The logical content of a type theory is given by a set of rules governing standard procedures such as weakening, substitution
etc
. Out of given types, new types can be formed, again through given rules that capture the (universal) properties that the newly constructed types should have. E.g. given two types
A,B
one might form their product
A\times B
, coproduct
A+B
or function type
A\to B
. This is different to the more
materialist
spirit of set theory, since it captures a more
structuralist
style of doing reasoning and constructions.

Of central importance is a variant due to Martin-Löf
[
Mar75
]
, named after its creator. Martin-Löf type theory MLTT is a constructive dependent type theory (
i.e.
it captures
type families
depending on other types) that internalizes proof-relevant equality using so-called
identity types

\Id_{A}
. In fact, when developing even simple fragments of arithmetic inside MLTT, one will encounter that a lot of expected equalities between elements
a,b:A
do not hold
judgmentally
(
i.e.
on the nose), but only
propositionally
, as witnessed by a proof term
p:\Id_{A}(a,b)
. Compared to a strict equality judgment
a\equiv b
, the identity type
\Id_{A}(a,b)
in general contains much richer information since two elements may be equal or isomorphic to each other in more than one way. In particular, this makes MLTT an
intensional
type theory.
4
4

4

It can be made
extensional
by reflecting propositional equality into judgmental equality, but this renders type-checking undecidable.

Any type
A
admits a family of identity types indexed over
A\times A
. Since any
\Id_{A}(a,b)
is again a type, this construction can be iterated to yield a hierarchy of types capturing identity proofs between identity proofs (between identity proofs
etc.
):

A,\Id_{A},\Id_{\Id_{A}},\Id_{\Id_{\Id_{A}}},\ldots

One can now ask: In MLTT, given elements
a,b:A
and two proofs
p,q:\Id_{A}(a,b)
, is it derivable that there is an identity proof of identity proofs in the next dimension,
i.e.
a term
\alpha:\Id_{\Id_{A}(a,b)}(p,q)
? This was answered negatively in 1994 by Hofmann and Streicher who constructed the groupoid model
[
HS94
]
which refuted the claim. In this model, types were interpreted as groupoids, families of types as fibrations of groupoids, and identity types as the sets of isomorphisms. In fact, a motivation to come up with this model was the observation that, inside MLTT, the identity types give rise to define a groupoid-like structure on every type. The units are given by the reflexivity proofs
{\mathrm{refl}}_{A,a}:\Id_{A}(a,a)
, and one can define maps
\mathrm{comp}_{A,a,b,c}:\Id_{A}(b,c)\to\Id_{A}(a,b)\to\Id_{A}(a,c)
and
\mathrm{inv}_{A,a,b}:\Id_{A}(a,b)\to\Id_{A}(b,a)
, resp., for composition and inversion, resp. However, the expected groupoid laws only hold up to higher propositional equalities.

All these considerations suggested the slogan that “types are (weak) higher-dimensional groupoids”. This is also reminiscent to Grothendieck’s
Homotopy Hypothesis
stating that “spaces are higher-dimensional groupoids”. Around 2006, independently Voevodsky and Streicher suggested to model intensional type theory in
Kan complexes
since those were known from categorical homotopy theory as a notion of
\infty
-groupoids. This connected well to previous work by Awodey–Warren
[
AW09
]
who had shown that MLTT can be seen as an internal language of any model category, interpreting type families as fibrations, and interpreting identity types as factorizations of diagonals. Furthermore, van den Berg and Garner showed in 2008 that, internally in MLTT, each type can be regarded as an
\omega
-groupoid in the sense of Batanin. Also in 2008, Gambino and Garner showed that every identity type gives rise to a weak factorization system on the syntactic category, cf. also later work by Emmenegger
[
Emm18
]
.

Further work by Voevodsky and Kapulkin–Lumsdaine up to 2012 led to establishing a model of
Homotopy Type Theory (HoTT)
, an extended version of MLTT, in the Kan model structure of simplicial sets. A crucial addition due to Voevodsky is the so-called
Univalence Axiom
which roughly can be subsumed under the slogan
Isomorphic types are equal
.

Since the Kan model structure is also a presentation of the
(\infty,1)
-topos of spaces, Awodey had generalized the internal language correspondence for MLTT and model categories to the following conjecture: Any
(\infty,1)
-topos admits a model of homotopy type theory.

A lot of work about the semantics of HoTT has been done in the past decade, and is still ongoing. This very notably includes Shulman’s positive confirmation of Awodey’s conjecture in
[
Shu19
]
. Statements of these kind prove particularly challenging, since to achieve a model of type theory, the interpretation has to be given in a coherent way that is stable under substitution up to honest set-theoretic equality. This is nontrivial for model categories or similar structures, because these typically do not come with distinguished, coherent choices.

On the internal or synthetic side, a lot of progress has been going on working internally in HoTT to develop synthetic accounts to homotopy theory. Many results have successfully been formalized and verified in a computer proof assistant.

4
Synthetic
(\infty,1)
-category theory in simplicial homotopy type theory

Given the discussion up to this point, one might ask if HoTT was suited as a homotopy-invariant language for reasoning about higher categories.

However, reasoning about higher category theories in HoTT is somehow underdeveloped to this date. A related problem is defining homotopy-coherent structures directly in HoTT, which has still been proven hard in general over the years. There do exist approaches, e.g. in
Two-level type theory
(going back to Voevodsky’s
Homotopy Type System (HTS)
). There one adds another
extensional/non-fibrant layer
to the theory, allowing to work partially “classically” as needed, as opposed to solely up to homotopy.

Another approach has been given in
[
RS17
]
under the name of
Simplicial Homotopy Type Theory (sHoTT)
. Somewhat similarly, one also adds extra layers of non-fibrant pre-types to the theory. These shall capture the
shapes
known from simplicial homotopy theory, generated by the standard simplices
\Delta^{n}
and their subpolytopes (such as the boundaries
\partial\Delta^{n}
or the horns
\Lambda_{k}^{n}
). As a further gadget, one adds to the theory type formers which capture
strict
extensions of (partial) sections in a family along a shape inclusion. For instance, this allows for defining the hom-types of a type
A
as

\lx@nobreakspace\hom_{A}(x,y)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\left\langle\Delta^{1}\to A\middle|^{\partial\Delta^{1}}_{[x,y]}\right\rangle,

whose terms are directed arrows
f:\Delta^{1}\to A
that coincide
strictly
with
x
and
y
on the boundary,
f\,0\equiv x
and
f\,1\equiv y
. Such a type, with strict computational behavior, is not definable in standard HoTT, where instead these equalities would only hold up to paths
p:\Id_{A}(f\,0,x)
and
q:\Id_{A}(f\,1,y)
, as a consequence producing unwieldy higher coherences.
5
5

5

This setup also has some parallels with developments of
Cubical Type Theories

[
BCH14
,
CCHM18
]
due to Coquand
et al.
, cf.
[
RS17
, Remark 3.2]
.

In this setting, the way to reason about synthetic
\infty
-categories is as follows. Using the extension types, one can form in particular function types such as
A^{\Phi}
for any (non-fibrant) tope
\Phi
. Using Voevodsky’s notion of
type-theoretic weak equivalence

f:X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}Y
, Riehl–Shulman define a (simplicial) type
A
to be a
synthetic pre-
(\infty,1)
-category
or
Segal type
if the induced map

A^{\Delta^{2}}\to A^{\Lambda_{1}^{2}}

is a weak equivalence. This map restricts any
2
-simplex (triangle with filled interior) in
A
to the sub-diagram of shape
(\bullet\to\bullet\to\bullet)
(
(2,1)
-horn
). Hence, this restriction being a weak equivalence means that, in
A
, any such pair of composable arrows possesses a composite, uniquely up to contractibility (
i.e.
the space of all possible such composition data is homotopy-equivalent to the point).

This constitutes a synthetic version of
Segal spaces
. Adding the so-called
Rezk-completeness
condition
6
6

6

Namely, this says that “categorical isomorphism” (defined through the hom-types) coincides with “homotopy equivalence” (given through the identity type). For this reason, the condition can also be called “local unvialence”, after Voevodsky’s Univalence Axiom.
gives rise to
synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categories
or
complete Segal types
, or
Rezk types
.

Analytically, Rezk spaces are known to also present
(\infty,1)
-categories, just as quasi-categories do, but based on
bisimplicial
sets, rather than simplicial sets. This idea was also independently suggested by Joyal.

Recall that HoTT can be modeled in the Kan model structure on simplicial sets
{\mathbf{sSet}}=\mathbf{Set}^{{{\text{\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{\includegraphics[height]{simplex}}}}}^{\Op}}
, presenting the
(\infty,1)
-topos of spaces
\mathscr{S}
. Analogously, simplicial HoTT can be modeled in the so-called
Reedy model structure
on bisimplicial sets
\mathbf{Set}^{{{\text{\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{\includegraphics[height]{simplex}}}}}^{\Op}\times{{\text{\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{\includegraphics[height]{simplex}}}}}^{\Op}}\cong{\mathbf{sSet}}^{{{\text{\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{\includegraphics[height]{simplex}}}}}^{\Op}}
, presenting the
(\infty,1)
-topos
\mathscr{S}^{{{\text{\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{\includegraphics[height]{simplex}}}}}^{\Op}}
of simplicial spaces. The simplicial types, interpreted by Reedy fibrant simplicial spaces, do not have intrinsic meaning for us per se. But importantly, they are presented by a model structure interpreting all of HoTT.
7
7

7

Interpreting directly in the model structure presenting Rezk spaces is not possible, because it does not support general
\Pi
-types, as is also the case with the Joyal model structure on simplicial sets, capturing the quasi-categories.
In our consideration, we will often restrict to (complete) Segal types, which can be done internally in the newly extended theory, since (complete) Segal-ness becomes a definable predicate.

In
[
RS17
]
Riehl–Shulman have developed a lot of basic synthetic
(\infty,1)
-category theory in this setting, including a study of
\infty
-(co-)presheaves and adjunctions. We present here extensions of this development, on the one hand taking place in the type-theoretic setting, on the other hand adapting and aiming to parallel parts of the theory in
\infty
-cosmoses from Riehl–Verity
[
RV22
]
.

Chapter 5
Preliminaries on synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categories

This section is essentially taken from the author’s joint work with Buchholtz
[
BW21
, Section 2]
, to make the presentation of the thesis as self-contained as possible w.r.t. to the variations and peculiarities of sHoTT
[
RS17
]
that are relevant for our treatise.

The parts present here have originally been written by the author of this thesis as well.

1
Exposition of Riehl–Shulman’s synthetic

(\infty,1)
-category theory

We recall some basic features and results from Riehl–Shulman’s synthetic
(\infty,1)
-category theory
[
RS17
]
, at a very brief and informal level. A significantly more thorough treatment is provided in the original paper. For a general introduction to homotopy type theory, cf.
The Book
, collaboratively authored by the
Univalent Foundations Project

[
Uni13
]
, or Rijke’s excellent book in progress
[
Rij20
]
. In particular, the latter emphasizes new perspectives informed by categorical homotopy theory and higher topos theory in the synthetic setting.

1
Shapes

In terms of homotopy theory, the shape layer enable us to reason about generating anodyne cofibrations using strict equalities.

In simplicial HoTT, next to the familiar layer of (univalent) intensional Martin-Löf type theory, there are new “non-fibrant” layers added that provide a logical calculus of geometric shapes. We start of from the
cube layer
,
i.e.
, a Lawvere theory generated by a single bi-pointed object
0,1:\mathbbn{2}
, the
standard
1
-cube
. A cube context

\Xi\equiv[I_{1},\ldots,I_{k}]

is thus a finite list of cubes

I_{m}~\mathrm{cube}

for
1\leq m\leq k
.

On top of the cube layer, we can form
topes
through logical comprehension via (intuitionistic) conjunction
\land
, disjunction
\lor
, and equality
\equiv
.
1
1

1

but
no
negation/reversals!
The
tope layer
hence captures sub-polytopes of
n
-cubes (with explicit embedding). A tope formula
\varphi(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})
together with a cube context
\Xi\equiv[I_{1},\ldots,I_{k},\vec{J}]
gives rise to a tope

\Xi\vdash\varphi~\mathrm{tope}.

The interval
\mathbbn{2}
under consideration shall also come equipped with an
inequality
tope

x,y:\mathbbn{2}\vdash x\leq y~\mathrm{tope}

making it a total order (w.r.t. the
strict
equality tope
\equiv
) with
0
and
1
as the bottom and top element, respectively.
2
2

2

For a comparison with the setup of
cubical type theory

[
CCHM18
]
cf.
[
RS17
, Remark 3.2]
.

In particular, we also sometimes make use of
connections
on the cube terms as discussed in
[
RS17
, Proposition 3.5]
.

A cube together with a tope is called a
shape
:

\frac{I\,\mathrm{cube}\qquad t:I\vdash\varphi\,\mathrm{tope}}{\{t:I\mid\varphi\}\,}

As an addition to the original theory by Riehl–Shulman, we will moreover coerce all shapes to be types, cf.
Section

2
. This is still in accordance with the intended class of models.

2
Extension types

In addition to the strict layers, the other new feature of simplicial type theory is a new type former called the
extension type
, the idea of which originally was due to Lumsdaine and Shulman. Given a shape
\Psi
and a type family
P:\Gamma\to\Psi\to\mathcal{U}
together with a
partial
section
a:\prod_{\Gamma\times\Phi}P
, where
\Phi\subseteq\Psi
denotes a subshape, we can form the corresponding family of extension types

\Gamma\vdash\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}P(t)\middle|^{\Phi}_{a}\right\rangle

which is interpreted as a (strict) pullback, cf.
[
RS17
, Theorem A.16]
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}P(t)\middle|^{\Phi}_{a}\right\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}^{\Psi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}^{\Phi}\times_{(\Gamma\times\Psi)^{\Phi}}(\Gamma\times\Psi)^{\Psi}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\overline{a},\overline{\id_{\Gamma\times\Psi}}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

This means, the elements of
\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}P(t)\middle|^{\Phi}_{a}\right\rangle
are total sections
b:\prod_{\Gamma\times\Psi}P
such that
b|_{\Phi}\equiv a
holds judgmentally:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi^{*}\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma\times\Phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma\times\Psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

The type-theoretic rules are analogous to the familiar rules of
\Pi
-types, but with the desired judgmental equalities added, cf.
[
RS17
, Figure 4]
.

In particular, non-dependent instances give rise to function types
A^{\Phi}
where
\Phi
is a shape rather than a type (even though, later on all of our shapes are assumed to be fibrant, cf. Subsection
2
). Semantically, this reflects the fact that the intended model is cotensored over simplicial sets, cf. also the discussion in
[
RS17
, Appendix A]
.

From the given rules one can show that the extension types interact well with the usual
\Pi
- and
\Sigma
-types, as shown in
[
RS17
, Subsections 4.1, 4.2]
. In particular, there is a version of the type-theoretic principle of choice
3
3

3

Sometimes this is referred to as the “type-theoretic
axiom
of choice” even though it is derivable.
involving extension types that will be used a lot.

Theorem 1.1

(Type-theoretic principle of choice for extension types,
[
RS17
, Theorem 4.2]
)
.

Let
\Phi\subseteq\Psi
be a shape inclusion. Suppose we are given families
P:\Psi\to\mathcal{U}
,
Q:\prod_{t:\Psi}(P(t)\to\mathcal{U})
and sections
a:\prod_{t:\Phi}P(t)
,
b:\prod_{t:\Phi}Q(t,a(t))
. Then there is an equivalence

\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}\sum_{x:P(t)}Q(t,x)\middle|^{\Phi}_{\lambda t.\langle a(t),b(t)\rangle}\right\rangle\simeq\sum_{f:\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}P(t)\middle|^{\Phi}_{a}\right\rangle}\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}Q(t,f(t))\middle|^{\Phi}_{b}\right\rangle.

A further important principle is
relative function extensionality
, which is added as an axiom:

Axiom 1.2

(Relative function extensionality,
[
RS17
, Axiom 4.6]
)
.

Let
\Phi\subseteq\Psi
be a shape inclusion.
Given a family
P:\Psi\to\mathcal{U}
such that each
P(t)
is contractible,
and a partial section
a:\prod_{t:\Phi}P(t)
, then the extension type
\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}P(t)\middle|^{\Phi}_{a}\right\rangle
is contractible.

An important consequence is the
homotopy extension property (HEP)
:

Proposition 1.3

(Homotopy extension property (HEP),
[
RS17
, Proposition 4.10]
)
.

Fix a shape inclusion
\Phi\subseteq\Psi
. Let
P:\Psi\to\mathcal{U}
be family,
b:\prod_{t:\Psi}P(t)
a total section, and
a:\prod_{t:\Phi}A(t)
a partial section. Then, given a homotopy
H:\prod_{t:\Phi}a(t)=b(t)
, there exist totalizations
a^{\prime}:\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}P(t)\middle|^{\Phi}_{a}\right\rangle
and
H^{\prime}:\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Psi}}a^{\prime}(t)=b(t)\middle|^{\Phi}_{H}\right\rangle
.

Semantics in simplicial spaces

A model of simplicial type theory is given by the Reedy model structure on bisimplicial sets, which presents the
(\infty,1)
-topos of simplicial spaces. The main steps in proving this are discussed in
[
RS17
, Appendix A]
, with previous work done in
[
Shu15
,
Cis14
]
. In fact, one can replace the base by an arbitrary (Grothendieck–Rezk–Lurie)
(\infty,1)
-topos
{\mathscr{E}}
so that the results developed synthetically will hold for Rezk objects (
i.e.
, internal
(\infty,1)
-categories) in
{\mathscr{E}}
. This is detailed later in
Chapter

9
.

In particular, following a guiding principle of Riehl–Verity’s
\infty
-cosmos theory
[
RV22
]
the definitions and constructions we are presenting fall in the frame of (synthetic) formal higher category theory: they have characterizations in terms of basic notions, such as (fibered) weak equivalences, (LARI) adjunctions, representability of distributors etc. See for instance the characterizations of cocartesian fibrations via
Theorems

2.6
and
2.7
.
By Riehl–Verity’s results on model-independence and notably Rasekh’s work on simplicial and (complete) Segal spaces one can systematically argue that, in essence, all of our internal notions externalize to their intended semantic counterparts—at least when restricting to the Rezk types, which are our objects of primary interest after all.

3
Synthetic higher categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 0,1\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 1,1\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 0,1\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 1,1\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 0,0\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 1,0\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 0,0\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 1,0\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 0,1\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 1,1\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 0,1\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 1,1\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 0,0\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 1,0\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 0,0\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle 1,0\rangle}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{1}^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial\Delta^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{0}}

Figure 1:
Some important shapes

Via the inequality tope of the interval
\mathbbn{2}
we can define simplices and subshapes familiar from simplicial homotopy theory. The first few low-dimensional simplices are given by

\displaystyle\Delta^{0}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\{t:\mathbf{1}\;|\;\top\},~~\Delta^{1}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\{t:\mathbbn{2}\;|\;\top\},~~\Delta^{2}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\{\!\langle t,s\rangle:\mathbbn{2}\times\mathbbn{2}\;|\;s\leq t\}.

The logical connectives of the tope layer enable us to carve out subshapes, such as boundaries and horns, e.g.

\displaystyle\partial\Delta^{1}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\{t:\mathbbn{2}\;|\;t\equiv 0\lor t\equiv 1\},\quad\Lambda_{1}^{2}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\{\langle t,s\rangle:\mathbbn{2}\times\mathbbn{2}\;|\;s\equiv 0\lor t\equiv 1\},

\displaystyle\partial\Delta^{2}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\{\langle t,s\rangle:\mathbbn{2}\times\mathbbn{2}\;|\;s\equiv t\lor s\equiv 0\lor t\equiv 1\}.

Cf.
Figure

1
for an illustration and
[
RS17
, Section 3.2]
for a detailed discussion.

We then can define, for any type
B
and fixed elements
b,b^{\prime}:B
the type of arrows from
b
to
b^{\prime}
as

\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\left\langle\Delta^{1}\to B\middle|^{\partial\Delta^{1}}_{[b,b^{\prime}]}\right\rangle.

Given a type family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
and an arrow
u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})
in the base, the type of arrows lying over
u
, from
e:P\,b
to
e^{\prime}:P\,b^{\prime}
, is given by

{\mathrm{hom}}^{P}_{u}(e,e^{\prime})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}}P(u(t))\middle|^{\partial\Delta^{1}}_{[e,e^{\prime}]}\right\rangle.

Such an arrow is also called a
dependent arrow
or
dependent homomomorphism
.

We will also be considering types of
2
-cells, defined by
4
4

4

The boundary here is given by

[u,v,w]:\partial\Delta^{2}\to B,\quad[u,v,w](t,s)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\begin{cases}u&s\equiv 0\\
v&t\equiv 1\\
w&s\equiv t\end{cases}

and similarly for the dependent case.

\hom_{B}^{2}(u,v;w)\equiv\left\langle\Delta^{2}\to B\middle|^{\partial\Delta^{2}}_{[u,v,w]}\right\rangle,\quad\hom^{2,P}_{\sigma}(f,g;h)\equiv\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}P(\sigma(t,s))\middle|^{\partial\Delta^{2}}_{[f,g,h]}\right\rangle.

We abbreviate

\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})\equiv(b\to_{B}b^{\prime})\equiv(b\to b^{\prime})

when the intent is clear from the context. For families
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
, we write

\hom_{u}^{P}(e,e^{\prime})\equiv(e\to^{P}_{u}e^{\prime}).

For a type
B
, we have two projections from the arrow type, given by evaluation

\partial_{k}:B^{\Delta^{1}}\to B,\quad\partial_{k}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda u.u(k).

Similarly to the notation introduced above, for the type of natural transformations between a fixed pair of functors we abbreviate

\underset{A\to B}{\hom}(f,g)\equiv(f\Rightarrow g).

Sometimes, we also denote the type of
2
-simplices by

\hom_{B}^{2}(u,v;w)\equiv(u,v\Rightarrow w),

and likewise for the dependent version.
With these prerequisites,
[
RS17
]
define a type
B
to be a
Segal type
such that the proposition

\isSegal(B)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\prod_{b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime}:B}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}u:b\to b^{\prime}\\
v:b^{\prime}\to b^{\prime\prime}\end{subarray}}\isContr\Big(\sum_{w:b\to b^{\prime\prime}}\hom_{B}^{2}(u,v;w)\Big)

is true. This means that
B
has
weak composition of directed arrows
. After Joyal, the Segal condition can be stated as

\isSegal(B)\simeq\isEquiv\Big((\Delta^{2}\to B)\to(\Lambda_{1}^{2}\to B)\Big).

Segal types can be thought of as synthetic
pre
-
(\infty,1)
-categories,
5
5

5

Informally, for
-2\leq k\leq\infty
and
0\leq n\leq k+1
an
(k,n)
-category
has
r
-dimensional morphisms for
r<k
, and for
r>n
, every
r
-morphism is invertible. Our study deals with
(\infty,1)
-categories, even though some of the ambient
(\infty,2)
-categorical structure will shine through in the type theory.
which here in simplicial homotopy type theory is expressed as a
property
rather than structure, echoing the familiar situation from the semantics in simplicial spaces. As discussed in
[
RS17
, Section 5]
, Segal types can be endowed with a weak composition
operation
which is weakly associative. For arrows
f:a\to b
,
g:b\to c
in some Segal type
B
, one writes
g\circ f
for the chosen composite arrow. The identity arrow of an element
b:B
is given by the constant map
\id_{b}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda t.b:\Delta^{1}\to B
.

Often, naturality w.r.t. directed arrows comes “for free”. In particular, any function
f:A\to B
between Segal types is a
functor
in the sense that it preserves composition and identities up to propositional equality, as proven in
[
RS17
, Section 6.1]
. The action of a functor on points already determines its actions on arrows as discussed in
loc. cit
.

Although the semantics is given by a structure presenting an
(\infty,1)
-topos—a certain kind of
(\infty,1)
-category—we, in fact, have access to portions of the
2
-dimensional structure present in the model as well. Since Segal types form an exponential ideal, the type
A\to B
is Segal if
B
is, and this allows us to study natural transformations between functors and lax diagrams of types, cf. Appendix
[
BW21
, Appendix A]
and the groundwork in
[
RS17
, Section 6]
. This enables us to adapt several developments from Riehl–Verity’s model-independent higher category theory from
\infty
-cosmoses to type theory.

Segal types come with two possible notions of isomorphism (analogous to the semantic situation for Segal spaces), the “spatial” one given by propositional equality, and the “categorical” one derived from the directed arrows. Namely, an arrow
f:a\to b
in a Segal type
B
is a (categorical)
isomorphism
if the type

\isIso(f)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{g,h:b\to a}(gf=\id_{a})\times(fh=\id_{b})

is inhabited. As discussed in
[
RS17
, Section 10]
, the type
\isIso(f)
turns out to be a proposition, so we can define the subtypes

{\mathrm{iso}}_{B}(a,b)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{f:\hom_{A}(a,b)}\isIso(a,b).

By path induction we define the comparison map

{\mathrm{idtoiso}}_{B}:\prod_{a,b:B}(a=_{B}b)\to{\mathrm{iso}}_{B}(a,b),\quad{\mathrm{idtoiso}}_{B,a,a}({\mathrm{refl}}_{a})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\id_{a},

and demanding that this be an equivalence leads to the notion of a
complete Segal type
,
aka

Rezk type
:

\isRezk(B)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\isSegal(B)\times\isEquiv({\mathrm{idtoiso}}_{B}).

The Rezk-completeness condition can be understood as a local version of the Univalence Axiom. In the simplicial space model, Rezk types are interpreted as Rezk spaces, which justifies viewing Rezk types as
synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categories
. Even though a lot of the development in
[
RS17
]
actually already works well on the level of (not necessarily complete) Segal types, our study of cocartesian families mostly restricts to complete Segal types, which is in line with preexisting studies of (co-)cartesian fibrations in the higher-categorical context
[
Joy08
,
Lur09
,
RV17
,
Ras17
,
Boa18
,
AF20
,
BS18
]
.

Among the synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categories, we can also consider types that are synthetic
(\infty,0)
-categories,
i.e.
,
\infty
-groupoids. These are called
discrete types
, which refers to the condition that all directed arrows be invertible, namely the comparison map defined inductively by

{\mathrm{idtoarr}}_{B}:\prod_{a,b:B}(a=_{B}b)\to\hom_{B}(a,b),\quad{\mathrm{idtoarr}}_{B,a,a}({\mathrm{refl}}_{a})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\id_{a},

be an equivalence:

\isDisc(B)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\prod_{a,b:B}\isEquiv({\mathrm{idtoarr}}_{B,a,b})

In fact, this discreteness condition entails Rezk-ness as shown by Riehl–Shulman. Furthermore, if
B
is a Segal type, for any
a,b:B
, the hom-type
\hom_{B}(a,b)
is discrete by
[
RS17
, Proposition 8.13]
.

Discrete Rezk spaces are also called complete Bousfield–Segal spaces and have been studied e.g. in
[
Ber08
,
Ste21
]
.

4
Covariant families

Riehl–Shulman have introduced the notion of
covariant family
,
i.e.
, families of discrete types varying functorially w.r.t. directed arrows in the base. A type family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
over a (Segal or Rezk) type
B
is
covariant
if

{\mathrm{isCovFam}}(P)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,b:B\\
u:a\to b\end{subarray}}\prod_{d:P\,a}\isContr\Big(\sum_{e:P\,b}(d\to^{P}_{u}e)\Big),

i.e.
, arrows in the base can be uniquely lifted w.r.t. a given source vertex.

Semantically, these correspond to
left fibrations
, which encode
(\infty,1)
-copresheaves. Hence, as expected, an example is given, for any
a:B
by the family

\lambda b.\hom_{B}(a,b):B\to\mathcal{U}.

The central topic of our work is to generalize this study to synthetic
cocartesian fibrations
,
i.e.
, the case where the fibers are Rezk rather than discrete.
6
6

6

Everything dualizes to the case of cartesian fibrations, of course, but we don’t spell this out.

2
Fibrant shapes

In our intended models, the shapes will arise as (spatially-discrete) fibrant objects. In fact, it is crucial for our treatment to have this reflected in the type theory, so we add the following rule:

\frac{I\,\mathrm{cube}\qquad t:I\vdash\varphi\,\mathrm{tope}}{\lparen t:I\mid\varphi\rparen\,}

In fact, we take the interval
\Delta^{1}
as a type, and the inequality relation
{\leq}:\Delta^{1}\to\Delta^{1}\to{\mathrm{Prop}}
as a type family, and then all shapes are types using the ordinary type formers.

Using relative function extensionality
[
RS17
, Section 4.4, Axiom 4.6]
,
1.2
, one can show that every strict extension type is equivalent to its “weak” counterpart, where the latter (essentially) is definable in Standard HoTT,
i.e.
we always have an equivalence as follows.

Proposition 2.1

(De-/strictification of extension types, cf.
[
BW21
, Section 2.4]
)
.

In cube context
I
, let
\varphi\hookrightarrow\psi
be a shape inclusion. Let
A:\lparen I\mid\psi\rparen\to\mathcal{U}
be a type family, and
a:\prod_{x:\lparen I\mid\varphi\rparen}
a partial section. Then there is an equivalence between the ensuing types of “strict” (judgmental) and “weak” (propositional) extensions:

\lx@nobreakspace\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{x:\lparen I\mid\psi\rparen}}{\textstyle\prod_{x:\lparen I\mid\psi\rparen}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{x:\lparen I\mid\psi\rparen}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{x:\lparen I\mid\psi\rparen}}A(x)\middle|^{\varphi}_{a}\right\rangle\simeq\sum_{f:\prod_{x:\lparen I\mid\psi\rparen}A(x)}\prod_{x:\lparen I\mid\varphi\rparen}(a\,x=f\,x),

A proof due to Buchholtz is in
[
BW21
, Section 2.4]
.

In particular, this gives the following formulation of (diagrammatic, weak) lifting problems in terms of (formulaic, strict) contractibility statements.

Observation 2.2
.

Consider a family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
and a shape inclusion
\Phi\subseteq\Psi
. Then, given a total diagram
\sigma:\Psi\to B
with a partial diagram
\kappa:\prod_{\Phi}\sigma^{*}P
lying over,
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}

possesses a diagonal filler uniquely up to homotopy if and only if the proposition

\mathrm{isContr}\Big(\Big\langle\prod_{t:\Psi}P(\sigma(s))\Big|^{\Phi}_{\kappa}\Big\rangle\Big)

is inhabited.

Example 2.3
.

Recall from
[
RS17
]
that a type
A
is Segal precisely if
A\to\mathbf{1}
is right orthogonal to
\Lambda_{1}^{2}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{2}
. Another example is given by the class of covariant families, namely
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
is covariant if and only if
\widetilde{P}\to B
is right orthogonal to the initial vertex inclusion
i_{0}:\mathbf{1}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{1}
.

We can also type-theoretically express the
Leibniz construction
familiar from categorical homotopy theory
[
RV22
, Definitions C2.8, C2.10, C3.8]
,
[
Rie14
]
as follows. We remark that Leibniz cotensor maps will be ubiquitous in our treatise since the fibrations of interest are defined by conditions on them.

Definition 2.4

(Leibniz cotensor)
.

Let
j:Y\to X
be a type map or shape inclusion, and
\pi:E\to B
a map between types. The
Leibniz cotensor of
j
and
\pi
(
aka

Leibniz exponential of
\pi
by
j
or
pullback hom
) is defined as the following gap map:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cdot}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{X}\times_{B^{Y}}E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\widehat{\pitchfork}\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\widehat{\pitchfork}\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\pitchfork}}

The map
\pi
is right orthogonal to
j
, meaning that for any square as below there exists a filler uniquely up to homotopy

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

if and only if the Leibniz cotensor map is an equivalence

j\widehat{\pitchfork}\pi:E^{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}B^{X}\times_{B^{Y}}E^{Y},

cf.
2.2
.

Though sparsely explicitly present in the text, we will also mention the dual operation.

Definition 2.5

(Pushout product)
.

Let
j:Y\to X
and
k:T\to S
each be type maps or shape inclusions. The
Leibniz tensor of
j
and
k
(or
pushout product
) is defined as the following cogap map:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\times S\bigsqcup_{Y\times T}X\times T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\times T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\times S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cdot}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\widehat{\otimes}k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\widehat{\otimes}k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\otimes}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}}

In particular, recall from
[
RS17
, Theorem 4.2]
, the explicit formula for the pushout product of two shape inclusions:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{t:I\,|\,\varphi\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{s:J\,|\,\chi\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{\langle t,s\rangle:I\times J\,|\,(\varphi\land\zeta)\lor(\psi\land\chi)\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{t:I\,|\,\psi\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{s:J\,|\,\zeta\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{\langle t,s\rangle:I\times J\,|\,\psi\land\zeta\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\otimes}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}}

3
Families vs. fibrations

Recall from
[
Uni13
]
that in presence of the univalence axiom, there is an equivalence between type families and fibrations.
7
7

7

Assuming universes with better structural properties—such as Segalness or directed univalence—would be fruitful for further considerations, but this is part of future work.

Consider the types

\mathrm{Fib}(\mathcal{U})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{A,B:\mathcal{U}}A\to B,\qquad\mathrm{Fam}(\mathcal{U})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{B:\mathcal{U}}(B\to\mathcal{U})

of functions in
\mathcal{U}
(viewed as type-theoretic fibrations
8
8

8

The inhabitants of
\mathrm{Fib}(\mathcal{U})
are just maps between arbitrary
\mathcal{U}

\mathcal{U}

), and families with
\mathcal{U}

\mathcal{U}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Fib}(\mathcal{U})}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda A,B,f.B}

Theorem 3.1

(Typal Grothendieck construction, cf.
[
Uni13
, Theorem 4.8.3]
)
.

There is a fiberwise quasi-equivalence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Fib}(\mathcal{U})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Fam}(\mathcal{U})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Un}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\St}

at stage
B:\mathcal{U}
given by a pair

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}/B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(B\to\mathcal{U})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\St_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Un_{B}}

with
straightening

\St_{B}(\pi)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda b.\mathrm{fib}_{b}(\pi)

and
unstraightening

\Un_{B}(P)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle\widetilde{P},\pi_{P}\rangle

(
\pi_{P}:\widetilde{P}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{b:B}P\,b\to B
the total space projection).

The spirit of dependent type theory somewhat favors type families over fibrations, but we will often resort to the fibrational viewpoint because it allows us to replay familiar categorical arguments. For instance, Riehl–Shulman’s covariant type families are a type-theoretic version of left fibrations, and we want to be able to conveniently make use of both incarnations of the same concept which motivates the following:

Definition 3.2

(Notions of families and fibrations)
.

A
notion of family (or notion of fibration)
is a family

\mathcal{F}:\mathrm{Fam}(\mathcal{U})\to{\mathrm{Prop}}

of propositions on the type of
\mathcal{U}
-small fibrations. For a notion of family
\mathcal{F}
, we say that a family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
is an
\mathcal{F}
-family
9
9

9

In practice, the name of
\mathcal{F}
often will be a linguistic predicate such as “covariant”, “cocartesian” etc. in which case we drop the hyphen and treat it as part of the natural meta-language, e.g. we will simply speak of “cocartesian” or “covariant fibrations”.
if and only if the proposition

{\mathrm{is}\mathrm{Fam}}_{\mathcal{F}}(P)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathcal{F}(P)

holds. A map
\pi:E\to B
is called an
\mathcal{F}
-fibration
if its family of fibers
\St_{B}(\pi)
is an
\mathcal{F}
-family.

By univalence and the Grothendieck construction, this definition is well-behaved,
i.e.
, a (
\mathcal{U}
-small) map is an
\mathcal{F}
-fibration if and only if it is (equivalent to) a projection associated to an
\mathcal{F}
-family (valued in
\mathcal{U}
).

In particular, we observe the following. Considering

\mathrm{Fib}_{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{U})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}E,B:\mathcal{U}\\
\pi:E\to B\end{subarray}}{\mathrm{is}\mathrm{Fam}}_{\mathcal{F}}(\St(\pi)),\quad\mathrm{Fam}_{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{U})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{P:\mathrm{Fam}(\mathcal{U})}{\mathrm{is}\mathrm{Fam}}_{\mathcal{F}}(P),

the Grothendieck construction descends to a fiberwise equivalence, for any notion of fibration/family
\mathcal{F}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Fib}_{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{U})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Fam}_{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{U})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Fib}(\mathcal{U})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Fam}(\mathcal{U})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{St}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Un}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Un}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{St}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Remark 3.3
.

As a convention, we will always state the definitions of the various notions of fibration in terms of
families
, and the above definition schema immediately yields the respective corresponding notion in fibrational terms.

We will also often denote a map which satisfies such a fibration condition (or possibly even just a usual map which is to be regarded as a type-theoretic fibration) by a double hooked arrow
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
, as is customary in homotopical algebra or categorical homotopy theory.
10
10

10

We use here the predicate
typal
to indicate that the universe considered is merely a general type, rather than Rezk or Segal. A (complete) Segal universe would give rise to a
categorical Grothendieck construction
, hence a synthetic version of straightening/unstraightening
[
Lur09
,
RV18
,
Ras18
,
Boa18
]
. But this is beyond the scope of this thesis.

Furthermore, as a consequence of univalence, any such propositionally defined notion of family/fibration is invariant under equivalence.

Proposition 3.4

(Homotopy invariance of notions of fibrations)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a notion of fibration. When given a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

the map
\xi
is an
\mathcal{F}
-fibration if and only if
\pi
is.

4
Comma and co-/cone types

Definition 4.1

(Comma types)
.

Consider a cospan of types

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

The
comma type

f\downarrow g
is given by the following pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\downarrow g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\times f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\partial_{1},\partial_{0}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

In the case that
f
is the identity
\id_{A}
, we write shorthand
A\mathbin{\downarrow}g
for
f\mathbin{\downarrow}g
, and dually
f\mathbin{\downarrow}A
if
g
is the identity.

Definition 4.2

(Co-/cone types)
.

Let
X

A

u:X\to A

X

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cst}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cst}_{A}}

give rise to the type

of

, and, dually

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u/A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big(A^{X})^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A/u}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{X}\times A^{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cst}_{A}\times u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\partial_{1},\partial_{0}\rangle}
