\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\partial_{1},\partial_{0}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Example 4.3

(Co-/slice types)
.

For a fixed point
b:B
, the
co-/slice types
are defined as
b/B
and
B/b
, resp. Note that
b/B\simeq b\mathbin{\downarrow}B
, and similarly for the slice types.

5
Orthogonal and LARI families

An important part in our study of synthetic fibered
(\infty,1)
-categories is to provide proofs of certain closure properties, which are chosen to parallel those of
\infty
-cosmoses
[
RV22
, Definition 1.2.1]
. Recall from
[
RV22
]
, that any
\infty
-cosmos
\mathcal{K}
provides an intrinsic notion of cocartesian fibrations, which themselves form an
\infty
-cosmos
{co\mathcal{C}art}({\mathcal{K}})
. The discrete cocartesian fibrations form an embedded
\infty
-cosmos
\mathcal{D}iscco\mathcal{C}art({\mathcal{K}})\hookrightarrow{co\mathcal{C}art}({\mathcal{K}})
. We prove type-theoretic analogues of the
\infty
-cosmological closure properties, formulated internally to the type theory of the “ambient”
(\infty,1)
-topos of simplicial objects.

First, we consider maps which are, more generally, defined by a unique right lifting property against an arbitrary map. Next, we discuss
j
-LARI maps which are defined by a left adjoint right inverse condition on a Leibniz cotensor map.

Specifically, let
j:Y\to X
be some type map.
11
11

11

This is often a shape inclusion; recall from Subsection
2
that we have coercion of (strict) shapes into types.
A map
\pi:E\to B
is called
j
-orthogonal
if any square as below has a contractible space of fillers:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

If a map
\pi:E\to B
is right orthogonal to a map
j:Y\to X
, we write
j\bot\pi
. Similarly, for families
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
, we write
j\bot P
if
j\bot\Un_{B}(P)
.

Classes of maps defined by such lifting conditions play an important role in categorical homotopy theory and have been extensively studied in various contexts. In particular, classes defined by right orthogonal lifting conditions necessarily satisfy certain closure properties. We are giving type theoretic proofs which will later apply for the specific kinds of
j
-orthogonal maps that we are interested in, namely (iso-)inner fibrations and left fibrations
aka
discrete covariant fibrations. For instance, a map
\pi:E\to B
(over a Segal type
B
) is a covariant fibration if and only if it is right orthogonal to the initial vertex inclusion
i_{0}:\mathbf{1}\to\Delta^{1}
.

In general,
\pi:E\to B
being
j
-orthogonal can be rephrased as the condition that the gap map in the following diagram be an equivalence:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{X}\times_{B^{Y}}E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Weakening this condition by requiring the gap map to only have a
left adjoint right inverse (LARI)
leads to the notion of
j
-LARI
map,
i.e.
,
\pi:E\to B
is a
j
-LARI map if and only if the induced map
E^{X}\to B^{X}\times_{B^{Y}}E^{Y}
has a LARI:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{X}\times_{B^{Y}}E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Between Rezk types, a map
\pi:E\to B
is a cocartesian fibration if and only if it is an
i_{0}
-LARI map.

Both
j
-orthogonal and
j
-LARI maps are closed under dependent products, composition, and pullback. In addition,
j
-orthogonal maps are closed under sequential limits and Leibniz cotensoring. They also satisfy left canceling.

6
(Iso-)inner families

Since at the most general level types are not Segal, as an intermediate step to defining cocartesian families, we have to deal with families of (complete) Segal types that are not necessarily functorial.
Inner families
are those type families for which the associated projection is right orthogonal to the horn inclusion
\Lambda_{1}^{2}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{2}
. Hence, between Segal types, inner families correspond to fibrations in the Segal model structure. Bringing in Rezk-completeness motivates our definition of
isoinner family
, which in addition to innerness requires all fibers to be Rezk-complete. Over Rezk types, this can be expressed by requiring the associated projection to be right orthogonal to the terminal projection from the free bi-invertible arrow
\mathbb{E}
. In the thesis, the running assumption is that the (anonymous) types considered are Rezk, so maps
E\to B
are iso-inner automatically. For a finer analysis of more general settings cf.
[
BW21
, Section 4]
.

7
Sliced constructions

Sometimes, we will also make use of sliced constructions, cf.
[
RV22
, Proposition 1.2.22]
.

Definition 7.1

(Sliced cotensor,
[
RV22
, Proposition 1.2.22(vi)]
)
.

Let
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
be a map, and
X
be a type or shape. The
sliced exponential (over
B
) of
\pi
by
X
is given by the map
X\boxtimes E\to B
defined as:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\boxtimes E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cst}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

This means
X\boxtimes E\simeq\sum_{b:B}X\to P\,b
.
In particular, for
X\equiv\Delta^{1}
, we obtain the
vertical arrow
object
{\mathrm{Vert}}_{\pi}(E)\to B
.

Definition 7.2

(Sliced product,
[
RV22
, Proposition 1.2.22(vi)]
)
.

Let
I
and
B
be types, and consider maps
\pi_{i}:E_{i}\twoheadrightarrow B
for
i:I
. The
sliced product
over the
\pi_{i}
is defined by pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\times_{i:I}^{B}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times_{i:I}^{B}\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i:I}\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Definition 7.3

(Sliced comma,
[
RV22
, Proposition 1.2.22(vi)]
)
.

Consider a cospan
\psi:F\to_{B}G\leftarrow_{B}E:\varphi
of fibered functors, giving rise to the sliced comma type
\varphi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}\psi
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi\downarrow_{B}\psi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{Vert}}(G)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\times E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G\times G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi\times\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\partial_{1},\partial_{0}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Chapter 6
Cocartesian families of synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categories

Cocartesian families
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
encode copresheaves of
(\infty,1)
-categories. All fibers
P\,b
are Rezk types, and
P
is (covariantly)
functorial
in the sense that an arrow
u:a\to b
in
B
induces a functor
u_{!}:P\,a\to P\,b
, and this transport operation is natural w.r.t. directed arrows in
B
,
i.e.
, it respects composition and identities. In fact, we will often reason about cocartesian families
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
in terms of their associated projection
\pi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\pi_{P}:E\to B
. Our study is informed by
[
RV22
, Chapter 5]
and
[
RV17
]
in an essential way, where Riehl–Verity develop a model-independent theory of cocartesian fibrations intrinsic to an arbitrary
\infty
-cosmos. While this constitutes more generally a fibrational theory of
(\infty,n)
-categories, for
0\leq n\leq\infty
, our present study restricts to
(\infty,1)
-categories, and at the same time extends Riehl–Shulman’s treatment of synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categories fibered in
\infty
-groupoids.

Reminiscent to the classical (1-categorical) definition, we introduce cocartesian families in terms of the existence of enough cocartesian liftings. However, we also give alternative characterizations, such as the
Chevalley criterion
which allows us to develop the theory in the style of formal category theory, as done by Riehl and Verity
[
RV22
]
for
\infty
-cosmoses. Their work in particular constitutes a vast generalization of the historic results of Gray
[
Gra66
]
and Street
[
Str74
,
Str80
,
Str87
]
to the model-independent higher case.

Specifically, over Rezk types cocartesian families are exactly the isoinner families that are
i_{0}
-LARI families in the sense of Subsection
5
, for
i_{0}:\mathbf{1}\to\Delta^{1}
. Spelled out, this means that the gap map in the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}\widehat{\pitchfork}\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\Delta^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

has a left adjoint right inverse
\chi:\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B\to E^{\Delta^{1}}
which yields the up-to-homotopy uniquely determined cocartesian lifts.

We find a similar characterization for
cocartesian functors
between cocartesian families (incarnated as fibrations). In
[
BW21
, Subsection 5.2.3 and 5.3.3]
, from this we prove type-theoretic versions of the
\infty
-cosmological closure properties of cocartesian fibrations, which in our case means that the
(\infty,1)
-category of cocartesian fibrations is complete w.r.t. to certain
(\infty,1)
-limits.
1
1

1

In more technical terms, our results can be interpreted as type-theoretic proofs of the completeness of the
(\infty,1)
-categorical core of the
\infty
-cosmos
co\mathcal{C}art(\mathcal{R}ezk)
, itself presenting an
(\infty,2)
-category (cf.
[
RV22
, Definition 12.1.10, Remark 12.1.11]
).
Note that, ideally, these would be statements involving universe types which themselves are Rezk. These are beyond the scope of the current discussion, but nevertheless we can “externalize” these completeness statements to our univalent universe
\mathcal{U}
of arbitrary simplicial types, yielding
3.6
.

We then prove characterizations of cocartesian functors extending the ones for cocartesian functors, e.g. the Chevalley criterion for cocartesian functors says that a fibered functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

between cocartesian fibrations is a cocartesian functor if and only if the mate of the induced square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

is invertible.
2
2

2

A development of the required results about adjunctions in simplicial type theory is given in
[
BW21
, Appendix A and B]
.

1
Cocartesian arrows

1
Definition and properties

The starting point are
cocartesian arrows
, which are dependent arrows in a fibration characterized by a certain initial universal property. Our type-theoretic definition generalizes the classical
1
-categorical picture, and is a synthetic version of the
(\infty,1)
-categorical one. Thanks to the extension types, cocartesian lifts of an arrow will lie
strictly
over their base arrow.

Definition 1.1

(Cocartesian arrow)
.

Let
B
be a type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an inner family.
Let
b,b^{\prime}:B
,
u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})
, and
e:P\,b
,
e^{\prime}:P\,b^{\prime}
. An arrow
f:\hom_{P\,u}(e,e^{\prime})
is a
(
P
-)cocartesian morphism
or
(
P
-)cocartesian arrow
iff

{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}_{P}f:\equiv\prod_{\sigma:\left\langle\Delta^{2}\to B\middle|^{\Delta_{0}^{1}}_{u}\right\rangle}\prod_{h:\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}P\,\sigma(t,t)}\isContr\Big(\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}P\sigma(t,s)\middle|^{\Lambda_{0}^{2}}_{[f,h]}\right\rangle\Big).

This is illustrated in
Figure

1
. Notice that being a cocartesian arrow is a homotopy proposition.

Figure 1:

Note that both definitions are propositions. The cubical implies the traditional “simplicial” one by degenerating the right upper horizontal arrows to identities. Conversely, by composition the cubical formulation is implied by the simplicial one by composing the arrows making up right upper halves of the diagram.

Proposition 1.3

(Chevalley Criterion for cocartesian arrows, cf.
[
RV22
, Theorem 5.1.7]
)
.

In an isoinner family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
over a Rezk type
B
, a dependent arrow
f
over
u:b\mathbin{\downarrow}B
with starting vertex
e:P\,b
is cocartesian if and only if
f
together with the identity homotopy makes the following triangle an absolute left lifting diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\Delta^{1}}\times_{B}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle b,u,e\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\pi}

We find several formal properties of cocartesian arrows:

Proposition 1.4

(Uniqueness of cocartesian lifts (in isoinner families);
[
RV22
]
, Lem. 5.1.3)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an isoinner family. Then
P
-cocartesian lifts of arrows of
B
are unique up to homotopy.

Note that this is also implied by their characterization as relative left adjoint.

Corollary 1.5
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an isoinner family over a Rezk type.

1.

If
f:e\to e^{\prime}
is a
P
-cocartesian arrow, and
h:e^{\prime}\to e^{\prime\prime}
is an arbitrary arrow, then
\mathrm{fill}_{f}(h)\circ f=h
.

2.

If
f:e\to e^{\prime}
is a
P
-cocartesian arrow, and
g:e^{\prime}\to e^{\prime\prime}
is an arbitrary arrow, then
\mathrm{fill}_{f}(gf)=g
.

Proposition 1.6
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a cocartesian family over a Rezk type
B
. For arrows
u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})
,
v:\hom_{B}(b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})
, with
b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime}:B
, consider dependent arrows
f:{\mathrm{hom}}_{P,u}(e,e^{\prime})
,
g:{\mathrm{hom}}_{P,v}(e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime})
lying over, for
e:P\,b
,
e^{\prime}:P\,b^{\prime}
,
e^{\prime\prime}:P\,b^{\prime\prime}
.

1.

If both
f
and
g
are are cocartesian arrows, then so is their composite
g\circ f
.

2.

If
f
and
g\circ f
are cocartesian arrows, then so is
g
.

Lemma 1.7

(
[
RV22
]
, Lem. 5.1.4)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an inner family over a Segal type
B
.

1.

If
f
is a dependent isomorphism in
P
over some morphism
u
in
B
, then
u
is itself an isomorphism.

2.

Any dependent isomorphism in
P
is cocartesian.

3.

If
f
is a cocartesian arrow in
P
over an identity in
B
, then
f
is an isomorphism.

2
Characterizations of cocartesian arrows

Proposition 1.8
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type, and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an isoinner family with total type
E\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{P}
. Let
u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})
,
b,b^{\prime}:B
, be a morphism in
B
and
f:{\mathrm{hom}}_{P,u}(e,e^{\prime})
,
e:P\,b
,
e^{\prime}:P\,b^{\prime}
a dependent morphism.

The morphism
f
is cocartesian if and only if, for any
b^{\prime\prime}:B
,
e^{\prime\prime}:P\,b^{\prime\prime}
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{E}(\langle b^{\prime},e^{\prime}\rangle,\langle b^{\prime\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\rangle)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{E}(\langle b,e\rangle,\langle b^{\prime},e^{\prime}\rangle)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{B}(b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\circ\langle u,f\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\circ u}

is a pullback.

Proof.

By fibrant replacement, de-/strictification, and choice for extension types, we can replace the square in question by:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathllap{\sum_{w:b\to b^{\prime\prime}}\sum_{\sigma:\left\langle\Delta^{2}\to B\middle|^{\Lambda_{0}^{2}}_{[u,w]}\right\rangle}\sum_{h:e\to^{P}_{w}e^{\prime\prime}}}\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{2}}}P(\sigma(t,s))\middle|^{\Lambda_{0}^{2}}_{[f,h]}\right\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sum_{w:b\to b^{\prime\prime}}(e\to_{w}^{P}e^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sum_{w:b\to b^{\prime\prime}}\left\langle\Delta^{2}\to B\middle|^{\Lambda_{0}^{2}}_{[u,w]}\right\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime\prime})}}

Undwinding what it means for this square to be a pullback precisely recovers the condition that
f
be cocartesian.
∎

We prove a characterization for cartesian edges, recovering the definition established by Joyal and Lurie, and transferred to complete Segal spaces by Rasekh.

Let
B
be a
Segal type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an isoinner family. Consider its total space
E:\equiv\sum_{b:B}P\,b
. For
b,b^{\prime}:B
, let
u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})
an arrow with a dependent arrow
f:{\mathrm{hom}}_{P,f}(e,e^{\prime})
above it, where
e:P\,b,e^{\prime}:P\,b^{\prime}
.

There is an induced commutative square involving comma objects, each of which can be described using extension types:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathllap{\sum_{\sigma:u\downarrow B}\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{1}}}{\textstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{1}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{1}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{1}}}P(\sigma(t))\middle|^{\Delta_{1}^{1}}_{f}\right\rangle}\simeq f/E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u/B\simeq\mathrlap{\left\langle\Delta^{2}\to B\middle|^{\Delta_{1}^{1}}_{u}\right\rangle}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathllap{\sum_{u:b\downarrow B}\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}}P(u(t))\middle|^{0}_{e}\right\rangle}\simeq e/E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b/B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}

Proposition 1.9

(Joyal’s Criterion)
.

Let
B
be Segal and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an inner family. Write
E\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{P}
and consider the canonical projection
\pi:E\to B
. A dependent arrow
f:{\mathrm{hom}}_{P,u}(e,e^{\prime})
,
e:P\,b,e^{\prime}:P\,b^{\prime}
,
u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})
,
b,b^{\prime}:B
, is cocartesian if and only if the mediating map
f/E\to e/E\times_{b/B}u/B
occurring in the pullback
Figure

2

is an equivalence.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f/E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e/E\times_{b/B}u/B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u/B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e/E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b/B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Figure 2:
Joyal’s Criterion for cocartesian edges

Proof.

Note that by precondition both
B
and
E
are Segal types. The map
\varphi
is an equivalence if and only if

\prod_{b^{\prime\prime}:B}\,\prod_{v:\hom_{B}(b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})}\,\prod_{e^{\prime\prime}:P\,b^{\prime\prime}}\,\prod_{h:{\mathrm{hom}}_{P,v\circ u}(e,e^{\prime\prime})}\,\isContr\Big(\sum_{g:{\mathrm{hom}}_{P.v}(e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime})}g\circ f=h\Big)

which is equivalent to
f
being cocartesian.
∎

2
Cocartesian families

Cocartesian families are families of Rezk types such that every map in the base has a cocartesian lift w.r.t. a choice of the source vertex. After showing elementary properties such as functoriality we prove the Chevalley criterion which exhibits cocartesian families as LARI fibrations (w.r.t. to the initial vertex inclusion
i_{0}:\mathbf{1}\to\Delta^{1}
). It then follows that cocartesian fibrations are closed under pullback, composition, and dependent products. We proceed by giving three kinds of examples of cocartesian families: the domain projection, the codomain projection in case the base category has all pushouts, and the cocartesian replacement of an arbitrary map between Rezk types. Relating to the latter, we show that cocartesian replacement really is a left adjoint.

Independently, definitions of cocartesian families have also been given in an unpublished section of
[
RS17
]
, and in the formalization
[
Lic19
]
. The definition presented here is due to the author’s joint work with Buchholtz
[
BW21
]
.

1
Definition and basic properties

Cocartesian type families are those (isoinner) families that have all cocartesian lifts, in the sense of
Definition

11.B.2
:

Definition 2.1

(Cocartesian lifting property)
.

A family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
is said to
have (all) cocartesian lifts
if

{\mathrm{hasCocartLifts}}\,P:\equiv\prod_{b,b^{\prime}:B}\prod_{u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})}\prod_{e:P\,b}\sum_{e^{\prime}:P\,b^{\prime}}\sum_{f:\hom_{P\,u}(e,e^{\prime})}{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}_{P}\,f.

Definition 2.2

(Cocartesian family)
.

For any type
B
, we call a family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
a
cocartesian family
if

{\mathrm{isCocartFam}}\,P:\equiv{\mathrm{isIsoInnerFam}}\,P\times{\mathrm{hasCocartLifts}}\,P.

If
B
is a Rezk type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
is a cocartesian family, then any arrow
u:\hom_{B}(a,b)
induces a map
{u}^{P}_{!}:P\,a\to P\,b
defined by

{u}^{P}_{!}:\equiv\lambda d.\partial_{1}{P}_{!}({u},{d}).

We will often omit the superscript if the family is clear from the context.

Definition 2.3

(Vertical arrow)
.

Let
\pi:E\to B
be an inner fibration over a Segal type. A dependent arrow
f:e\to e^{\prime}
is called
vertical
if
\pi\,f
is an isomorphism. We also write
f:e\rightsquigarrow e^{\prime}
to indicate that
f
is vertical.

Observe that, since being an isomorphism is a proposition in a Segal type by Proposition 1.10,
[
RS17
]
, being a vertical arrow is a proposition when in an inner family over a Segal type. In a cocartesian family one recovers the classically well-known fact that any dependent arrow factors as a cocartesian arrow followed by a vertical arrow. Furthermore, in a cocartesian family, vertical arrows are stable under pullback.

Cocartesian families, generalizing
1
-categorical Grothendieck opfibrations,
6
6

6

Note that the liftings really are strict up to honest equality thanks to the extension types. This matches with the situation of cocartesian fibrations of quasi-categories or Rezk spaces.
implement the idea of a
functorial
family of Rezk types,
i.e.
in addition to transport along paths—which exists for arbitrary type families—there is also a notion of transport along
directed
arrows, which turns out to be compatible with the familiar path transport.

Proposition 2.4

(Functoriality)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
a cocartesian family. For any
a:B
and
x:P\,a
there is an identity

{P}_{!}({\id_{b}},{x})=\id_{x},

and for any
u:\hom_{B}(a,b)
,
v:\hom_{B}(b,c)
, there is an identity

{P}_{!}({v\circ u},{x})={P}_{!}({v},{{u}_{!}(x)})\circ{P}_{!}({u},{x}).

Proof.

The first claim follows from
1.7
(
3
), in combination with
1.4
.

The second claim follows from
1.6
(
1
).
∎

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a cocartesian family. For any arrow
u:\hom_{B}(a,b)
and terms
d:P\,a
,

, we have equivalences between the types of (cocartesian) lifts of arrows from
d
to
e
and maps (equivalences) from
u_{!}d
to
e
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(u_{!}d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(u_{!}d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\,b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\,b}

Consider the maps

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle:(d\to_{u}e)\longrightarrow(u_{!}d\to^{P\,b}e),\quad\Phi(f):\equiv\mathrm{fill}_{P_{!}(u,d)}(f),

\displaystyle\Psi

\displaystyle:(u_{!}d\to^{P\,b}e)\longrightarrow(d\to_{u}e),\quad\Psi(p):\equiv p\circ P_{!}(u,d).

From the universal property of cocartesian fillers, we find

\displaystyle\Phi(\Psi(g))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{fill}_{P_{!}(u,d)}(g\circ P_{!}(u,d))=g,

\displaystyle\Psi(\Phi(h))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{fill}_{P_{!}(u,d)}(h)\circ P_{!}(u,d)=h.

is as well since cocartesian arrows are closed under composition. Hence,
\Psi
restricts to a map

\lx@nobreakspace\Psi^{\prime}:(u_{!}d=_{P\,b}e)\longrightarrow(d\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

∎

Thus, just as in the classical case, our cocartesian families capture the notion of covariantly functorial families of categories.

Due to
1.4
, over Rezk types being a cocartesian family is a proposition, and indeed this is the setting that we are interested in. In particular, cocartesian families over Rezk types are thus “cloven up to homotopy”. Given an arrow
u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})
together with
e:P\,b
, we write
P_{!}(u,e)
for homotopically unique cocartesian lift of
u
. Even more, from the point of view of homotopy type theory, these cleavages are automatically “
split
’’.
7
7

7

in the sense analogous to
[
Str21
]
, Definition 3.1

2

Cocartesian families via lifting

We find that cocartesian families are exactly the
i_{0}
-LARI families, for
i_{0}:\mathbf{1}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{1}
the inclusion of the initial vertex.

Theorem 2.6

(Chevalley criterion: Cocartesian families via lifting,
[
RV22
]
, Prop. 5.1.11(ii))
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type,
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an isoinner family, and denote by
\pi:E\to B
the associated projection map. The family
P
is cocartesian if and only if the Leibniz cotensor map
i_{0}\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\pi:E^{\Delta^{1}}\to\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B
has a left adjoint right inverse:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}\widehat{\pitchfork}\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\Delta^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Proof.

This follows formally from
Theorem

11.B.3
. ∎

Cocartesian families via transport

There is another characterization of cocartesian families in terms of an adjointness condition. Any map
\pi:E\to B
between Rezk types is exhibited as a retract of the pullback map
\partial_{0}^{*}\pi:\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B\to B^{\Delta^{1}}
in the following way:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cst}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}^{*}\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

The mediating map

\iota\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\iota_{\pi}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda\langle b,e\rangle.\langle\id_{b},e\rangle:E\to\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B

is a fibered functor from
\pi:E\to B
to its cocartesian replacement,
8
8

8

Explicitly,
\partial^{\prime}_{1}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda u,e.u(1):\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B\twoheadrightarrow B
, cf.
2.11
.

i.e.
there is a commutative triangle:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial^{\prime}_{1}}

Denote the family of fibers of
\pi
by
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
. If
P
is cocartesian, it has “directed transport”

\lx@nobreakspace\tau\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\tau_{P}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathrm{transp}_{P}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda u,e.u^{P}_{!}e:\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B\to E.

This transport map
\tau
is easily checked to be a fibered functor from
\partial^{\prime}_{1}
to
\pi
. We will show that it is a fibered left adjoint of
\iota_{\pi}
, and conversely, the existence of a fibered left adjoint
\tau_{\pi}
to
\iota_{\pi}
will imply that
\pi
is cocartesian.

Theorem 2.7

(Cocartesian families via transport,
[
RV22
]
, Prop. 5.1.11(ii))
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type, and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
an isoinner family with associated total type projection
\pi:E\to B
.

Then,
P
is cocartesian if and only if the map

\iota\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\iota_{P}:E\to\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B,\quad\iota\,\langle b,e\rangle:\equiv\langle\id_{b},e\rangle

has a fibered left adjoint
\tau\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\tau_{P}:\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B\to E
as indicated in the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Proof.

Assume
P
is cocartesian. For the candidate left adjoint we take the map given by cocartesian transport

\lx@nobreakspace\tau:\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B\to E,\quad\tau(u,e)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle\partial_{1}\,u,u_{!}e\rangle.

Then
\pi(\tau(u,e))\equiv\partial_{1}\,u\equiv\partial_{1}^{\prime}(u,e)
, so
\tau
is a fibered functor from
\partial_{1}^{\prime}
to
\pi
. We show that for any
\langle u:b\to b^{\prime},e:P\,b\rangle
and
\langle c:B,d:P\,c\rangle
in
E
the maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hom_{E}(\langle b^{\prime},u_{!}e\rangle,\langle c,d\rangle)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hom_{\pi\downarrow B}(\langle u,e\rangle,\langle\mathrm{id}_{c},d\rangle)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{\langle u,e\rangle,\langle c,d\rangle}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{\langle u,e\rangle,\langle c,d\rangle}}

defined by

\Phi_{\langle u,e\rangle,\langle c,d\rangle}(v,g)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle vu,v,g\circ P_{!}(u,e)\rangle,\quad\Psi_{\langle u,e\rangle,\langle c,d\rangle}(w,v,h)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle v,\mathrm{fill}_{P_{!}(u,e)}(h)\rangle

form a quasi-equivalence (cf.
Figure

4
for illustration).
We have

\Phi(\Psi(w,v,h))=\Phi(v,\mathrm{fill}_{P_{!}(u,e)}(h))=\langle vu,v,\mathrm{fill}_{P_{!}(u,e)}(h)\circ P_{!}(u,e)\rangle=\langle w,v,h\rangle

by
1.5
(
1
), and noting that for any square
\langle w,v\rangle:u\to\id_{c}
there is an identification
w=vu
.
Next, we find

\lx@nobreakspace\Psi(\Phi(v,g))=\Psi(vu,v,g\circ P_{!}(u,e))=\langle v,\mathrm{fill}_{P_{!}(u,e)}(g\circ P_{!}(u,e))\rangle=\langle v,g\rangle.

using again the properties of the fillers defined by the cocartesian lifts, cf.
1.5
(
2
).

So indeed
\tau
is left adjoint to
\iota
. Moreover, it is a fibered left adjoint as can be seen as follows. The unit is defined by

\eta_{\langle u,e\rangle}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Phi(\id_{b^{\prime}},\id_{u_{!}e})=\langle u,\id_{b^{\prime}},P_{!}(u,e)\rangle:\hom_{\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B}(\langle u,e\rangle,\langle\id_{b^{\prime}},u_{!}e\rangle).

Since the second component is an identity this is a vertical arrow in
\partial_{1}^{\prime}:\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B\twoheadrightarrow B
which proves the fiberedness of the adjunction.

Suppose on the converse that
\tau\dashv_{B}\iota
is some fibered left adjunction. Since
\tau
is a fibered functor, for
\langle u:b\to b^{\prime},e:P\,b\rangle
we can assume

\lx@nobreakspace\tau(u,e)\equiv\langle b^{\prime},\widehat{\tau}(u,e)\rangle.

Next,
\eta
being a
fibered
natural transformation fixes its part in
B
,
i.e.
since in the square the lower horizontal edge has to be an identity the upper horizontal edge must be
u
(up to identification), so the only degree of freedom is the dependent arrow
f_{u,e}
as indicated:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\tau}(u,e)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta_{u,e}:}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{u,e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

Hence, we can assume

\eta_{u,e}\equiv\langle u,\id_{b^{\prime}},f_{u,e}\rangle.

By assumption the transposing map induced by the unit

\Phi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Phi_{\eta}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda v,g.\iota(v,g)\circ\eta_{u,e}\equiv\langle vu,v,g\circ f_{u,e}\rangle:\hom_{E}(\iota(u,e),\langle c,d\rangle)\longrightarrow\hom_{\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B}(\langle u,e\rangle,\langle\id_{c},d\rangle)

is an equivalence. Spelled out, this means for any
v:b^{\prime}\to c
,
h:e\to^{P}_{vu}d
there exists an arrow
g_{h}:\widehat{\tau}(u,e)\to_{v}d
, uniquely up to homotopy, s.t.
h=g_{h}\circ f_{u,e}
. This says exactly that
f_{u,e}:e\to\widehat{\tau}(u,e)
is a cocartesian lift of
u
w.r.t.
e
.
∎

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{!}e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{!}e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces vu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\circ P_{!}(u,e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{fill}_{P_{!}(u,e)}(h)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

Figure 4:
Transposing maps of the fibered adjunction
\tau_{P}\dashv_{B}\iota_{P}

Examples of cocartesian families

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
g:C\to A\leftarrow B:f
be a cospan of Rezk types. Then the codomain projection from the comma object

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\downarrow g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\times f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\partial_{1},\partial_{0}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}}

is a cocartesian fibration.

Proof.

Cf.
[
BW21
, Proposition 5.2.15]
, or later the version for fibered comma objects,
Proposition

1.5
.
∎

Corollary 2.9

(Codomain opfibration)
.

For any Rezk type
B
, the projection

\lx@nobreakspace\partial_{1}:B^{\Delta^{1}}\to B,~\lx@nobreakspace\partial_{1}:\equiv\lambda f.f(1).

is a cocartesian fibration, called the
codomain opfibration
.

Indeed the domain projection of a Rezk type is a cocartesian fibration given that the base has pushouts.

Proposition 2.10

(Domain opfibration)
.

If
B
is a Rezk type that has all pushouts, then the domain projection

\lx@nobreakspace\partial_{0}:B^{\Delta^{1}}\to B,\quad\partial_{0}(u):\equiv u(0)

is a cocartesian fibration.

Proof.

Cf.
[
BW21
, Proposition 3.2.10]
.
∎

Towards monadicity: the free cocartesian family

As discussed in
[
AF20
,
GHN17
,
RV21
]
cocartesian fibrations are monadic over general functors (over a fixed base). This means that for any functor
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
there is a
free cocartesian fibration

L(\pi):L(E)\twoheadrightarrow B
. Due to the current lack of categorical universes in our type theory we postpone a discussion with emphasis on a global perspective similar to the cited works. However, we can still state and prove the universal property for this construction, so that later on, in the presence of the desired universes the actual monadicity statement will easily follow. Namely, we define a “unit map”
\iota_{\pi}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\iota:\pi\to_{B}L(\pi)
, and prove that precomposition constitutes an equivalence of types
9
9

9

In general,
{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B}(\pi,\xi)
is the
\Sigma
-type of fiberwise maps from
\pi
to
\xi
which preserve cocartesian lifts. Cf.
Section

3
for a more thorough treatment.

-\circ\iota_{P}:{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B}(L(\pi),\xi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{Fun}_{B}(\pi,\xi),

for any
cocartesian
fibration
\xi:F\to B
.

Definition 2.11

(Free cocartesian family)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an isoinner family. Then the family

L(\pi)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda b.\sum_{u:B\mathbin{\downarrow}b}P(\partial_{0}u):B\to\mathcal{U}

is the
free cocartesian family associated to
P
, or the
cocartesian replacement of
P
.

In more categorical terms, the free cocartesian family—in its incarnation as a fibration—is constructed by first pulling back the map
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
along the domain projection, and then postcomposing with the
codomain
projection:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L(\pi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}^{\prime}}

Morphisms in the cocartesian replacement can be depicted as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

We will see that, indeed the free cocartesian family is a cocartesian family.

Theorem 2.12
.

If
B
is a Rezk type, and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
is an isoinner family then the family
L(P):B\to\mathcal{U}
is cocartesian.

Proof.

By the closure properties of isoinner families, since
P
is an isoinner family, so is
L(P)
.

Let
u:\hom_{B}(b,b^{\prime})
be an arrow in
B
, and
\langle v,e\rangle:LP(b)
a point over
b
, where
v:\hom_{B}(a,b)
and
e:P\,a
.

We define the candidate lift to be
\langle\id_{a},u,\id_{e}\rangle:{\mathrm{hom}}_{LP,u}(\langle v,e\rangle,\langle vu,e\rangle)
,
i.e.
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces vu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

Cocartesianness is readily verified.
10
10

10

Compare cf.
2.9
.
Namely, for
u^{\prime}:\hom_{B}(b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})
, let
t:\hom_{B}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})
,
e^{\prime}:P\,a^{\prime}
, together with
w:\hom_{B}(a,a^{\prime})
and
f:{\mathrm{hom}}_{P,u^{\prime}u}(e,e^{\prime})
s.t.
t\circ w=(u^{\prime}u)\circ v
. We find the ensuing filler over
v
as indicated:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L(\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces vu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}

By construction the dashed arrows are unique up to homotopy.
∎

We define the “unit map”

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}^{\prime}}

as the “inclusion”

\iota\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda b,e.\langle\id_{b},e\rangle:\prod_{b:B}P\,b\to(LP)\,b.

Proposition 2.13

(Universal property of cocartesian replacement)
.

For a Rezk type
B
, consider an isoinner fibration
\pi:E\to B
, and a cocartesian fibration
\xi:F\to B
. Then the map

\lx@nobreakspace{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B}(L(\pi),\xi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Psi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}-\circ\iota_{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{Fun}_{B}(\pi,\xi)

is an equivalence of types.

Proof.

We aim to give a quasi-inverse of the precomposition map. Let

\Psi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda\varphi.\varphi^{\prime}:\mathrm{Fun}_{B}(\pi,\xi)\to{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B}(L(\pi),\xi)

where

\lx@nobreakspace\varphi^{\prime}_{b}(v,e)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}v_{!}^{Q}(\varphi_{a}\,e),

for
v:a\to b
,
e:P\,a
.
First, we are to show that this operation is really valued in cocartesian functors.
For this, we have to show that, for any
v:a\to b
,
e:P\,a
, the arrow

\lambda t.(u(t)\circ v)_{!}^{Q}(\varphi_{b}\,e):v_{!}Q(\varphi_{b}\,e)\longrightarrow^{P}_{u}(uv)_{!}^{Q}(\varphi_{b}\,a)

is
Q
-cocartesian. To that end, we observe the following. Let
a:B
,
d:Q\,a
be fixed. Consider the maps
\mathrm{cst}(d),\tau_{Q}(-,d):a\mathbin{\downarrow}B\to E
defined by

\lx@nobreakspace\mathrm{cst}(d)(v:a\to b)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}d,\quad\tau_{Q}(-,d)(v:a\to b)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}v^{Q}_{!}(d):d.

We define the natural transformation

Q_{!}(-,d):\underset{\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B\to E}{\hom}(\mathrm{cst}(d),\tau_{Q}(-,d)),\quad Q_{!}(-,d)(v:a\to b)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}Q_{!}(v,d):d\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.28 0 L 0.55 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}
}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{v}v^{Q}_{!}(d).

Morphisms in
a\mathbin{\downarrow}B
are given by commutative triangles
u:v\to w
, so for fixed
v
the type of morphisms in
a\mathbin{\downarrow}B
starting at
v
is equivalent to the type
(\partial_{1}\,v)\mathbin{\downarrow}B
. Hence, any morphism in
a\mathbin{\downarrow}B
can be taken to be of the form
u:v\to uv
, for
v:a\to b
,
u:b\to b^{\prime}
. The naturality squares of
Q_{!}(-,d)
thus are of the following form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v_{!}^{Q}(d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(uv)_{!}^{Q}(d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces vu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u:v\to uv)^{Q}_{!}(d)}

Note that the lower vertical arrow is given by

\lambda t.(u(t)\circ v)^{Q}_{!}(d)=(u:v\to uv)^{Q}_{!}(d).

By right cancelation,
(u:v\to uv)^{Q}_{!}(d)
is cocartesian, and hence we have an identity of arrows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(uv)_{!}^{Q}(d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v^{Q}_{!}(d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u^{Q}_{!}(v_{!}^{Q}(d))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{!}(u,v_{!}^{Q}(d))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u:v\to uv)^{Q}_{!}(d)}

In the cocartesian replacement
\partial_{1}^{\prime}:\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B\twoheadrightarrow B
, the cocartesian lift of
u:b\to b^{\prime}
w.r.t.
\langle v:a\to b,e:P\,a\rangle
is given by
\langle\id_{a},u,\id_{e}\rangle
. Now, by the previous discussion we have

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}_{u}(\id_{a},u,\id_{e})

\displaystyle=\lambda t.(u(t)\circ v)_{!}^{Q}(\varphi_{a}\,e)

\displaystyle=(u:v\to uv)^{Q}_{!}(\varphi_{a}\,e)

\displaystyle=Q_{!}(u,v^{Q}_{!}(\varphi_{a}\,e))

\displaystyle=Q_{!}(u,\varphi^{\prime}_{a}(v,e))

which shows that
\varphi^{\prime}:L(\pi)\to_{B}\xi
is a cocartesian functor, as desired.

We now turn to showing that precomposing with
\iota
gives an equivalence

\mathrm{Fun}_{B}(\pi,\xi)\simeq{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B}(L(\pi),\xi).

We define

\Phi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda\psi.\iota^{*}\psi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda\psi.\psi\circ\iota:\mathrm{Fun}_{B}(\pi,\xi)\to{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B}(L(\pi),\xi),

and recall that in the converse direction

\Psi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda\varphi.\varphi^{\prime}:{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B}(L(\pi),\xi)\to\mathrm{Fun}_{B}(\pi,\xi)

with
\varphi^{\prime}_{b}(v,e)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}v_{!}^{Q}(\varphi_{a}\,e)
.
Let
\psi:L\,E\to_{B}F
be a cocartesian functor.
We compute

(\iota^{*}\psi)_{b}^{\prime}(v,e)=v_{!}^{Q}(\iota^{*}\psi_{a}(e))=v_{!}^{Q}(\psi_{a}(\id_{a},e)).

Since
\psi
is cocartesian, we have
v_{!}^{Q}(\psi_{a}(\id_{a},e))=\psi_{b}(v_{!}^{LP}(\id_{a},e))
. Now, the
LP
-cocartesian lift of
v:a\to b
w.r.t
\langle\id_{a},e\rangle
is given by
\langle\id_{a},v,\id_{e}\rangle:\langle\id_{a},e\rangle\to\langle v,e\rangle
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}

As a dependent arrow in
LP
, the codomain of this morphism is the pair
\langle v,e\rangle
. In sum, this means

v_{!}^{Q}(\psi_{a}(\id_{a},e))=\psi_{b}(v_{!}^{LP}(\id_{a},e))=\psi_{b}(v,e)=(\iota^{*}\psi)_{b}^{\prime}(v,e),

i.e.

\Psi(\Phi(\psi))=\psi
.
On the other hand, for an arbitrary fiberwise map
\varphi:E\to_{B}F
, we find that

\lx@nobreakspace(\iota^{*}\varphi)^{\prime}(b,e)=\varphi_{b}^{\prime}(\id_{b},e)=(\id_{b})_{!}^{Q}(\varphi_{b}\,e)=\varphi_{b}(e)

since cocartesian lifts of identities are themselves identities. This gives an identification
\Phi(\Psi(\varphi))=\varphi
.
∎

3
Cocartesian functors

We turn to the study of the right notion of morphism between cocartesian fibrations: The cocartesian functors. These are fibered functors preserving the cocartesian arrows. Again, these can be characterized in terms of Chevalley criteria, and by
[
BW21
]
several closure properties hold, capturing internally the closure properties of the
\infty
-cosmos of cocartesian fibrations of
(\infty,1)
-categories.

1
Definition and properties

Let us first review fibered maps between type families.

Definition 3.1

(Fiberwise maps)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
and
Q:C\to\mathcal{U}
be families. A
fiberwise map
\Phi
from
P
to
Q
is a pair of functions

•

j:B\to C
,

•

\varphi:\prod_{b:B}(P\,b\to Q\,j(b))
.

We call
F
an
equivalence
if
j
and
\varphi
are equivalences.

We write
{\mathrm{Fun}}_{B,C}(P,Q)
for the ensuing type of fiberwise maps.

Note that (by fibrant replacement) the type of commutative squares is equivalent to the type of maps between families.

Observe that given a map between families
P
and
Q
as above we get a strictly commutative square:

\textstyle{\widetilde{P}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle j,\varphi\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{P}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{Q}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{C}

In the above setting, for any
u:\hom_{B}(a,b)
,
a,b
, the fiberwise map
\Phi
acts on arrows over
u
in the following way. For
f:{\mathrm{hom}}_{P,u}(d,e)
,
d:Pa
,
e:Pb
, we define

\varphi_{u}(f):\equiv\lambda t.\varphi_{u(t)}(f(t)):{\mathrm{hom}}_{Q,ju}(\varphi_{a}(d),\varphi_{b}(e)).

One can think of the following picture:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\longrightarrow}}e)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi_{a}d\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi_{u}f}}{{\longrightarrow}}\varphi_{b}e)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u}}{{\longrightarrow}}b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(ja\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ju}}{{\longrightarrow}}jb)}

We now turn to the desired cocartesian functors

Definition 3.2

(Cocartesian functors)
.

If
P
and
Q
are cocartesian families, and furthermore the map

\Phi:\widetilde{P}\to\widetilde{Q},\quad\Phi\,b\,e:\equiv\langle j(b),\varphi_{j}(e)\rangle

preserves cocartesian arrows, then we call
\Phi
a
cocartesian map
:
11
11

11

This is a proposition because being a cocartesian arrow is a proposition.

{\mathrm{isCocartFun}}_{P,Q}(\Phi):\equiv\prod_{f:\Delta^{1}\to\widetilde{P}}{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}_{P}(f)\to{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}_{Q}(\varphi f).

In particular, if
B
and
C
in addition are Segal (or Rezk) types we speak of a
cocartesian functor
.

We define

{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B,C}(P,Q):\equiv\sum_{F:{\mathrm{Fun}}_{B,C}(P,Q)}{\mathrm{isCocartFun}}_{P,Q}(F).

Given families
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
to
Q:B\to\mathcal{U}
, a
fibered functor
from
P\to Q
is a section
\varphi:\prod_{x:B}Px\to Qx
. It is cocartesian if

{\mathrm{isCocartFun}}_{P,Q}(\langle\id_{B},\varphi\rangle)\simeq\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}u:\Delta^{1}\to B\\
f:\Delta^{1}\to u^{*}P\end{subarray}}{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}_{P}(f)\to{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}_{Q}(\varphi f).

We define

{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B}(P,Q):\equiv\sum_{\varphi:\prod_{B}P\to Q}{\mathrm{isCocartFun}}_{P,Q}(\langle\id_{B},\varphi\rangle).

This is, again, an instance of
Definition

11.B.4
, for the map
i_{0}:\mathbf{1}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{1}
.

Proposition 3.3

(Naturality of cocartesian liftings)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type,
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
,
Q:C\to\mathcal{U}
cocartesian families. Then a fibered functor
\Phi\equiv\langle\varphi,j\rangle
from
P
to
Q
is a cocartesian functor if and only if
\Phi
commutes with cocartesian lifts,
i.e.
for any
u:\hom_{B}(a,b)
there is an identification of arrows

\lx@nobreakspace\varphi\big(P_{!}(u,d)\big)=_{\Delta^{1}\to(ju)^{*}Q}Q_{!}(ju,\varphi_{a}d)

and hence of endpoints

\varphi_{b}(u_{!}^{P}d)=_{Q(jb)}(ju)_{!}^{Q}(\varphi_{a}d).\lx@nobreakspace

In particular there is a homotopy commutative square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Pa}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Qa}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Pb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Qb}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u}^{P}_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(ju)}^{Q}_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{b}}

Proof.

The first claim follows formally from
11.B.5
.
Specifically, for the naturality square, we find the following. For
u:\hom_{B}(a,b)
and
d:P\,a
, consider the
P
-cocartesian lift
{P}_{!}({u},{d}):{\mathrm{hom}}_{P,u}(d,{u}^{P}_{!}(d))
. Since
\varphi
is a cocartesian functor the arrow
\varphi_{u}({P}_{!}({u},{d})):{\mathrm{hom}}_{Q,ju}(\varphi_{a}d,\varphi_{b}({u}^{P}_{!}(d)))
is
Q
-cocartesian. On the other hand,
{Q}_{!}({ju},{\varphi_{a}d}):{\mathrm{hom}}_{Q,ju}(\varphi_{a}d,{ju}^{Q}_{!}(\varphi_{a}d))
is as well a
Q
-cocartesian lift of
ju
with domain
\varphi_{a}d
, thus coincides with
\varphi_{u}({P}_{!}({u},{d}))
up to a path, in particular this gives an identification
\varphi_{b}({u}^{P}_{!}(d))={ju}^{Q}_{!}(\varphi_{a}d)
.
∎

Corollary 3.4

(Naturality over a common base (discrete case:
[
RS17
]
, Prop. 8.17))
.

Consider a Rezk type
B
, cocartesian families
P,Q:B\to\mathcal{U}
, and a cocartesian functor
\varphi:{\mathrm{CocartFun}}_{B}(P,Q)
. Then
\varphi
commutes with the actions of arrows,
i.e.
for any
a,b:B
,
u:\hom_{B}(a,b)
,
d:P(a)
, we get an
identification

\varphi_{b}({u}^{P}_{!}(d))=_{Qb}{u}^{Q}_{!}(\varphi_{a}(d)),

thus a homotopy commutative square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Pa}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Qa}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Pb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Qb}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u}^{P}_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u}^{Q}_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{b}}

2
Characterization of cocartesian functors

Theorem 3.5

(
[
RV22
, Theorem 5.3.4]
)
.

Let
A
and
B
be Rezk types, and consider cocartesian families
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
and
Q:A\to\mathcal{U}
with total types
E\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{P}
and
F\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{F}
, resp.

For a fibered functor
\Phi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle j,\varphi\rangle
giving rise to a square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

the following are equivalent:

1.

The fiberwise map
\Phi
is a cocartesian functor.

2.

The mate of the induced canonical fibered natural isomorphism is invertible, too:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rightsquigarrow}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\downarrow j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\downarrow j}

3.

The mate of the induced canonical natural isomorphism is invertible, too:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\downarrow j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\Delta^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rightsquigarrow}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\downarrow j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\Delta^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

Proof.

Consider the situation of the first (fibered) adjunction, where the mate of the canonical isomorphism cell is constructed through the following pasting diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\downarrow j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

The unit
\eta:\hom_{\xi\downarrow A}(\id_{F},\kappa i)
at
\langle u:a\to a^{\prime},d:P_{a}\rangle
is given as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{!}d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{!}(u,d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

The pasting
2
-cell can be identified with the natural transformation

\lx@nobreakspace\alpha:\hom_{F\to\pi\downarrow B}(\kappa^{\prime}\circ\varphi\downarrow j,\varphi\circ\kappa)

whose components at
\langle u:a\to a^{\prime},d:Pa\rangle
are given by the fillers

\alpha_{\langle u,d\rangle}:\equiv(\kappa^{\prime}\circ\varphi\downarrow j)\eta_{\langle u,d\rangle}:\langle jd^{\prime},(ju)_{!}(\varphi d)\rangle\to\langle jd^{\prime},\varphi(u_{!}d)\rangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi(u_{!}d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{!}d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi(d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(ju)_{!}(\varphi d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j(a^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{!}(u,d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rightsquigarrow}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{!}(ju,\varphi d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\langle u,d\rangle}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\big(P_{!}(u,d)\big)}

If
\Phi\equiv\langle j,\varphi\rangle
is a cocartesian functor there is an identification
\varphi(Q_{!}(u,d))=P_{!}(ju,\varphi d)
in
E^{\Delta^{1}}
, hence
\alpha_{\langle u,d\rangle}
is an identity.

On the other hand, if the induced filler
\alpha_{\langle u,d\rangle}
happens to be an isomorphism, and thus an identity, we obtain an identification
\varphi(Q_{!}(u,d))=P_{!}(ju,\varphi d)
rendering
\Phi
a cocartesian functor.

In case of the second adjunction, this is an instance of
Theorem

11.B.6
.
∎

3
Closure properties of cocartesian functors

In
[
BW21
]
, we have shown that one obtains the following list of closure properties, capturing the structure of the
\infty
-cosmos internally:

Proposition 3.6

(Cosmological closure properties of cocartesian families,
[
BW21
, Proposition 5.3.17]
)
.

Over Rezk bases, it holds that:

Cocartesian families are closed under composition, dependent products, pullback along arbitrary maps, and cotensoring with maps/shape inclusions. Families corresponding to equivalences or terminal projections are always cocartesian.

Between cocartesian families over Rezk bases, it holds that:
Cocartesian functors are closed under (both horizontal and vertical) composition, dependent products, pullback, sequential limits,
12
12

12

all three objectwise limit notions satisfying the expected universal properties w.r.t. to cocartesian functors
and Leibniz cotensors.

Fibered equivalences and fibered functors into the identity of
\mathbf{1}
are always cocartesian.

The following proposition is not contained in
[
BW21
]
, and is recorded here for later use in the chapter on two-sided cartesian fibrations.

Proposition 3.7

(Pullback of fibered cocartesian sections)
.

For a Rezk type
B
, consider cocartesian families
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
,
Q:\widetilde{P}\to\mathcal{U}
, and a fiberwise map
\varphi:\prod_{b:B}P\,b\to Q\,b
. We write the unstraightenings as
\pi:E\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{P}\twoheadrightarrow B
,
\xi:F\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{Q}\twoheadrightarrow B
. Consider the following diagram, induced by a section
\ell
of (the totalization of)
\varphi
over
B
, and
A
and a map
k:A\to B
between Rezk types:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}

If
\ell
is a cocartesian functor, then the induced section
\ell^{\prime}
is, too.

Proof.

First, fibrant replacement yields:

E\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{b:B}P\,b,\quad F\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b:B\\
e:P\,b\end{subarray}}Q\,b\,e,E^{\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{a:A}P\,ka,\quad F^{\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a:B\\
d:P\,ka\end{subarray}}Q\,ka\,d.

The section
\ell
is then taken to be

\ell(b,e)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle b,e,\widehat{\ell}(b,e)\rangle

for
b:B
,
e:P\,b
.
Cocartesianness means that there is a path

\displaystyle\ell(u,P_{!}(u,e))

\displaystyle\equiv\langle u,P_{!}(u,e),\widehat{\ell}(u,P_{!}(u,e))\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv\langle u,P_{!}(u,e),Q_{!}(u,\langle u^{P}_{!}e,\widehat{\ell}(b,e)\rangle)\rangle

for
u:b\to_{B}b^{\prime}
,
e:P\,b
.
The induced section
\ell^{\prime}
arises as
\ell^{\prime}(a,d)\equiv\ell(ka,d)\equiv\langle ka,d\widehat{\ell}(ka,d)\rangle
for
a:A
,
d:P^{\prime}\,a\simeq P\,ka
. Applying this to the
P^{\prime}
-cocartesian lift of
v:a\to a^{\prime}
w.r.t.
d:Q\,a
yields

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime}(v,P^{\prime}_{!}(v,d))

\displaystyle\equiv\ell(kv,P^{\prime}_{!}(kv,d))=\ell(kv,P_{!}(v,d))\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle kv,P_{!}(v,d),\widehat{\ell}(kv,P_{!}(v,d))\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle kv,P_{!}(kv,d),Q_{!}(kv,\langle(kv)^{P}_{!}(d),\widehat{\ell}(kv,P_{!}(kv,d))\rangle)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle kv,P^{\prime}_{!}(v,d),Q^{\prime}_{!}(v,\langle v^{P^{\prime}}_{!}(d),\widehat{\ell^{\prime}}(v,P^{\prime}_{!}(v,d))\rangle)\rangle

confirming the claim.
∎

4
Cartesian families

Completely dually, one can formulate a theory of
cartesian families
which are
contravariantly functorial
w.r.t. to directed paths.
I.e.
, for
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
a cartesian family, for any arrow
u:b\to a
and
d:P\,a
there exists a
cartesian
lift
f\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}P^{*}(u,d):u^{*}d\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

, satisfying the dual universal property: For any
v:c\to a
, and any

there exists a filler
g:e\to^{P}_{v}u^{*}\,d
, uniquely up to homotopy, s.t.
h=P^{*}(u,d)\circ g
In particular, this induces a map

u^{*}:P\,a\to P\,b.

Likewise, we have a notion of
cartesian functor
. The Chevalley condition(s) turn out to be
right adjoint
right inverse (RARI) conditions instead. Furthermore, the cartesian arrows are pullback stable, and any dependent arrow factors as
