.

Sometimes, we will distinguish in the notation between cartesian and cocartesian filling by writing
\mathrm{cartFill}_{\ldots}(\ldots)
or
\mathrm{cocartFill}_{\ldots}(\ldots)
, resp. Also, especially in
Chapter

7
, we will denote vertical arrows (resp. their types) by a squigglyarrow
\cdot\rightsquigarrow\cdot
.

1
Lex families

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\zeta_{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\zeta_{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\zeta_{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!^{b}_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{\zeta_{b}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{b}}

Figure 5:
Construction of fiberwise terminal objects

A specific class that becomes important in the next chapter are the so-called
lex families
, which have fibered terminal elements and pullbacks.

Note that, e.g. for a functor
f:A\to B
preserving a terminal object
z:A
is a propositional condition. If
z^{\prime}
denotes the terminal object in
B
there is a path
f(z)=z^{\prime}
if and only if
f(z):B
is terminal,
i.e.

\prod_{b:B}\isContr(b\to_{B}f(z))
if and only if the homotopically unique arrow
!_{f(z)}:f(z)\to z^{\prime}
is an isomorphism.

We will not discuss this further here, but similar considerations hold for limits in general, by their defining universal property as terminal objects of the respective Rezk types of cones.

In principle, we also think in the synthetic setting there could be a more uniform and abstract treating of “
X
-shaped limit fibrations”, for a given shape or type
X
, after
[
Bor94
, Definition 8.5.1]
, but we do not develop this here.

Instead we follow the account of
[
Str21
, Section 8]
, adapting it to the synthetic setting.

The aim is to recover the standard characterization of lex cartesian fibrations: Fix a base with the desired limits. Then the total type has those limits and they are preserved by the fibration if and only if the fibers each have the respective limits, and the reindexing functors preserve them.

First, we consider the case of terminal elements.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a cartesian family and
B
be a Rezk type with terminal object
z:B
. Denote by
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
the unstraightening of
P
. Then the following are equivalent:

1.

The total Rezk type
E
has a terminal object
r
, and
\pi
preserves it,
i.e.

\pi(r):B
is terminal.

2.

For all
b:B
, the fiber
P\,b
has a terminal object, and for all arrows
u:b\to_{B}a
the functor
u^{*}:P\,a\to P\,b
preserves the terminal object.

Proof.

\ref{it:term-obj-fib-i}\implies\ref{it:term-obj-fib-ii}
:

For the visualization of both parts, cf.
Figure

5
. Denote by
\langle z,r\rangle:E
the terminal object of
E
, with
z:B
terminal. For
b:B
, consider the point
\zeta_{b}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}(!_{b})^{*}r:P\,b
. We claim that this is the terminal object of the fiber
P\,b
. Indeed, consider the canonical arrow
!_{r}:e\to_{!_{b}}r
. Then there is a unique arrow
!^{b}_{e}:e\to_{P\,b}\zeta_{b}
s.t.
P^{*}(!_{b},r)\circ!^{b}_{e}=!_{r}
. But by terminality of
r
, the cartesian lift
P^{*}(!_{b},r)
also is propositionally equal to the terminal projection
!_{\zeta_{b}}:\zeta_{b}\to r
. Now, for any given map
g:e\to_{P\,b}\zeta_{b}
we have that
!_{\zeta_{b}}\circ g=!_{e}
, but by the universal property of
!_{\zeta_{b}}
there is only a unique such arrow
g
up to homotopy. Hence,
\zeta_{b}
is terminal in
P\,b
.

Let
u:b\to_{B}a
. We will show that there is a path
u^{*}\,\zeta_{a}=\zeta_{b}
. As we have just seen, we have
\zeta_{a}=(!_{a})^{*}(r)
, and similarly for
\zeta_{b}
. Consider their terminal projections to
r
, which are necessarily cartesian arrows. From this and the identification
!_{b}=!_{a}\circ u
in
B
, we get a unique filler
h:\zeta_{b}\to_{u}\zeta_{a}
. Moreover,
h
is cartesian by left cancelation, so
h=P^{*}(u,\zeta_{a}):\zeta_{b}\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.28 0 L 0.55 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}
}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{u}^{P}\zeta_{a}
. This establishes the desired path.

\ref{it:term-obj-fib-i}\implies\ref{it:term-obj-fib-ii}
:

Conversely, consider the section
\zeta:\prod_{b:B}P\,b
choosing the terminal element in each fiber. Let
b:B
. By assumption, the cartesian lift of the terminal map
!_{b}:b\to z
has
\zeta_{a}
as its source vertex, up to a path. Let
e:P\,b
be some point. Since
\zeta_{b}
is terminal in
P\,b
, there exists a unique morphism
!_{e}^{b}:e\to_{P\,b}\zeta_{b}
, and post-composition with the cartesian lift
P^{*}(!_{b},\zeta_{z}):\zeta_{b}\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.28 0 L 0.55 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}
}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\zeta_{z}
gives a morphism
t_{e}:e\to\zeta_{z}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\zeta_{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\zeta_{z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{e}^{b}}

Finally, any morphism
f:e\to\zeta_{z}
, up to homotopy, lies over
!_{b}:b\to z
, and necessarily has the same factorization again, hence is identified with
t_{e}
. Therefore,
\langle z,\zeta_{z}\rangle
defines the (“global”) terminal element of
E
, and we have
\pi(z,\zeta_{z})\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}z
.

∎

We are now turning to the analogous statement for pullbacks, which requires more preparation.

First, we give two conditions on dependent squares being pullbacks.

Lemma 4.2

(
[
Str21
, Lemma 8.1(1)]
)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a cartesian family. Writing
E
for the total type, then any a square in
E
all of whose sides are cartesian arrows, is a pullback.

Proof.

Consider a square in
E
together with a cone, and the fillers induced by cartesianness of
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
, resp.:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

We have
fh=(fg^{\prime})m
and
gh^{\prime}=g(f^{\prime}m^{\prime})=(fg^{\prime})m^{\prime}
. But also
fg=gh^{\prime}
, so
(fg^{\prime})m=(fg^{\prime})m^{\prime}
. But since
fg^{\prime}
is cartesian as the composition of two cartesian arrows,
m=m^{\prime}
as desired.
∎

Lemma 4.3

(
[
Str21
, Lemma 8.1(2)]
)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a fibration. Then any dependent square in
P
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}

is a pullback.

Proof.

Consider a point
d
and maps
h:d\to^{\prime}e
,
h^{\prime}:d\to e^{\prime\prime}
s.t.
g^{\prime}h^{\prime}=fh
. By cartesianness of
f^{\prime}
, there uniquely exists
k:d\to e^{\prime\prime\prime}
s.t.
f^{\prime}k=h^{\prime}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(?)}

To show that also
h=gk
, it suffices to show that
f(gk)=g^{\prime}h^{\prime}
, since also
fh=g^{\prime}h^{\prime}
, which taken together implies
h=gk
by cartesianness of
f
. Indeed, by the above we have a chain of paths

f(gk)=(fg)k=(g^{\prime}f^{\prime})k=g^{\prime}(f^{\prime}k^{\prime})=g^{\prime}h^{\prime},

which implies the claim that
h=gk
. Furthermore,
k
is already unique with the property
f^{\prime}k=h^{\prime}
, so we are done.
∎

The next lemma presents a sufficient condition for the “local” pullbacks being “global” pullbacks.

Lemma 4.4

(cf.
[
Str21
, Lemma 8.2]
)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a cartesian family and
B
be a Rezk type where all pullbacks exist. Assuming that all fibers have pullbacks, and these are preserved by the reindexing functors, we have: A pullback in a fiber
P\,b
is also a pullback in
\widetilde{P}
.

Proof.

Let
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
be the unstraightening of
P
.

For some
b:B
, consider a pullback square in
P\,b
, together with a cone in
E
, as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}

(2)

Projecting down we find that
\pi(h_{1})=u=\pi(h_{2})
for some
u:b\to_{B}a
. Consider the factorizations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u^{*}\,e_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{n}}

for
n=1,2
.
We claim that
(u^{*}\,f_{1})\circ m_{1}=(u^{*}\,f_{2})\circ m_{2}
. To see this, consider the following induced diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u^{*}\,e_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u^{*}\,e_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u^{*}\,e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}\,f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}\,f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{1}}

Now, in fact the remaining sub-square commutes as well because both sides are equalized by the cartesian arrow
k
: By assumption we have
(f_{1}k_{1})m_{1}=(f_{2}k_{2})m_{2}
,
i.e.

(k(u^{*}f_{1}))m_{1}=(k(u^{*}f_{2}))m_{2}
, hence
(u^{*}f_{1})m_{1}=(u^{*}f_{2})m_{2}
as claimed.

Now, by assumption the square of vertical arrows in (
2
), is a pullback in
P\,b
, and gets preserved by
u^{*}:P\,a\to P\,b
. Then the gap map
\ell:d\rightsquigarrow u^{*}\,e^{\prime}
as indicated below is vertical:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u^{*}\,e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u^{*}\,e_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u^{*}\,e_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u^{*}\,e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}

Then, for
k^{\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}P^{*}(u,e^{\prime}):u^{*}\,e^{\prime}\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.28 0 L 0.55 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}
}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{u}e^{\prime}
we claim that the mediating arrow for the original diagram
2
is given by

\ell^{\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}k^{\prime}\circ\ell:d\to e^{\prime}.

Indeed, we find

g_{n}\ell^{\prime}=g_{n}(k^{\prime}\ell)=(k_{n}\circ u^{*}\,g_{n})\ell=k_{n}m_{n}=h_{n}

for
n=1,2
.
Furthermore,
\ell^{\prime}
is unique with this property because its vertical component is determined uniquely up to homotopy as a gap map of a pullback in
P\,b
.
∎

Finally, we can state the desired chracaterization.

Proposition 4.5

(cf.
[
Str21
, Theorem 8.3]
)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a cartesian family and
B
be a Rezk type where all pullbacks exist. Denote by
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
the unstraightening of
P
. Then the following are equivalent:

1.

The total Rezk type
E
has all pullbacks, and
\pi
preserves them.

2.

For all
b:B
, the fiber
P\,b
has all pullbacks, and for all arrows
u:b\to_{B}a
the functor
u^{*}:P\,a\to P\,b
preserves them.

Proof.

Since
\pi
preserves pullbacks, every pullback of vertical arrows in a fiber
P\,b
is a pullback in
P\,b
,
i.e.
given a cone of vertical arrows, the mediating arrow is necessarily vertical as well. What is left to show is that the reindexing functors preserve the pullbacks. Let
u:b\to_{B}a
. Consider a pullback square in
P\,a
, together with the cartesian liftings of
u
w.r.t. to each point. Then, by
Lemma

4.3
the ensuing squares are pullbacks, as indicated in:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u^{*}\,e^{\prime\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u^{*}\,e^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u^{*}\,e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u^{*}\,e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

By
[
Rij20
, Remark 26.1.5(ii)]
, we obtain that the left hand square is a pullback, as desired.

Conversely, consider a cospan an
E
, comprised of dependent arrows
(f:d\to e\leftarrow d^{\prime}:f^{\prime})
. First, we consider their vertical/cartesian-factorizations,
f=gk
,
f^{\prime}=g^{\prime}m
. This gives rise to the following situation, which we will readily explain:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{\prime}}

First of all, the diagram
\tau
is a pulback by
Lemma

4.2
. The (vertical) fillers
k^{\prime}
and
m^{\prime}
, resp. are induced by
h^{\prime}
and
h
being cartesian, resp. Then by
Lemma

4.3
, the squares
\sigma
,
\sigma^{\prime}
are pullbacks, too. Since the fibers have pullbacks, the square
\tau^{\prime}
exists. As the reindexings preserve the local pullbacks, we can apply
Lemma

4.4
, so
\tau^{\prime}
is a pullback in
E
. Altogether, this yields the pullback square of
f^{\prime}
along
f
.
∎

Definition 4.6

(Lex families)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type with terminal object and all pullbacks. A cartesian fibration over
B
is
lex
if it satisfies the conditions from
Propositions

4.1
and
4.5
,
i.e.
all the fibers of
P
have terminal objects and pullbacks, and both notions are preserved by the reindexing functors.

A Rezk type is
lex
if it it has all pullbacks and a terminal object. A functor between Rezk types is a
lex functor
if it preserves these notions.

By the above, this is equivalent to
\widetilde{P}
having terminal objects and pullbacks, and the projection to
B
preserving them.

Chapter 7
Bicartesian families of synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categories

In this section, we consider families that are both cartesian and concartesian, corresponding to
bicartesian fibrations
. Specifically, we are interested in such fibrations satisfying a so-called Beck–Chevalley condition (BCC). This form of the BCC has its origins in the work of Bénabou–Roubaud leading to their famous chracterization of descent data of a fibration
[
BR70
]
. In the
(\infty,1)
-categorical context such fibrations play a role in homotopical ambidexterity
[
HL13
]
.

In our context, we are eventually interested in a specific subclass of BCC fibrations, which go by the name of
(l)extensive
or
Moens fibrations
. These are a fibrational generalization of (l)extensive categories
[
CLW93
]
.

Ultimately, this leads to Moens’ Theorem which says that Moens fibrations over a fixed base type can be identified with lex functors
from
this type into some other lex type. This is crucial to develop the
fibered view of geometric morphisms
, cf.
[
Str21
, Section 15
et seq
]
,
[
Str97
,
Lie98
]
. Applications in realizability have been given by Frey in his doctoral thesis
[
Fre14
]
.

We successively generalize Streicher’s exposition and proofs
[
Str21
, Section 15]
to the synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categorical setting, also making explicit some arguments not detailed in
op. cit.

1
Bicartesian families

1
Bicartesian families

Definition 1.1
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type. A
bicartesian family
is a type family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
which is both cartesian and cocartesian.

Bicartesian families are hence equipped with both co- and contravariant transport operations for directed arrows. In fact, these induce adjunctions on the fibers.

Proposition 1.2
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a bicartesian family. For any
a,b:B
,
u:a\to_{B}b
, there is an adjunction:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P\,a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P\,b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Proof.

For fixed
a,b:B
, we define a pair of maps

\Phi:(u_{!}\,d\to e)\rightleftarrows(d\to u^{*}e):\Psi,

intended to be quasi-inverse to each other, through

\Phi(g)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathrm{cartFill}_{P^{*}(u,e)}^{P}(g\circ P_{!}(u,d)),\quad\Psi(k)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathrm{cocartFill}_{P_{!}(u,d)}^{P}(P^{*}(u,e)\circ k).

We write
f\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}P_{!}(u,d):d\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}u_{!}\,d
and
r\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}P^{*}(u,e):u^{*}e\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.28 0 L 0.55 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

{{}}
}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}e
. For a dependent arrow
g:u_{!}\,d\to^{P}e
we have
g^{\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Phi(g)\circ r=g\circ f
by construction. Next, we find that
\Psi(g^{\prime})\circ f=r\circ\Phi(g)
. Combining these identities it follows that
\Psi(g^{\prime})=\Psi(\Phi(g))=g
by cocartesianness of
f
. The other roundtrip is analogous.
∎

We now explain a few important constructions producing bicartesian fibrations.

2
The family fibration

Recall the construction of the free cocartesian fibration associated to an arbitrary map
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
between Rezk types.

Definition 1.3
.

If the base
B
has all pullbacks and
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
is assumed to be a cartesian fibration, then the free cocartesian fibration

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L(\pi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

is itself a also cartesian fibration. This bifibration is called the
family fibration associated to
P
.

Proposition 1.4

(Cartesian lifts in the family fibration)
.

Let
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
be a cartesian fibration over a Rezk type
B
with all pullbacks. The cartesian lift of an arrow
u:a\to b
in
B
w.r.t.
\langle v:b^{\prime}\to b,e:P\,b^{\prime}\rangle
in
L(\pi)
is given by
\langle\mathrm{pb}(u,v),P^{*}(v^{*}u,e)\rangle
.

Proof.

This follows by computing the lifts fiberwisely, cf.
[
BW21
, Subsection 5.2.3]
.
∎

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L(\pi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(u^{\prime})^{*}e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a\times_{b}b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{*}(u^{\prime},e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}^{\prime}}

Figure 1:
Cartesian lifts in the family fibration

3
The Artin gluing fibration

Definition 1.5

(Artin gluing)
.

Let
B,C
be Rezk types and
F:B\to C
a functor. Then the map
\mathrm{gl}(F):C\mathbin{\downarrow}F\twoheadrightarrow B
constructed by pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\downarrow F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\Delta^{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{gl}(F)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

is called the
Artin gluing
(or simply
gluing
) of
F
.

Since the codomain projection
\partial_{1}:C^{\Delta^{1}}\twoheadrightarrow C
is always a cocartesian fibration the gluing
\mathrm{gl}(F)
is a cocartesian fibration as well. We will be concerned with the case that
C
has all pullbacks. In this case
\partial_{1}:C^{\Delta^{1}}\twoheadrightarrow C
also is a cartesian fibration, hence a bifibration, and consequently the same is true for
\mathrm{gl}(F):C\mathbin{\downarrow}F\twoheadrightarrow B
.

Hence, from the description of the co-/cocartesian lifts in pullback fibrations, the respective lifts in
\mathrm{gl}(F)
can be computed as illustrated in
Figure

2
. Given an arrow
u:b\to b^{\prime}
in
B
, a cocartesian lift w.r.t. an arrow
v:c\to F\,b
in
C
is the square with boundary
[v,\id_{b},F\,u,F\,u\circ v]
. A cartesian lift of
u:b\to b^{\prime}
w.r.t. an arrow
w:b\to F\,c^{\prime}
is given by the pullback square
\mathrm{pb}(F\,u,w)
.

A vertical arrow in the gluing fibration is exactly given by a square of the form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\downarrow F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\,b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\,b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\downarrow F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\,b\times_{F\,b^{\prime}}c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\,b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\,b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\,b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\,b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\,u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fu\circ v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\,u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

Figure 2:
Cocartesian and cartesian lifts in the gluing fibration

2
Beck–Chevalley families

In categorical logic, Beck–Chevalley conditions say that substitution (
i.e.
pullback) commutes with existential quantification (
i.e.
dependent sums). This generalizes to the fibrational setting by considering cartesian arrows in place of substitutions (acting contravariantly), and cocartesian arrows in place of dependent sums (acting covariantly). We refer to
[
Str21
, Section 6]
for more explanation.

1
Beck–Chevalley condition

Definition 2.1

(Beck–Chevalley condition,
[
Str21
, Definition 6.1]
)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an isoinner family over a Rezk type. Then
P
is said to satisfy the
Beck–Chevalley condition (BCC)
if for any dependent square of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

it holds that: if
f
is cocartesian, and
g,g^{\prime}
are cartesian, then
f^{\prime}
is cocartesian.

Proposition 2.2

(Dual of the Beck–Chevalley conditions)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an isoinner family over a Rezk type. Then
P
satisfies the BCC
Definition

2.1
if and only if it satisfies the
dual BCC
, which says: Given any dependent square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

in
P
over a pullback, then: If
f
and
f^{\prime}
are cocartesian, and
g^{\prime}
is cartesian, then
g
is cartesian as well.

Proof.

Assume, the BCC from
Definition

2.1
is satisfied. We consider a square, factoring the arrow
g
as a vertical arrow followed by a cartesian arrow, we obtain:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v^{*}e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}

Then, applying the BCC to the “smaller” square (still lying over the same pullback since
m
is vertical),
m^{\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}m\circ f^{\prime}
must be cocartesian. But then
m
is, too, by right cancelation, as
f^{\prime}
is cocartesian. But since it is also vertical, it is an isomorphism, so
g
is cartesian, as claimed.

The converse direction is analogous.
∎

2
Beck–Chevalley families

Preparing the treatment of Moens fibrations, we state a few first results about BCC fibrations,
aka

fibrations with internal sums
.

Definition 2.3
.

A map between
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
is a
Beck–Chevalley fibration
or a
cartesian fibration with internal sums
if:

1.

The map
\pi
is a bicartesian fibration,
i.e.
a cartesian and cocartesian fibration.

2.

The map
\pi
satisfies the Beck–Chevalley condition.

Recall the criterion characterizing cocartesian fibrations via the existence of a fibered left adjoint which acts as the “cocartesian transport” functor. The Beck–Chevalley condition is equivalent to this functor being
cartesian
.

Theorem 2.4

(Beck–Chevalley fibrations via cartesianness of the coocartesian transport functor, cf.
[
Str21
, Theorem 6.1]
)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a cartesian family over a Rezk type
B
which has all pullbacks, with unstraightening
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
a cartesian fibration. Then
\pi
is a Beck–Chevalley fibration if and only if the mediating fibered functor

\lx@nobreakspace\iota_{P}:E\to_{B}\pi\mathbin{\downarrow}B,~\iota_{P}(b,e)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle b,\id_{b},e\rangle

has a fibered left adjoint which is also a cartesian functor:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi\downarrow B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Proof.

Recall from
Theorem

2.7
that the existence of the fibered left adjoint is equivalent to
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
being a
cocartesian
fibration (already without requiring
B
to have pullbacks and
\pi
to be a cartesian fibration). For
b:B
the action of the fiberwise map
\tau\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\tau_{\pi}
at
b:B
is given by

\tau_{b}(v:a\to b,e:P(a))\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\partial_{1}\,P_{!}(v,e).

Let
u:a\to b
be a morphism in
B
. Over
u
, the action on arrows of
\tau
maps a pair
\langle\sigma,f\rangle
consisting of a commutative square
\sigma
in
B
with boundary
[v^{\prime},u^{\prime},u,v]
and a dependent arrow
f:d\to^{P}_{u^{\prime}}e
to the dependent arrow

\tau_{u}(\sigma,f)=\mathrm{fill}_{P_{!}(v,e)\circ f}(P_{!}(v^{\prime},e)),

cf.
Figure

3
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v^{\prime}_{!}(d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v_{!}(e)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{!}(v^{\prime},d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{u}(\sigma,f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{!}(v,e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rightsquigarrow}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

Figure 3:
Action on arrows of
\tau
(over
u:a\to b
in
B
)

Recall the description of cartesian lifts in the family fibration,
Proposition

1.4
. Then,
\tau
mapping these
L(P)
-cartesian lifts to
P
-cartesian arrows is equivalent to the Beck–Chevalley condition.
∎

Next is a useful result stating that any functor (between Rezk types with pullbacks) preserves pullback if and only if its Artin gluing satisfies the Beck–Chevalley condition.

Proposition 2.5

(Internal sums for gluing,
[
Str21
, Lemma 13.2]
)
.

Let
A
and
B
be Rezk types with pullbacks and
F:A\to B
an arbitrary functor (hence an isoinner map). Then the following are equivalent:

1.

The functor
F
preserves pullbacks.

2.

The gluing fibration
\mathrm{gl}(F)\equiv\partial_{1}:B\mathbin{\downarrow}F\twoheadrightarrow A
is a Beck–Chevalley fibration.

Proof.

We note first that since
B
has all pullbacks,
\mathrm{gl}(F):B\mathbin{\downarrow}F\twoheadrightarrow A
is a fibration since it is a pullback of the fundamental fibration
\partial_{1}:B^{\Delta^{1}}\twoheadrightarrow B
. Hence,
\mathrm{gl}(F)
is a bifibration in this case.

\ref{it:gl-intsums-i}\implies\ref{it:gl-intsums-ii}
:

For a pullback square in
A
we consider a square lying over in the gluing fibration which is a cube as in
Figure

4

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}\times_{a}c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}\times_{b}c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\downarrow F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}b^{\prime}\times_{b}a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{*}v^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(v^{\prime})^{*}v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces uv^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(uv^{\prime})^{*}w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w^{*}(uv^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime}}

Figure 4:
Verifying the Beck–Chevalley condition

where the pullback square at the bottom of the cube is the image of the given pullback square in
C
. By composition and right cancelation of pullbacks, the square on the left is also a pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}\times_{a}c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}\times_{b}c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(v^{\prime})^{*}v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(uv^{\prime})^{*}w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w^{*}(uv^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces uv^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Then,
r^{\prime}
turns out to be an isomorphism. By Rezk-completeness, it can be taken to be the identity
\id_{c}:c\to c
, exhibiting the back square of the cube as a cocartesian arrow in the gluing fibration, as desired.

\ref{it:gl-intsums-ii}\implies\ref{it:gl-intsums-i}
:

From the Beck–Chevalley condition we obtain, for any pullback square in
A
a commutative cube above as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\downarrow F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces uv^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(uv^{\prime})^{*}w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w^{*}(uv^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Since the right outer square is a pullback, by composition also the bottom square is. This shows that
\mathrm{gl}(F)
preserves pullbacks.

∎

3
Moens families

1
(Pre-)Moens families and internal sums

Recall from classical
1
-category theory that a category
\mathbb{C}
with pullbacks and coproducts is
extensive
(or
lextensive
depending on convention) if and only if, for all small families
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
the induced functor
\prod_{i\in I}\mathbb{C}/A_{i}\to\mathbb{C}/\coprod_{i\in I}A_{i}
is an equivalence. This is equivalent to the condition that injections of finite sums are stable under pullback, and for any family of squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\coprod_{i\in I}A_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{k}}

all of these are pullbacks if and only if all
g_{k}:B_{k}\to B
are coproduct cones. This generalizes fibrationally as follows.

Definition 3.1

(Stable and disjoint internal sums)
.

Let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a lex fibration with internal sums over a Rezk type
B
. Then
P
has
stable
internal sums if cocartesian arrows are stable under arbitrary pullbacks. The internal sums of
P
are
disjoint
1
1

1

In a category, a coproduct is
disjoint
if the inclusion maps are monomorphisms, and the intersection of the summands is an initial object.
if for every cocartesian arrow
f:d\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

the fibered diagonal is cocartesian, too:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d\times_{e}d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Definition 3.2

((Pre-)Moens families)
.

Let
B
be a lex Rezk type. A lex Beck–Chevalley family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
is a
pre-Moens family
if it has stable internal sums. We call a pre-Moens family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}

Moens family
(or
extensive family
or
pre-geometric family
) if, moreover, all its (stable) internal sums are also disjoint.

An immediate result is the following.

Lemma 3.3

(
[
Str21
]
, Lem. 15.1)
.

Let
B
be a lex Rezk type and
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
be a Moens fibration. Then, for
d,e,e^{\prime}:E
, and cocartesian morphisms
g:e\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}e^{\prime}
, in any pullback of the following form, the gap map is cocartesian, too:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

Proof.

Consider the following diagram, arising from canonical factorizations over the pullbacks
d^{\prime}\equiv d\times_{e^{\prime}}e
and
e\times_{e}e^{\prime}
, resp:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e\times_{e^{\prime}}e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}

Extensive internal sums

We can now provide a characterization of Moens families among the BCC families.

In particular, we obtain a fibered version of
Lawvere-extensivity
as an alternative characterization for (internal) extensivity. Classically, a category
\mathbb{C}
is
Lawvere-extensive
if for any small set
I
, the categories
\mathbb{C}^{I}
and
\mathbb{C}/\coprod_{i\in I}\mathbf{1}
are canonically isomorphic.

To prepare, consider first the following construction.

Let
B
be a lex Rezk type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a cocartesian family.

Definition 3.5

(Terminal transport functor)
.

For a terminal element
z:B
, we define the functor
4
4

4

In
[
Str21
]
, the functor
\omega
is called
\mathbf{\Delta}
.
5
5

5

Note that we can suppress the dependency on a specified terminal element
z:B
.

\lx@nobreakspace\omega_{P,z}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\omega:\widetilde{P}\to P\,z,\quad\omega\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda b,e.(!_{b})_{!}(e).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\omega(e)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\omega(e^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{!}(!_{b^{\prime}},e^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{!}(!_{b},e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{b^{\prime}}}

Figure 5:
Action on morphisms of the transport functor
\omega_{P,z}

The action on arrows of this functor is illustrated in
5
. The arrow
\omega_{f}
is vertical over the terminal element
z:B
, for any
f:\Delta^{1}\to B
.

Definition 3.6

(Choice of terminal elements)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an isoinner family such that every fiber has a terminal element. Then we denote, by the Principle of Choice, the section choosing fiberwise terminal elements by

\lx@nobreakspace\zeta_{P}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\zeta:\prod_{b:B}P\,b,

i.e.
for any
b:B
the element
\zeta_{b}:P\,b
is terminal.

We define

\omega^{\prime}\equiv\omega^{\prime}_{P}:B\to P\,z,\quad\omega^{\prime}(b)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\omega(\zeta_{b})\equiv(!_{b})_{!}(\zeta_{b}).

We are now ready for the promised characterization.

Proposition 3.7

(Stable disjoint sums in terms of extensive sums,
[
Str21
]
, Lem. 15.3)
.

Let
B
be a lex Rezk type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a Beck–Chevalley family. Then, the following are equivalent:

1.

The family
P
is a Moens family,
i.e.

P
has stable disjoint sums.

2.

The bicartesian family
P
has
internally extensive
sums,
i.e.
for vertical arrows
f:d\to d^{\prime}
,
k:e\to e^{\prime}
, cocartesian arrows
g:e\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

, in a square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

the arrow
h:d\to e
is a cocartesian arrow if and only if the square is a pullback.

3.

The internal sums in
P
are
Lawvere-extensive
,
i.e.
in any square of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\zeta_{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\omega^{\prime}(a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{d}^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{!}(!_{a},\zeta_{a})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

where
k:e\rightsquigarrow\omega^{\prime}(a)
is vertical the arrow
h:d\to e
is cocartesian if and only if the given square is a pullback.

4.

Let
z:B
be a terminal element in
B
. For any
a:B
, the transport functor
(!_{a})_{!}:P\,a\to P\,z
reflects isomorphisms and
k^{*}P_{!}(!_{a},\zeta_{a})
is cocartesian in case
k
is vertical.

Again, as remarked by Streicher, the equivalences between all but the first statement hold already in the case that cocartesian arrows are only stable under pullback along vertical arrows.

Proof.

(\ref{it:ext-sums-stable})\implies(\ref{it:ext-sums-ext})
:

Consider a square as given in (
2
). If it is a pullback we have an identification
h=k^{*}g
, and by stability
h
is cocartesian, too. Conversely, given such a square where
h
is cocartesian, consider the factorization:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

The arrow
g^{\prime}=k^{*}g
is cocartesian by stability of sums. The arrow
k^{\prime}=g^{*}k
is vertical since
k
is. By the same reason, they are left cancelable, hence
f^{\prime}
is vertical. But since
P
is a Moens family, by
Proposition

3.4
, cocartesian arrows also satisfy left cancelation, hence
f^{\prime}
is cocartesian, too, and thus an equivalence.

(\ref{it:ext-sums-ext})\implies(\ref{it:ext-sums-lawvere})
:

The latter is an instance of the former.

(\ref{it:ext-sums-lawvere})\implies(\ref{it:ext-sums-transp})
:

By assumption, for any vertical arrow
f:d\rightsquigarrow d^{\prime}
in
P\,a
, the induced arrow
(!_{a})_{!}(f):(!_{a})_{!}(d)\to(!_{a})_{!}(d^{\prime})
is a path:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(!_{a})_{!}(d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(!_{a})_{!}(d^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(!_{a})_{!}(f)}

Consider the cube induced by cocartesian filling w.r.t.
P_{!}(!_{a},\zeta_{a})\circ!_{d}
and
P_{!}(!_{a},\zeta_{a})\circ!_{d}^{\prime}
, resp.:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(!_{a})_{!}(d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\zeta_{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\omega^{\prime}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(!_{a})_{!}(d^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\zeta_{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\omega^{\prime}(a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{d^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{fill}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{fill}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

The bottom and top squares are pullbacks by Lawvere extensivity. Then, by
Proposition

12.A.2
, the map
f
is an identity as well.

For
a:B
and a vertical arrow
k:e\rightsquigarrow_{z}\omega^{\prime}(a)
consider the pullback square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\zeta_{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\omega^{\prime}(a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{!}(!_{a},\zeta_{a})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Since
P
is a bifibration,
k^{\prime}
is vertical. By Lawvere extensivity,
f
is cocartesian.

(\ref{it:ext-sums-transp})\implies(\ref{it:ext-sums-lawvere})
:

Given a square as in
(\ref{it:ext-sums-lawvere})
, we see that the arrow
h
is cocartesian if and only if it is a pullback, by the second condition in
(\ref{it:ext-sums-lawvere})
.

(\ref{it:ext-sums-lawvere})\implies(\ref{it:ext-sums-ext})
:

This is mostly analogous to
[
Str21
, Lemma 15.3]
.

∎

Corollary 3.8

(
[
Str21
, Corollary 15.4]
)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
a Moens family.

Then, for all arrows
u:a\to b
in
B
and points
d:P\,a
, the functor

u_{!}\mathbin{\downarrow}d:P\,a\mathbin{\downarrow}d\to P\,b\mathbin{\downarrow}u_{!}d

is an equivalence.
In particular, for
P\,a\simeq P\,a\mathbin{\downarrow}\zeta_{a}
we have equivalences

u_{!}\mathbin{\downarrow}\zeta_{a}:P\,a\mathbin{\downarrow}\zeta_{a}\simeq P\,b\mathbin{\downarrow}u_{!}\zeta_{a},\quad(!_{a})_{!}\mathbin{\downarrow}\zeta_{a}:P\,a\mathbin{\downarrow}\zeta_{a}\simeq P\,z\mathbin{\downarrow}\omega^{\prime}(a).

Corollary 3.9

(Left exactness of covariant transport in Moens families,
[
Str21
, Corollary 15.5]
)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
a Moens family.

Then for all
u:a\to b
in
B
, the covariant transport functor

\lx@nobreakspace u_{!}:P\,a\to P\,b

is left exact.

As one crucial ingredient for Moens’ Theorem, the gluing of pullback-preserving functors always is a Moens fibration.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
B
and
C
be a lex Rezk types and
F:B\to C
be a pullback-preserving functor. Then
\mathrm{gl}(F):C\mathbin{\downarrow}F\twoheadrightarrow B
is a Moens fibration.

Proof.

Since
F
preserves pullback,
\mathrm{gl}(F)
is a Beck–Chevalley fibration by
Proposition

2.5
. Then, by
Proposition

3.7
, it suffices to prove that the internal sums in
\mathrm{gl}(F)
are extensive. But this follows from considering a dependent cube as given in
Figure

6
and the fact that
k:c\to c^{\prime}
is an isomorphism if and only if the top square is a pullback.
6
6

6

The identities in the cube are part of the prerequisites to prove extensivity.

∎

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\,b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\,b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\downarrow F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\,b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\,b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\,b}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}

\mathrm{gl}(F)

Lemma 3.11

(cf.
[
Str21
, Lemma 15.6]
)
.

Let
B
be a lex Rezk type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a Moens fibration. Then the gap arrow in any diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime\prime}}

is cocartesian as well.

Proof.

Since
P
is a Moens fibration, cocartesian arrows and (vertical arrows, too) are stable under pullback along arbitrary arrows. By the Pullback Lemma, this gives rise to the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime\prime}}

Hence,
f^{\prime\prime}
is (up to identification) the composition of two cocartesian arrows, hence cocartesian itself.
∎

Proposition 3.12

(Left exactness of terminal transport,
[
Str21
, Lemma 15.16]
)
.

Let
B
be a lex Rezk type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a Moens family. Then the terminal transport functor
\omega:\widetilde{P}\to P\,z
is lex.

Proof.

Preservation of the terminal object follows from

\lx@nobreakspace\omega(z,\zeta_{z})\equiv(!_{z})_{!}(\zeta_{z})=\id_{\zeta_{z}}.

Consider a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

in
E
, where
f
lies over an arrow
u
, and
g
over an arrow
v
in
B
. Considering the induced diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v_{!}\,e^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u_{!}\,e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

by
Lemma

3.11
, we find that the gap map
h
is cocartesian. By naturality, the image of the outer diagram under
\omega
is identifiable with the image of
\sigma
under cocartesian transport
(!_{b})_{!}
, which preserves pullbacks by
Corollary

3.9
.
∎

2
Moens’ Theorem

We are now ready to prove a version of Moens’ Theorem,
7
7

7

Note that in the absence of categorical universes, we have to consider the “naive”
\Sigma
-types instead of the respective Rezk types of fibrations or lex functors, resp. Thus, our version of the theorem should be considered as a statement “on the level of objects” but not arrows.
chracterizing the type of Moens fibrations over a fixed lex base as the type of lex functors from this type into some other lex type.

Theorem 3.13

(Moens’ Theorem,
[
Str21
, Theorem 5.18]
)
.

For a small lex Rezk type
B:\mathcal{U}
the type

\mathrm{MoensFam}(B)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{P:B\to\mathcal{U}}\mathrm{isMoensFam}\,P

of
\mathcal{U}
-small Moens families is equivalent to the type

\lx@nobreakspace B\downarrow^{\mathrm{lex}}\mathrm{LexRezk}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{C:\mathrm{LexRezk}}(B\to^{\mathrm{lex}}C)

of lex functors from
B
into the type
\mathrm{LexRezk}
of
\mathcal{U}
-small lex Rezk types.

Proof.

We define a pair of quasi-inverses

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{MoensFam}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\downarrow^{\mathrm{lex}}\mathrm{LexRezk}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

by setting

\displaystyle\Phi(P:B\to\mathcal{U})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\omega_{P}^{\prime}\equiv\lambda b.(!_{b})_{!}(\zeta_{b}):B\to P\,z,

\displaystyle\Psi(F:B\to C)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\big(\mathrm{gl}(F):C\mathbin{\downarrow}F\to B\big).

The values of
\Phi
and
\Psi
, resp., are indeed terms of the respective types due to
Propositions

3.12
and
3.10
.

For the first roundtrip, let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be a Moens family. We have
\Phi(P)\equiv\omega_{P}^{\prime}:B\to P\,z
and
\Psi(\Phi(P))\equiv\mathrm{gl}(\omega^{\prime}):P\,z\mathbin{\downarrow}\omega^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow B
. We want to give an identification
P=\mathrm{gl}(\omega^{\prime}_{P})
in the type of Moens families over
B
which amounts to a fiberwise equivalence
\widetilde{P}\simeq_{B}P\,z\mathbin{\downarrow}\omega_{P}^{\prime}
. We are to define a pair of fibered quasi-inverses:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P\,z\downarrow\omega^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

We introduce the following notation. For
b:B
,
e:P\,b
, consider the following canonical square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(!_{b})_{!}e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\zeta_{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\omega^{\prime}(b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{e}^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{e}}

We denote by
!_{e}^{b}:e\to\zeta_{b}
the terminal map of
e
in
P\,b
, and by
\nu_{b}:\zeta_{b}\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.28 0 L 0.55 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}
}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{!_{b}}\omega^{\prime}(b)
the cocartesian lift of
!_{b}
w.r.t.
\zeta_{b}:P\,b
.

By
\kappa_{e}
, we denote the cocartesian lift of
!_{b}
w.r.t.
e:P\,b
. We abbreviate by
\mu_{e}:(!_{b})_{!}e\to\omega^{\prime}(b)
the filler

\lx@nobreakspace\mu_{e}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathrm{fill}_{\kappa_{e}}(\nu_{e}\circ!_{e}^{b}).

Then, we define

\varphi:\prod_{b:B}P\,b\to P\,z\mathbin{\downarrow}\omega^{\prime}\,b,\quad\varphi_{b}(e)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mu_{e}:(!_{b})_{!}e\rightsquigarrow_{z}\omega^{\prime}(b)

and

\psi:\prod_{b:B}P\,z\mathbin{\downarrow}\omega^{\prime}\,b\to P\,b,\quad\psi_{b}(f:e\rightsquigarrow_{z}\omega^{\prime}(b))\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\zeta_{b}\times_{\omega^{\prime}(b)}e:P\,b.

For the first part of the round trip, we take
e:P\,b
which gets mapped to
\varphi_{b}(e)=\mu_{e}:(!_{b})_{!}e\rightsquigarrow_{z}\omega^{\prime}(b)
. Computing the pullback
\psi_{b}(\mu_{e})
recovers
e
by
Proposition

3.7
(
3
) (or (
2
)).
The reverse direction is established as follows. Starting with a (vertical) arrow
f:e\rightsquigarrow_{z}\omega^{\prime}(b)
, we consider the dependent pullback, with a pasted identity of arrows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(!_{b})_{!}e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\zeta_{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\omega^{\prime}(b)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{e}^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Then, the arrow
\psi_{b}(\varphi_{b}(e))
is given by the composite

(!_{b})_{!}e{\mathrel{\to}^{~~}}e\xlongrightarrow{f}\omega^{\prime}(b)

which can be identified with
f
. In sum, we have proven
\Psi\circ\Phi=\id
. We are left with the other direction.

Let
C
be a lex Rezk type and
F:B\to C
a lex functor. Then
\Psi(f)=\mathrm{gl}(F):C\mathbin{\downarrow}F\twoheadrightarrow B
. The section chosing the terminal elements is given by

\zeta\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda b.\langle b,F\,b,\id_{F\,b}\rangle:\prod_{b:B}\sum_{c:C}c\to F\,b.

Since
F
is lex we have an identification
F(z)=y
where
y:C
is terminal. Then the terminal transport functor of the fibration
\mathrm{gl}(F)
yields

\lx@nobreakspace\omega^{\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle F\,b,F(!_{z}):F\,b\to y\rangle:B\to\sum_{c:C}\lx@nobreakspace c\to y.

We have
\Phi(\Psi(F))=\omega^{\prime}_{\St_{B}(\mathrm{gl}(F))}
, and since
y
is terminal, we can identify this map with
F
.
∎

Chapter 8
Two-sided cartesian families of synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categories

Two-sided cartesian families are type families
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
which fibrationally are presented by
spans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

where
\xi
is cocartesian,
\pi
is cartesian, and some compatibility conditions between the two respective liftings are satisfied. An instructive example is given by the “hom span”
\partial_{1}:A\leftarrow A^{\Delta^{1}}\to A:\partial_{0}
of a Rezk type
A
, and from ensuing properties one also obtains comma spans
g:C\leftarrow A\to B:f
.
1
1

1

In fact, these are even
discrete
two-sided fibrations, cf.
[
RS17
, Section 8.6]
,
[
RV22
, Section 7.2]
,
[
LR20
, Theorem 2.3.3]
.
Semantically, two-sided families correspond to
categorical
(\infty,1)
-distributors
,
i.e.
bifunctors
A^{\Op}\times B\to{\mathrm{Cat}}
into the
(\infty,1)
-category of small
(\infty,1)
-categories.
2
2

2

Even though this cannot be expressed in our theory yet, cf.
Section

2
.
The significance for
\infty
-cosmos theory is that the discrete variant,
i.e.
the
(\infty,1)
-distributors or
modules
, form a
virtual equipment

[
CS10
]
, a rich double-categorical structure that presents the formal
\infty
-category theory of an
\infty
-cosmos. The Model Independence theorem stats that a biequivalence between
\infty
-cosmoses lifts to a biequivalence of the associated virtual equipments.
3
3

3

Note the parallel to axiomatic homotopy theory where a Quillen equivalence between “homotopy theories” presented through model categories lifts to an equivalence of their associated homotopy categories.
In this thesis, however we will deal with the categorical two-sided case. Namely, we will provide a structured analysis, leading up to characterizations and closure properties generalizing the one-sided case. This follows the thread of of
[
RV22
, Section 7.1]
, but with a more explicit accounts of various (auxiliary) notions of fibered (or
sliced
) fibrations, owed to the lack of categorical universes in the present theory. Our treatise nevertheless often times make use of techniques from “formal” category theory, by reasoning about the various conditions in terms of statements about (fibered) adjunctions, and their closure properties. We view this as a fruitful pratical effect of the
\infty
-cosmological philosophy on the synthetic theory formulated in simplicial type theory.

Our treatise ends with a two-sided Yoneda Lemma, and a (very brief) note on discrete two-sided families.

1
Sliced cocartesian families

1
Sliced cocartesian families

Definition 1.1

(Sliced cocartesian families)
.

Let
B
be a Rezk type. A
sliced cocartesian family over
B
is given by the following data:

•

an isoinner family
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
,

•

an isoinner family
K:\widetilde{P}\to\mathcal{U}
,

•

and, writing
Q\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Sigma_{P}K
, a witness for the proposition
4
4

4

cf.
1.4

\lx@nobreakspace\prod_{b:B}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:\Delta^{1}\to P\,b\\
x:Q(b,f0)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x^{\prime}:Q(b,f1)\\
k:x\to^{Q}_{b,f}x^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\mathrm{isCocartArr}}^{Q}_{f}(k).

We call
K
a
cocartesian family sliced over
B
with base
P
, and denote the ensuing cocartesian lifts as

K_{!}^{b}(f,x)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}K_{!}(b,f,x):x\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.28 0 L 0.55 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}

}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{K}_{\langle b,f\rangle}f_{!}^{K,b}\,x.

Perhaps more familiarly, in fibrational terms, a
cocartesian fibration sliced over
B
with base
\pi
is given by a fibered functor
\varphi:\xi\to_{B}\pi
, where
\xi:F\twoheadrightarrow B
and
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
are isoinner fibrations over
B
, visualized through

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

moreover satisfying the analogous lifting property: any
\pi
-vertical arrow has a
\varphi
-cocartesian lift.

As previously with ordinary cocartesian families, we will often bring in the fibrational viewpoint and reason diagrammatically.

This is a type-theoretic formulation of what, more generally in
\infty
-cosmos theory, defines for any
\infty
-cosmos
\mathcal{K}
and an object
B\in\mathcal{K}
a cocartesian family in the slice-
\infty
-cosmos
\mathcal{K}/B
. This is captured internally by the following theorem, which shows that the above condition precisely amounts to the sliced version of the familiar LARI condition for cocartesian families.

Theorem 1.2

(Characterization of sliced cocartesian families)
.

Given a Rezk type
B
, let
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
and
K:\widetilde{P}\to\mathcal{U}
be isoinner families. We write
Q\equiv\Sigma_{P}K:B\to\mathcal{U}
, and denote

\pi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Un_{B}(P):E\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{P}\twoheadrightarrow B,\quad\xi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Un_{B}(Q):F\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{Q}\twoheadrightarrow B,\quad\varphi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\Un_{E}(K):F\to E:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{Q}\simeq\widetilde{K}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Q}\equiv\pi_{\Sigma_{P}\,K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{P}}

Then the following are equivalent propositions:

1.

The family
K:E\to\mathcal{U}
is a cocartesian family sliced over
B
.

2.

The sliced Leibniz cotensor
i_{0}\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}_{B}\varphi:{\mathrm{Vert}}_{\xi}(F)\to_{B}\varphi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}E
has a fibered LARI:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{Vert}}_{\xi}(F)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi\downarrow_{B}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}\widehat{\pitchfork}_{B}\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

3.

The fibered inclusion map
\iota_{\varphi}:F\to_{E}\varphi\mathbin{\downarrow}_{B}E
has a fibered left adjoint:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi\downarrow_{B}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Proof.

\ref{it:sl-cocart-fam-char-ii}\implies\ref{it:sl-cocart-fam-char-i}
:

We abbreviate
r\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}i_{0}\widehat{\pitchfork}_{B}\varphi
. After the usual fibrant replacement, we can identify it as the fiberwise map with components

r_{b}\big(f:e\to_{P\,b}e^{\prime},k:x\to^{Q}_{f}x^{\prime}\big)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle e,f,x\rangle\equiv\langle\partial_{0}\,f,f,\partial_{0}k\rangle

for
b:B
.

Assume, the stated fibered LARI condition is satisfied.
First we note that the invertible unit, for every
b:B
, exhibits
\chi_{B,b}
as a (strict) section of
r_{b}
,
i.e.
given
e:P\,b
,
f:e\mathbin{\downarrow}P\,b
and
x:Q(b,e)
, we can assume
\chi_{B,b}(e,f,x):x\to^{Q}_{f}x^{\prime}
for some
x^{\prime}:Q(b,\partial_{1}\,f)
.
We have a fibered equivalence

\lx@nobreakspace\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}b,b^{\prime}:B\\
u:b\to_{B}b^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}e,e^{\prime}:P\,b\\
f:e\to_{P\,b}e^{\prime}\\
x:Q(b,e)\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}d,d^{\prime}:P(b^{\prime})\\
g:d\to_{P\,b^{\prime}}d^{\prime}\\
y:Q(b^{\prime},d),y^{\prime}:Q(b^{\prime},d^{\prime})\\
m:y\to^{Q}_{g}y^{\prime}\end{subarray}}(\chi_{B}(e,f,x)\to_{u}\langle g,m\rangle)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\langle e,f,x\rangle\to_{u}\langle d,g,y\rangle).

Just as in the second part of the proof of
Theorem

2.6
, by specializing to the case that
m=\id_{x^{\prime\prime}}
and
g=\id_{e^{\prime\prime}}
for some
e^{\prime\prime}:P(b^{\prime})
and
x^{\prime\prime}:Q(b^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime})
, we find that the lift
\chi_{B,b}(e,f,x)
is a
K
-cocartesian lift of the
P
-vertical
arrow
f:e\to_{P\,b}e^{\prime}
.

\ref{it:sl-cocart-fam-char-i}\implies\ref{it:sl-cocart-fam-char-ii}
:

On the other hand, suppose that
K
-cocartesian lifts of all

-cocartesian maps exist, w.r.t. to a given initial vertex. Accordingly, we define
\chi_{B,b}(e,f,x)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}K^{b}_{!}(f,x):x\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.28 0 L 0.55 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}

{{}}
}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{f}^{K}x^{\prime}
. Again, analogously to the first part of the proof, we define a pair of maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left(\chi_{B,b}(e,f,x)\to_{u}\langle g,m\rangle\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left(\langle e,f,x\rangle\to_{u}\langle d,g,y\rangle\right)}}
