{{}}
}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{P}_{\langle u,b\rangle}u_{*}\,e
in
P
as desired.
∎

2
Pullback and reindexing

Proposition 3.6

(Pullback stability of two-sided cartesian families, cf.
[
RV22
, Proposition 7.2.4]
)
.

Let
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
be a two-sided cartesian family over Rezk types
A
and
B
. Then for any pair of maps
k:C\to A
,
m:D\to B
, the pullback family

\lx@nobreakspace(k\times m)^{*}P:C\to D\to\mathcal{U}

is two-sided as well.
Diagrammatically, if the two-sided fibration
\varphi:E\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
denotes the unstraightening of
P
, this means that the map
\psi
in the following diagram is a two-sided fibration:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(k\times m)^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\times D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(k\times m)^{*}\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\times m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Furthermore, we claim that this square is a two-sided cartesian functor.

Recalling the notation from
Section

1
, we write

\displaystyle P_{m}:A\to\mathcal{U},

\displaystyle P_{m}(a)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{b^{\prime}:B^{\prime}}P_{m\,b^{\prime}}(a)\equiv\sum_{b^{\prime}:B^{\prime}}P(a,m\,b^{\prime}),

\displaystyle P^{k}:B\to\mathcal{U},

\displaystyle P^{k}(b)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\sum_{a^{\prime}:A^{\prime}}P^{k\,a^{\prime}}(b)\equiv\sum_{a^{\prime}:A^{\prime}}P(k\,a^{\prime},b),

for the families induced from reindexing on just one side, and then projection down,
i.e.
:
P_{m}
and
P^{k}
arise fibrationally as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{m^{*}\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k^{*}\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\times\id_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Un_{B}(P^{k})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Un_{A}(P_{m})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{A}\times m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

In particular, for the case of
k=\id_{A}:A\to A
and
m=\id_{B}:B\to B
we have
P^{A}=P^{\id_{A}}
and
P_{B}=P_{\id_{B}}
, cf.
Section

1
.

Proof.

This follows by employing the characterization
Theorem

2.8
,
Item

4
, and then the closure property
Proposition

1.11
. In particular, letting either of the maps
k,m
be an identity, we can conclude that
P^{k}
is cocartesian, and
P_{m}
is cartesian.

That the square is a two-sided cartesian functor follows by separately projecting to the factors in the base, and then using
[
BW21
, Proposition 5.3.9]
or its dual. Namely, e.g. since
P_{m}:A\to\mathcal{U}
is cocartesian, so is its pullback along
k:A^{\prime}\to A
which arises as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(k\times m)^{*}\widetilde{P}\simeq k^{*}\widetilde{P_{m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{P_{m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

and the pullback square is known to be a cocartesian functor.
∎

Proposition 3.7

(Pullback stability of two-sided cartesian functors)
.

In the following, let all types be Rezk. Consider two-sided cartesian families
P,Q:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
with unstraightenings
E\twoheadrightarrow\to A\times B
of
P
, and
F\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
of
Q
, resp. Let
\kappa:P\to_{A\times B}Q
be a two-sided cartesian functor. Given maps
k:A^{\prime}\to A
,
m:B^{\prime}\to B
, then the functor
\kappa^{\prime}:P^{\prime}\to_{A^{\prime}\times B^{\prime}}Q^{\prime}
induced by pullback along
k\times m
is two-sided cartesian as well:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}\times B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\times m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Proof.

Let
u^{\prime}:a_{0}^{\prime}\to_{A^{\prime}}a_{1}^{\prime}
,
b^{\prime}:B^{\prime}
,
e:P^{\prime}(a_{0}^{\prime},b^{\prime})\simeq P(k\,a_{0}^{\prime},m\,b^{\prime})
. Straightforward calculation gives

\displaystyle\kappa^{\prime}_{u^{\prime},b^{\prime}}(P_{!}^{\prime}(u^{\prime},b^{\prime},e))=\kappa_{k\,u^{\prime},m\,^{\prime}}(P_{!}(k\,u^{\prime},m\,b^{\prime},e))

\displaystyle=Q_{!}(k\,u^{\prime},m\,b^{\prime},\kappa_{k\,u^{\prime},m\,b^{\prime}}(e))=Q_{!}^{\prime}(u^{\prime},b^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}_{u^{\prime},b^{\prime}}(e)),

where the second identity is given by
\kappa
being cocartesian. The dual case for cartesian lifts works similarly (cf. also
[
BW21
, Proposition 5.3.18]
).
∎

3
Dependent and sliced product

Proposition 3.8

(Product stability of two-sided cartesian families)
.

Let
A,B:I\to\mathcal{U}
be families of Rezk types for a small type
I
. Consider a two-sided family
P:\prod_{i:I}A_{i}\to B_{i}\to\mathcal{U}
. Then the induced product family

\prod_{i:I}P_{i}:\prod_{i:I}A_{i}\to\prod_{i:I}B_{i}\to\mathcal{U}

is two-sided cartesian as well.

Moreover, denoting the unstraightenings of the
P_{i}
by
\varphi_{i}:E_{i}\twoheadrightarrow A_{i}\times B_{i}
, the squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}A_{i}\times\prod_{i:I}B_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{k}\times B_{k}}}

are two-sided cartesian functors. Furthermore, these product cones are terminal w.r.t. two-sided cartesian functors.
14
14

14

Here, and in the following we will not formally spell out the universal properties, but they are analogous to the respective propositions in
[
BW21
, Subsection 5.3.3]
. The addition/generalization is that the base types are binary products, and the fibrations and functors are
two-sided
cartesian.

Fibrationally, the proposition says that given a family of two-sided fibrations
\varphi_{i}:E_{i}\twoheadrightarrow A_{i}\times B_{i}
for
i:I
, the product fibration
\prod_{i:I}\varphi_{i}:\prod_{i:I}\lx@nobreakspace E_{i}\twoheadrightarrow\prod_{i:I}\lx@nobreakspace A_{i}\times\prod_{i:I}\lx@nobreakspace B_{i}
is also two-sided cartesian.

Proof.

This is a consequence of the characterization
Theorem

2.8
,
Item

4
, in combination with the closure property
Proposition

1.9
. Two-sided cartesianness of the projection squares follows upon postcomposition with the respective projection, and then employing either
[
BW21
, Proposition 5.3.7]
or its dual. Similarly, one argues for the universal property for two-sided cartesian functors, using
[
BW21
, Proposition 5.3.8]
or its dual, resp.
∎

Corollary 3.9

(Sliced product stability of two-sided cartesian families)
.

Let
A,B
be small Rezk types. Consider a two-sided family
P:\prod_{i:I}A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
. Then the induced fiberweise product family

\times_{i:I}^{A\times B}P_{i}:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}

is two-sided cartesian as well.

Moreover, for every
k:I
there is an induced canonical commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\times_{i:I}^{A\times B}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times_{i:I}^{A\times B}\varphi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{k}}

which is a two-sided functor. The two-sided fibration
\prod_{i:I}E_{i}\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
is the terminal cone over the
\varphi_{k}:E_{k}\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
w.r.t. (triangle-shaped) cones into the
\varphi_{k}
whose horizontal map is two-sided cartesian.

Proof.

Recall that we have equivalences

\displaystyle\prod_{i}E_{i}

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha:I\to A\\
\beta:I\to B\end{subarray}}\prod_{i:I}P_{i}(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})\twoheadrightarrow A^{I}\times B^{I},

\displaystyle\times_{i:I}^{A\times B}E_{i}

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a:A\\
b:B\end{subarray}}\prod_{i:I}P_{i}(a,b)\twoheadrightarrow A\times B.

Denote by
E_{i}\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
the unstraightening of the family
P_{i}
. By
Proposition

3.6
, the induced map
\prod_{i:I}E_{i}\twoheadrightarrow(A\times B)^{I}
is two-sided cartesian:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\times_{i:I}^{A\times B}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(A\times B)^{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cst}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Invoking pullback-stability, and then considering the straightening of this map to recover a type family establishes the claim.

Now, by the above description via fibrant replacement, we have evaluation maps yielding the desired cones
\mathrm{ev}_{k}:{\times_{i:I}^{A\times B}E_{i}}\to_{A\times B}E_{k}
for
k:I
. But by the universal property of the standard dependent product
Proposition

3.8
(cf.
[
BW21
, Proposition 5.3.8]
), these factor as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\times_{i:I}^{A\times B}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{i:I}E_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{I}\times B^{I}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{cst}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

where the upper horizontal induced functor is two-sided cartesian, as are the evaluations from the standard dependent product. Hence, so is their composite, as desired, by
Proposition

3.2
.
∎

Proposition 3.10

(Pullback cones are two-sided cartesian functors)
.

Consider two-sided families over Rezk types

\lx@nobreakspace P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U},\quad\lx@nobreakspace P^{\prime}:A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}\to\mathcal{U},\quad\lx@nobreakspace P:A^{\prime\prime}\to B^{\prime\prime}\to\mathcal{U}.

Furthermore, assume there are maps

\alpha:A^{\prime}\to A,\quad\alpha^{\prime\prime}:A^{\prime\prime}\to A,\quad\beta^{\prime}:B^{\prime}\to B,\quad\beta^{\prime\prime}:B^{\prime\prime}\to B

and two-sided cartesian functors

\kappa^{\prime}:P^{\prime}\to_{A^{\prime}\times B^{\prime},A\times B}P,\quad\kappa^{\prime\prime}:P^{\prime\prime}\to_{A^{\prime\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime},A\times B}P.

Denote by

\varphi:E\twoheadrightarrow A\times B,\quad\varphi^{\prime}:E^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime}\times B^{\prime},\quad\varphi^{\prime\prime}:E^{\prime\prime}\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime}

the unstraightenings of
P
,
P^{\prime}
, and
P^{\prime\prime}
, resp. Consider the induced pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}\times_{E}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(A^{\prime}\times B^{\prime})\times_{A\times B}(A^{\prime\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}\times B^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}\times_{\varphi}\varphi^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime\prime}\times\beta^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}\times\beta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\prime}}

Then the mediating map

\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\varphi^{\prime}\times_{\varphi}\varphi^{\prime\prime}:E^{\prime\prime\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}{E^{\prime}\times_{E}E^{\prime\prime}}\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime\prime\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime\prime}

where

A^{\prime\prime\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}A^{\prime}\times_{A}A^{\prime\prime},\quad B^{\prime\prime\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}B^{\prime}\times_{B}B^{\prime\prime}

is a two-sided cartesian fibration.
15
15

15

Note in particular that we have an equivalence
A^{\prime\prime\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime\prime}\simeq(A^{\prime}\times B^{\prime})\times_{A\times B}(A^{\prime\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime})

Moreover, each of the projection squares from
\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}
is a two-sided cartesian functor, and
\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}:E^{\prime\prime\prime}\to A^{\prime\prime\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime\prime}
satisfies the expected terminal universal property for cones which are two-sided cartesian functors (analogous to
[
BW21
, Propositions 5.3.10,11]
).

Proof.

We use fibrant replacement so that we can take the fibers of
\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}
to be
16
16

16

Where
a:A
,
a^{\prime}:A^{\prime}
lies strictly over
a
via
k^{\prime}

etc
.

\lx@nobreakspace P^{\prime\prime\prime}(a,a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime},b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime})\equiv P^{\prime}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\times_{P(a,b)}P^{\prime\prime}(a^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime}).

We claim that the cocartesian lifts in
P^{\prime\prime\prime}
are then given by

\lx@nobreakspace P_{!}^{\prime\prime\prime}(u,u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime},b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime},\langle e,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime}\rangle)\equiv\langle P_{!}(u,b,e),P_{!}^{\prime}(u^{\prime},b^{\prime},e^{\prime}),P_{!}^{\prime\prime}(u^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime},e^{\prime\prime})\rangle,

which can be checked to be cocartesian since the conditions are validated fiberwise. In particular, the cocartesian lifts in
P^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime\prime}
indeed lie over the ones in
P
by two-sided cartesian-ness of
\kappa^{\prime}
and
\kappa^{\prime\prime}
. So far, this is analogous to
[
BW21
, Proposition 5.3.10]
, but we have the additional triple of points
\langle b,b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime}\rangle
as data.

The argument for the cartesian lifts works dually.
Now, the compatibility condition from
Proposition

2.7
have to be checked. But by the fibrant replacement above, the ensuing proposition is just witnessing that the condition is satisfied component-wise for triples
\langle\sigma,\sigma^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime\prime}\rangle
where
\sigma
is a square of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}

and
\sigma^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime\prime}
are of the same shape, lying above. Since the compatbility condition is satisfied for each of those, we are done. This shows that
\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}
is a two-sided cartesian fibration, as desired.

From the discussion of the lifts, it is also clear that both the projection squares are two-sided cartesian functors, since we just project to the respective coordinates. Furthermore, the universal property is established, again, by postcomposing separately with the projections to either
A^{\prime\prime\prime}
or
B^{\prime\prime\prime}
, then applying either
[
BW21
, Proposition 5.3.11]
for the one-sided cocartesian case, or its dual for the cartesian case.
∎

Corollary 3.11

(Pullback cones in a slice are two-sided cartesian functors)
.

Consider two-sided families over Rezk types
P,P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime}:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
with unstraightenings
\varphi:E\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
,
\varphi^{\prime}:E^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime}\times B^{\prime}
, and
\varphi^{\prime\prime}:E^{\prime\prime}\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime}
. Given two-sided cartesian functors
\kappa^{\prime}:P^{\prime}\to_{A\times B}P
and
\kappa^{\prime\prime}:P^{\prime\prime}\to P
,
consider the induced pullback over
A\times B
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}\times_{E}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\prime}}

Then the mediating map

\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\varphi^{\prime}\times_{\varphi}\varphi^{\prime\prime}:E^{\prime\prime\prime}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}{E^{\prime}\times_{E}E^{\prime\prime}}\twoheadrightarrow A\times B

is a two-sided cartesian fibration.

Moreover, each of the projection squares from
\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}
is a two-sided cartesian functor, and
\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}:E^{\prime\prime\prime}\to A\times B
satisfies the expected terminal universal property for cones which are two-sided cartesian functors over
A\times B
.

4
Sequential limit

Proposition 3.12

(Sequential limit cones are cocartesian functors)
.

Consider an inverse diagram of two-sided cartesian fibrations as below where all of the connecting squares are two-sided cartesian functors:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{\infty}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{\infty}\times B_{\infty}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{2}\times B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{1}\times B_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{0}\times B_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\alpha_{3},\beta_{3}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}

Then the induced map
\pi_{\infty}:E_{\infty}\to B_{\infty}
between the limit types is a two-sided cartesian fibration, and the projection squares constitute two-sided cartesian functors.

Furthermore,
\pi_{\infty}:E_{\infty}\to B_{\infty}
together with the projection squares satisfies the universal property of a sequential limit w.r.t. to cones of two-sided cartesian functors.

Proof.

Using the closure properties from this chapter, we can argue along the lines of
[
BW21
, Proposition 5.3.12]
.
17
17

17

I thank Ulrik Buchholtz for initially suggesting this proof in
[
BW21
]
because it circumvents dealing with unwieldy coherence data that would occur in different presentations of the sequential limit.
This means, the limit fibration, again is constructed via the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{\infty}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}E_{2n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}E_{2n+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}E_{2n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{\infty}\times B_{\infty}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathllap{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{2n}}\times\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}B_{2n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{2n+1}\times\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}B_{2n+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathllap{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}A_{n}}\times\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}B_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

and is two-sided cartesian due to
Proposition

3.10
. From
[
BW21
, Proposition 5.3.12]
and its dual we get that the projection squares are two-sided cartesian functors. The universal property is established using
[
BW21
, Proposition 5.3.13]
and its dual.
∎

Corollary 3.13

(Sequential limit cones in a slice are cocartesian functors)
.

Consider an inverse diagram of two-sided cartesian fibrations as below where all of the connecting squares are two-sided cartesian functors:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{\infty}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}

Then the induced map
\pi_{\infty}:E_{\infty}\to A\times B
between the limit types is a two-sided cartesian fibration, and the projection squares constitute two-sided cartesian functors.

Furthermore,
\pi_{\infty}:E_{\infty}\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
together with the projection squares satisfies the universal property of a sequential limit, relativized to the basis
A\times B
.

Proof.

The sequential limit of a diagram of identity maps is the object itself, e.g.

\seqlim_{n}\langle B,\id_{B}\rangle\simeq\sum_{\sigma:\mathbb{N}\to B}\prod_{n,k:\mathbb{N}}\sigma(n)=\sigma(n+k)\simeq B.

Thus, the claim follows from
3.12
.
∎

5
Cotensors

Proposition 3.14

(Cocartesian fibrations are cotensored over maps/shape inclusions)
.

Let
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
be a two-sided cartesian family with associated projection
\varphi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle\xi,\pi\rangle:E\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
. For any type map or shape inclusion
j:Y\to X
, the maps
\varphi^{X}
and
\varphi^{Y}
are two-sided cartesian fibrations, and moreover the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{X}\times B^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{Y}\times B^{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{Y}}

is a two-sided cartesian functor.

Proof.

By closedness under products, the maps
\varphi^{X}
,
\varphi^{Y}
are two-sided cartesian fibrations. From
[
BW21
, Proposition 5.3.15]
, we know that the square formed by the composites

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{X}\times B^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{Y}\times B^{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{Y}}

is a cocartesian functor (since by precondition
\xi:E\twoheadrightarrow A
is a cocartesian fibration). The cartesian case over
B
works the same.
∎

Corollary 3.15

(Cocartesian fibrations in a slice are cotensored over maps/shape inclusions)
.

Let
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
be a two-sided cartesian family with associated projection
\varphi\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\langle\xi,\pi\rangle:E\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
. For any type map or shape inclusion
j:Y\to X
, the maps
X\boxtimes\varphi
and
Y\boxtimes\varphi
are two-sided cartesian fibrations, and moreover the triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\boxtimes E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\boxtimes E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\boxtimes\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\boxtimes\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\boxtimes\varphi}

is a two-sided cartesian functor.

Proposition 3.16

(Cocartesian functors are closed under Leibniz cotensors)
.

Let
j:Y\to X
be a type map or shape inclusion. Then, given two-sided cartesian fibrations
\psi:F\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
,
\varphi:E\twoheadrightarrow C\times D
, and a cocartesian functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\times D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle k,m\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

the square induced between the Leibniz cotensors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{Y}\times_{E^{Y}}E^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(A\times B)^{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(A\times B)^{X}\times_{(A\times B)^{Y}}(C\times D)^{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}\langle k,m\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{X}}

is a cocartesian functor.

Proof.

This works, again, analogously to
[
BW21
, Proposition 5.3.16]
, using
3.14
, and then
Proposition

3.10
.
∎

Corollary 3.17

(Cocartesian functors in a slice are closed under Leibniz cotensors)
.

Let
j:Y\to X
be a type map or shape inclusion. Then, given two-sided cartesian fibrations
\psi:F\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
,
\varphi:E\twoheadrightarrow A\times B
, and a two-sided cartesian functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

the square induced between the Leibniz cotensors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\boxtimes F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y\boxtimes F\times_{Y\boxtimes E}X\boxtimes F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\mathbin{\widehat{\pitchfork}}_{A\times B}\kappa}

is a two-sided functor.

In sum, we obtain a synthetic analogue of the cosmological closure properties of two-sided cartesian fibrations, w.r.t. varying as well as a fixed base:

Theorem 3.18

((Sliced) cosmological closure properties of two-sided cartesian families)
.

Over Rezk bases, it holds that:

Two-sided cartesian families are closed under composition, dependent products, pullback along arbitrary maps, and cotensoring with maps/shape inclusions. Families corresponding to equivalences or terminal projections are always cocartesian.

Between two-sided cartesian families over Rezk bases, it holds that:
Two-sided cartesian functors are closed under (both horizontal and vertical) composition, dependent products, pullback, sequential limits,
18
18

18

all three objectwise limit notions satisfying the expected universal properties w.r.t. to cocartesian functors
and Leibniz cotensors.

Fibered equivalences and fibered functors into the identity of
\mathbf{1}
are always cocartesian.

Furthermore, all of this is analogously true w.r.t. two-sided cartesian families over the same base and applying sliced versions of the constructions,

4
Two-sided Yoneda Lemma

1
Two-sided cartesian sections

Definition 4.1

(Two-sided cartesian sections)
.

Let
P:A\times B\to\mathcal{U}
be a two-sided family with associated cocartesian fibration
\xi:E\twoheadrightarrow A
and cartesian fibration
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
, resp.

A section
\sigma:\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a:A\\
b:B\end{subarray}}P(a,b)
is
two-sided cartesian
if it maps pairs
\langle u,\id_{b}\rangle
to
\xi
-cocartesian sections and
\langle\id_{a},v\rangle
to
\pi
-cartesian sections,
i.e.
: for all arrows
u:\Delta^{1}\to A
,
v:\Delta^{1}\to B
and elements
a:A
,
b:B
the dependent arrow
\sigma(u,\id_{b}):\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}P(u(t),b)
is
\xi
-cocartesian while
\sigma(\id_{a},v):\prod_{t:\Delta^{1}}P(a,v(t))
is
\pi
-cartesian.

Note that this yields a proposition, and the (sub-)type of such sections is denoted by

\lx@nobreakspace\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a:A\\
b:B\end{subarray}}^{\mathrm{2sCart}}P(a,b)\rightarrowtail\lx@nobreakspace\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a:A\\
b:B\end{subarray}}P(a,b).

Of central importance will be the following map. We fix a
P:A\times B\to\mathcal{U}
a two-sided family, and assume
a:A
to be initial and
b:B
to be terminal. We then define

\lx@nobreakspace\mathbf{y}:P(a,b)\to\prod_{A\times B}P,\quad\mathbf{y}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda d,x,y.(\emptyset_{x})_{!}((!_{y})^{*}d).

Note that by two-sidedness of
P
we have a path

\mathbf{y}(d)(x,y)=(\emptyset_{x})_{!}((!_{y})^{*}b)=(!_{y})^{*}((\emptyset_{x})_{!}d).

In the following, we first show that
\mathbf{y}
is, in fact, valued in two-sided cartesian sections. We then conclude that it is a quasi-inverse of the evaluation map, constituting a quasi-equivalence:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{A\times B}^{\mathrm{2sCart}}P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(a,b)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{\langle a,b\rangle}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Finally, the Yoneda Lemmas will follow as instances from this.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
P:A\times B\to\mathcal{U}
be a two-sided family. Assume
a:A
is initial and
b:B
is terminal. Then for all
d:P(a,b)
, the section
\mathbf{y}\,d:\prod_{A\times B}\lx@nobreakspace P
is two-sided cartesian.

Proof.

This is an extension of
[
BW21
, Proposition 7.1.3]
to the two-sided case. We write
E\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\widetilde{P}
and fix an element
d:P(a,b)
. Let
\xi:E\twoheadrightarrow A
denote the associated cocartesian fibration. Again, we will only establish one of the two dual parts of the statement, namely that
\mathbf{y}d(u,\id_{y})
is
\xi
-cocartesian for any
u:\Delta^{1}\to A
and
y:B

From this, we define the map that yields the cartesian transport along the terminal maps in
B
,
i.e.

\lx@nobreakspace\tau\equiv\lambda x,y.(!_{y})^{*}d:A\times B\to E.

Next, consider the family of cocartesian lifts over the initial maps in
A
, starting at the points given by
\tau
. This is realized by the
2

defined by

The action of the
2
-cell
\chi
on a pair
\langle u,\id_{y}\rangle
for
u:\Delta^{1}\to A
and
y:B
is given by the following dependent square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau d(x,y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau d(x^{\prime},y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{y}d(x,y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{y}d(x^{\prime},y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\prime}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{(!_{y})^{*}d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{y}d(u,\id_{y})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset_{x^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{y}}

By right cancelation of cocartesian arrows,
\mathbf{y}d(u,\id_{y})
is cocartesian, too.
∎

We need one more lemma before we are ready to prove the main theorem of this subsection, which in turn will specialize to the desired versions of the Yoneda Lemma. The lemma gives canonical identities in the presence of inital and terminal elements, resp., in the base types.

Lemma 4.3

(Coherence of terminal transport with two-sided cartesian sections)
.

Let
A
and
B
types with an initial element
a:A
and terminal element
b:B
. Furthermore, consider a two-sided family
P:A\times B\to\mathcal{U}
with associated cocartesian fibration
\xi:E\twoheadrightarrow A
and cartesian fibration
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
, resp. Given a section
\sigma:\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a:A\\
b:B\end{subarray}}^{\mathrm{2sCart}}P
, for any
x:A
,
y:B
there are identifications

(!_{y})^{*}\sigma(a,b)=\sigma(a,y)\quad\text{and}\quad(\emptyset_{x})_{!}\sigma(a,b)=\sigma(x,b).

Proof.

We only treat the first named case since the second named one is completely dual.

Consider on the one hand the cartesian lift of the terminal map
!_{y}:y\to b
w.r.t.
\sigma(a,b)
(over the identity
\id_{a}:a=a
),
i.e.
the dependent arrow
f\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}(!_{y})^{*}\sigma(a,b)\hbox to0pt{\vbox to5.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-2.58333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

.
∎

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sigma(a,y)}}
