Figure 12:
Coherence of terminal transport with two-sided cartesian sections

In analogy with
[
BW21
, Proposition 7.1.4]
,
[
RV22
, Theorem 5.7.18]
,
[
RS17
, Theorem 9.7]
, the map
\mathbf{y}:P(a,b)\to\prod_{A\times B}^{\mathrm{2sCart}}P
mediates an equivalence between these two types:

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
A,B
be Rezk types with an initial element
a:A
and a terminal element
b:B
. For a two-sided family
P:A\times B\to\mathcal{U}
, evaluation at
\langle a,b\rangle
given by

\mathrm{ev}_{\langle a,b\rangle}:\Big(\prod_{A\times B}^{\mathrm{2sCart}}P\Big)\to P(a,b)

is an equivalence.

Proof.

We show that for the map
\mathbf{y}
as defined above we have identifications
\mathrm{ev}_{\langle a,b\rangle}\circ\mathbf{y}=\id_{P(a,b)}
and
\mathbf{y}\circ\mathrm{ev}_{\langle a,b\rangle}=\id_{\prod_{A\times B}P}
. The first case is easy: the initial map into the initial element
a
is just the identity, whose cocartesian lift is an identity as well, and the same holds analogously for the terminal element
b
,
i.e.

\mathbf{y}(d)(\sigma)(a,b)=(\emptyset_{a})_{!}(!_{b})^{*}(d)=(\id_{a})_{!}(\id_{b})^{*}(d)=d.

For the other round-trip, we have to give an identification

(\mathbf{y}\circ\mathrm{ev}_{\langle a,b\rangle})(\sigma)(x,y)=\sigma(x,y)\lx@nobreakspace

Fix elements
x:A
and
y:B
.
Note that by
Lemma

4.3
there is a path

\lx@nobreakspace q:\sigma(a,y)=_{P(a,y)}(!_{y})^{*}(\sigma(a,b)).

Since
\sigma
is two-sided cartesian, we obtain the following dependent square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sigma(a,y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sigma(x,y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(!_{y})^{*}\sigma(a,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{y}d(\sigma(a,b))(x,y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma(\emptyset_{x},\id_{y})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{!}(\emptyset_{x},(!_{y})^{*}\sigma(a,b))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Since the filler
g
is cocartesian by right cancelation, and vertical at the same time it is an isomorphism, hence an identity
\sigma(x,y)=\mathbf{y}d(\sigma(a,b))(x,y)
.
∎

2
Dependent and absolute two-sided Yoneda Lemma

Following
[
BW21
, Theorem 7.2.3]
, we obtain the
dependent Yoneda Lemma for two-sided families
by
Proposition

4.4
, and again this will in turn imply the absolute version.

Theorem 4.5

(Dependent Yoneda Lemma for two-sided families)
.

Let
Q:a\mathbin{\downarrow}A\times B\mathbin{\downarrow}b\to\mathcal{U}
be a two-sided family over Rezk types
A
and
B
. For any
a:A
and
b:
, the evaluation map

\mathrm{ev}_{\id_{\langle a,b\rangle}}:\Big(\prod_{a\mathbin{\downarrow}A\times B\mathbin{\downarrow}b}^{\mathrm{2sCart}}Q\Big)\rightarrow Q(\id_{a},\id_{b})

is an equivalence.

Proof.

Recall that by
[
RS17
, Lemma 8.9]
, the identity map
\id_{a}
is an initial object of the comma type
a\mathbin{\downarrow}A
, while analogously the identity map
\id_{b}
is a terminal object of the cocomma type
B\mathbin{\downarrow}b
. Thus, the claim follows as an instance of
Proposition

4.4
.
∎

Theorem 4.6

(Absolute Yoneda Lemma for two-sided families,
[
RV22
, Theorem 7.3.2]
)
.

Let
P:A\times B\to\mathcal{U}
be a two-sided family over Rezk types
A
and
B
. For any
a:A
and
b:B
, the evaluation map

\mathrm{ev}_{\langle\id_{a},\id_{b}\rangle}:\Big(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}u:a\mathbin{\downarrow}A\\
v:B\mathbin{\downarrow}b\end{subarray}}^{\mathrm{2sCart}}P(\partial_{1}\,u,\partial_{0}\,v)\Big)\rightarrow P(a,b)

is an equivalence.

Proof.

The claim follows by setting
Q\equiv\langle\partial_{1},\partial_{0}\rangle^{*}P:a\mathbin{\downarrow}A\times B\mathbin{\downarrow}b\to\mathcal{U}
in
Theorem

4.5
. In particular,
Q
is a two-sided fibration again by pullback stability. ∎

5
Discrete two-sided families

1
Definition and characterization

Definition 5.1

(Two-sided discrete families,
[
RS17
, Definition 8.28]
)
.

Let
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
be a two-variable family over Rezk types
A
and
B
. Then
P
is a
two-sided discrete family
if for all
a:A
,
b:B
the family
P_{b}:A\to\mathcal{U}
is covariant and
P^{a}:B\to\mathcal{U}
is contravariant.

Proposition 5.2

(Two-sided discrete families as discrete objects, cf.
[
RV22
, Prop. 7.2.4]
)
.

Given
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
over Rezk types, the following are equivalent:

1.

The family
P
is two-sided discrete.

2.

The family
P
is cocartesian on the left and cartesian on the right. Additionally, every bifiber
P(a,b)
is discrete, for
a:A
,
b:B
.

Proof.

\ref{it:2s-disc-ii}\implies\ref{it:2s-disc-i}
:

By
Proposition

2.3
,
Item

3
, there is a fibered adjunction which pulls back as follows by
[
BW21
, Proposition B.2.3]
:
19
19

19

In contrast to the current version of
[
BW21
, Proposition B.2.3]
one only needs the fibrations involved to be isoinner, and not cocartesian, which in any case becomes clear from the given proof.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\xi_{b}\downarrow A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi\mathbin{\downarrow}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times\mathbf{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\times B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{A}\times b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

This means exactly that
P^{a}
is covariant. The analogous reasoning establishes the claim for
P_{b}
being covariant since
P
is cartesian on the right, for any
b:B
.
Now, since any
P(a,b)
is discrete, and the fibers of
P_{b}
are given by
P(a,b)
for any
a:A
, we obtain that
P^{a}:B\to\mathcal{U}
is a cocartesian family with discrete fibers, which is equivalent to
P^{a}
being covariant by
[
BW21
, Corollary 6.1.4]
.

\ref{it:2s-disc-i}\implies\ref{it:2s-disc-ii}
:

The fibrations
P_{a}
and
P^{b}
, resp., being contra- and covariant, resp., imply that all bifibers
P(a,b)
are discrete, for all
a:A
,
b:B
.

Furthermore, for any
b:B
, the family
P^{b}:A\to\mathcal{U}
being cocartesian means that
P^{B}:A\to\mathcal{U}
is cocartesian, and in addition all
P^{B}
-cocartesian lifts are
P_{A}
-vertical,
i.e.
lie over an identity in
B
.
20
20

20

This could have also been used as a more direct argument to prove “
\ref{it:2s-disc-ii}\implies\ref{it:2s-disc-i}
” as well.

∎

Corollary 5.3

(Co-/cart. arrows and two-sided cart. functors, cf.
[
RV22
, Lemma 7.4.3]
)
.

In a two-sided discrete family
P:A\to B\to\mathcal{U}
an arrow is
P_{B}
-cocartesian if and only if it is
P^{A}
-vertical. Similarly, an arrow is
P^{A}
-cartesian if and only if it is
P_{B}
-vertical.
In particular, any two-sided discrete cartesian family is two-sided cartesian, and any fibered functor between two-sided discrete families is two-sided cartesian.

Proof.

The first statement follows from the inspection in the proof of
Proposition

5.2
, together with
[
BW21
, Proposition 6.1.5]
.

This also establishes that any two-sided discrete cartesian family is, in fact, two-sided cartesian: the commutation condition
Proposition

2.7
is readily verified, because for dependent arrows being vertical (in the respective appropriate sense) is already sufficient for being co-/cartesian, resp.

By naturality, this implies that any fibered functor between two-sided discrete families is two-sided cartesian.
∎

Chapter 9
On the semantics of simplicial homotopy type theory

1
Simplicial diagram models

In
[
Shu19
]
, Shulman provided the final missing part to establish the far-reaching conjecture that any Grothendieck
(\infty,1)
-topos gives rise to a model of homotopy type theory. Indeed, he proved that any such higher topos can be presented by a model structure in which provides enough univalent universes, validating propositional resizing, and which are
strictly
closed under all desired type formers.
1
1

1

with more general kinds of higher inductive types being work in progress
This had previously been established for certain classes of special cases.
[
Shu15a
,
Shu15
,
Shu17
,
Cis14
]
More generally, work connected to “internal languages” of higher categories is found in
[
AW09
,
GK17
,
KL18
,
KS19
,
Kap17
]
.

Given a type-theoretic model topos
\mathscr{E}
, the internal presheaf category
\mathrm{s}\mathscr{E}:=[{{\text{\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{\includegraphics[height]{simplex}}}}}^{\Op},\mathscr{E}]
of simplicial objects in
\mathscr{E}
is again a type-theoretic model topos.
2
2

2

with the injective model structure
[
Shu19
, Corollary 8.29]
These are the “standard models” for simplicial homotopy type theory. Inside any such
\mathrm{s}\mathscr{E}
we find a copy of the TTMT of spaces
\mathscr{S}
, which is embedded fibrantly and spatially-discretely via the constant diagram functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Set}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rightsquigarrow}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{s}\mathscr{E}\mathrlap{=\mathscr{E}^{{\text{\raisebox{-0.3014pt}{\includegraphics[height]{simplex}}}}^{\Op}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\mathscr{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{\mathscr{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\mathrm{s}\mathscr{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{\mathrm{s}\mathscr{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

i.e.
the map given by

\Delta_{s\mathscr{E}}:\mathscr{S}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{E},\quad X\mapsto\Big([n]\mapsto\coprod_{X_{n}}\mathbf{1}\Big).

We adapt the coherence construction used in
[
KL21
,
LW15
,
Awo18
,
Str14a
,
Str14
,
LS20
]
, based on
[
Voe09
,
Voe17
]
, so that in the model extension types à la
[
RS17
]
, going to back to earlier work by Lumsdaine and Shulman, can be chosen in a way that is strictly stable under pullback.

Our presentation is inspired by
[
KL21
,
Awo18
]
and also
[
LW15
]
in method and style. We define the type formers and the data required from the rules by performing the corresponding constructions in suitable
generic contexts
. Substitution then corresponds to precomposition with the reindexing map, and applying type formers corresponds to postcomposition with an ensuing map between universes. This implies strict substitution stability on the nose, in particular avoiding choices of pullbacks (and dealing with the coherences that would ensue).

This is done with respect to just a single universe as e.g. in
[
Awo18
,
Str14
,
Str14a
]
. Because extension types are similar to
\Pi
-types, the splitting is also analogous to the method for
\Pi
-types from the aforementioned sources.
3
3

3

Since the same ideas have successfully generalized from
global
to
local universes

[
LW15
,
Shu19
]
, we believe the same generalization would also work for strict extension types (which we do not consider in the present text, however). In particular, a formulation in (an appropriate extension of) Shulman’s setting
[
Shu19
, Appendix A]
should be possible.

We briefly recall Shulman’s setting and results that we are building on, although we will rarely need to be explicit about the machinery under the hood from here on.

A
type-theoretic model topos (TTMT)

\mathscr{E}
is given by a Grothendieck
1
-topos
\mathscr{E}
, together with the structure of a right proper simplicial Cisinski model category, which is in addition simplicially cartesian closed (
i.e.
reindexing preserves simplicial copowers) and is also equipped with an appropriate notion of ‘‘type-theoretic’’ fibration.
4
4

4

in Shulman’s terminology, a
locally presentable
and
relatively acyclic

notion of fibration structure
, satisfying in addition a “fullness” condition
An immediate example is given by the prime model of homotopy type theory, the type-theoretic model topos of spaces
\mathscr{S}
, but there are many more examples. In particular, the class of TTMTs is closed under typical operations, such as slicing and small products, as well as passage to diagram categories and internal localizations. Moreover, any Grothendieck–Rezk–Lurie-
(\infty,1)
-topos can be presented by a type-theoretic model topos. As a consequence, the study of Rezk types in simplicial HoTT can be understood as a synthetic version of internal
\infty
-category theory in an arbitrary given
(\infty,1)
-topos.

Crucially, any TTMT hosts enough
5
5

5

bounded from below by cardinal
strict fibrant univalent universes
[
Shu19
, Theorem 5.22, Theorem 11.2]
.
6
6

6

as usual, in a strong enough meta-theory such as ZFC with inaccessibles
Abstractly from the given fibration structure, the universes can be constructed using a version of the small object argument
[
Shu19
, Theorem 5.9]
. Then a splitting method due to Voevodsky
[
Voe09
,
KL21
]
akin to Giraud’s
left adjoint splitting

[
Gir71
,
LW15
,
Str14a
]
is applied to obtain
strict
type formers for these universes.

2
Strictly stable extension types

1
General ideas

We adapt the coherence construction presented in
[
KL21
,
LW15
,
Awo18
]
, based on
[
Voe09
,
Voe17
]
, so that in the model extension types à la
[
RS17
]
can be chosen in a way that is strictly stable under pullback.

Our presentation is close to
[
KL21
,
Awo18
]
in style. We define the type formers and the data required from the rules by performing the corresponding constructions in suitable
generic contexts
. Substitution then corresponds to precomposition with the reindexing map. Applying type formers/operations corresponds to postcomposition with an ensuing map between universes. This implies strict substitution stability on the nose.

We work w.r.t. just a single universe, as is also done in
[
Awo18
]
. The extension types of
[
RS17
, Figure 4]
are both syntactically and semantically similar to
\Pi
-types: Intuitively, the extension type
\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{\psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\psi}}A\middle|^{\varphi}_{a}\right\rangle
consists of sections
b:\prod_{\psi}A
that on the subshape
\varphi\rightarrowtail\psi
coincide with the given section
a
in the sense of
judgmental
equality. Hence, they can be seen as a kind of “
\Pi
-types with side conditions” (for functions whose domain is a tope,
a priori
a
pre
-type).

Since in the case of ordinary
\Pi
-types the splitting method has been generalized from
global
to
local universes

[
LW15
,
Shu19
]
, we believe the same should also work for strict extension types (which we do not consider in the present text, however). This has also been claimed in
[
RS17
, Appendix A.2]
.

In particular, a formulation in (an appropriate extension of) Shulman’s setting
[
Shu19
, Appendix A]
should then be possible.

In contrast to most of
[
RS17
, Appendix A]
we will work completely internally to the topos, disregarding the extra “non-fibrant” structure made explicit in a
comprehension category with shapes

[
RS17
, Definition A.5]
.

This is justified, because shapes are reflected into types by a newly added rule, cf.
Section

2
. In the model, this is validated because the simplicial shapes turn up in the model as (spatially-discrete) fibrant objects, cf.
Section

1
.

In sum, this allows us to focus the presentation on the splitting of the extension types, without having to deal with also splitting the extra layers, introducing universes for ‘‘cofibrations’’ or similar.
7
7

7

Even though all of this should be possible if desired, cf. also the remarks about strict stability in
[
RS17
, Appendix A.2]
.

We will work in a classical meta-theory with global choice and universes. Sometimes we use the internal extensional type theory (ETT) of the topos for an easy-to-parse denotation of the objects presenting generic contexts. This is along the lines of the presentation in
[
Awo18
]
and also
[
KL21
,
LW15
]
.

As in
[
RS17
, Theorem A.16]
, in the (injective) model structure of the ttmt presenting
\mathrm{s}\mathscr{E}
we interpret the extension types as ordinary
1
-categorical pullbacks of Leibniz cotensors. Since the model structure at hand is type-theoretic
[
Shu15a
, Definition 2.12]
the Leibniz cotensors are fibrations, so are all their pullbacks, which in particular includes the (dependent) extension types.
8
8

8

In the case of non-fibrant shapes, the Leibniz cotensor is still a fibration because the model structure is cartesian monoidal, cf.
[
RS17
, Section 1, Section A.2]
.

Specifically, in the non-dependent case the extension type
\left\langle\psi\to A\middle|^{\varphi}_{a}\right\rangle
is given simply by (ordinary,
1
-categorical) pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left\langle\psi\to A\middle|^{\varphi}_{a}\right\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\psi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\varphi}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

For the dependent case the construction will have to be relativized as we will see later on.

We want to show that the interpretation of the extension types can be chosen in a strictly pullback-stable way. In particular, after
[
RS17
, Definition A.10, Theorem A.16]
given a type family
A\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma\times\psi
and a partial section
a:\Gamma\times\varphi\to_{\Gamma\times\psi}A
substitution of the extension type
9
9

9

Here and in the following, notation such as
\Pi(A,B)
or
\langle\Pi[\varphi][A]|^{\varphi}_{a}\rangle
instead of
\prod_{A}B
,
\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{\psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\psi}}A\middle|^{\varphi}_{a}\right\rangle
designates the (strictly stable) type formers (to be) defined in the interpretation, in line with
[
RS17
,
KL21
,
KL21
,
Awo18
]
.

\langle\Pi[\varphi][A]|^{\varphi}_{a}\rangle
is only considered along (type) context morphisms
\sigma:\Delta\to\Gamma
, leaving the shape inclusion
j:\varphi\rightarrowtail\psi
fixed. Hence, we will understand the type former of the extension type as a
family
of type formers
{\mathrm{Ext@}j}
, given by

{\mathrm{Ext@}j}(A,a):=\langle\Pi[\psi][A]|^{\varphi}_{a}\rangle

indexed by the shape inclusions
j
.
10
10

10

Note that,
a posteriori
this also yields stability w.r.t. to reindexing along cube context morphisms
\tau:J\to I
pulling back shape inclusions
j:\varphi\to_{I}\psi
: By their defining universal property, which only involves a condition on reindexings of the type context
\Gamma
, the (pseudo-stable) extension types from
[
RS17
, Definition A.10]
are determined uniquely up to isomorphism. The splitting then yields uniqueness up to equality. Then, considering reindexings along shape maps does not lead out of this class, so the choice remains strictly stable.

Theorem 2.1

(Strict stability of extension types)
.

Let
{\mathscr{E}}
be a type-theoretic model topos, and consider the interpretation of simplicial homotopy type theory à la
[
RS17
,
Shu19
]
in the type-theoretic model topos
\mathrm{s}\mathscr{E}
. Then, a fixed splitting of the standard type formers induced by the universal fibration
\pi:\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}\to\mathcal{U}
for small fibrations yields an interpretation of the extension types
{\mathrm{Ext@}j}
(where
j:\varphi\to_{I}\psi
is a fixed shape inclusion relative to some cube
I
) that is strictly stable under pullback. This means, for families
A:\Gamma\times\psi\to\mathcal{U}
and partial sections
a:\Gamma\times\varphi\to_{\Gamma\times\psi}A
we have:

\displaystyle\sigma^{*}\big({\mathrm{Ext@}j}(A,a)\big)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{Ext@}j}(\sigma^{*}A,\sigma^{*}a)

\displaystyle\sigma^{*}\big({{\lambda\mathrm{@}j}}_{A,a}(b)\big)

\displaystyle={{\lambda\mathrm{@}j}}_{\sigma^{*}A,\sigma^{*}a}(\sigma^{*}b)

\displaystyle\sigma^{*}\big({\mathrm{app@}j}_{A,a}(f,s)\big)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{app@}j}_{\sigma^{*}A,\sigma^{*}a}(\sigma^{*}f,\sigma^{*}s)

The rest of this section lays out the splitting of the extension type formers, assuming all the necessary and the pre-established logical and model-categorical present in the background.

2
Global universe splitting

Depending on the precise setup—in particular with extra shape layers—one might have to use e.g. the local universe method
[
LW15
]
to strictify the
pseudo
-stable tope logic beforehand
[
RS17
, Definition A.7, Remark A.8]
. But we will neglect this here, as mentioned before, since we are staying inside the given model structure on the topos, so it is enough just to a consider a splitting of this.

A family in simplicial type theory is modeled by a composition

A\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\Phi\rightarrowtail\Xi,

where
\Phi\rightarrowtail\Xi
plays the role of a shape inclusion. Let
\pi:\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}\to\mathcal{U}
be the type-theoretic universe in
\mathrm{s}\mathscr{E}
(strictly à la Tarski, closed under all standard type formers) which classifies small Reedy fibrations, which exists by
[
Shu19
, Corollary 8.29]
. This means in particular we are considering the injective model structure on internal presheaves, which in the case of the base
coincides with the Reedy model structure, cf. also
[
Cis14
,
Shu15
]
.

We just have to split w.r.t. the universe
\pi:\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}\to\mathcal{U}
. This is done after Streicher
[
Str14a
, Appendix C]
and Voevodsky
[
Voe09
,
Voe17
]
in the following way:

Using meta-theoretic global choice, for each family
A:\Gamma\to\mathcal{U}
, we select a distinguished square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

A map from
B:\Delta\to\mathcal{U}
and
A:\Gamma:\Gamma\to\mathcal{U}
is given by a square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{A}}

which is split cartesian if and only if
B=A\circ\sigma
and
q_{B}=q_{A}\circ q_{\sigma}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

This then necessarily implies that the left-hand side is a pullback as well.
Since the identites involved are strict equalities between objects and morphisms involving distinguished squares, this models substitution up to equality on the nose by defining
\sigma^{*}A:=A[\sigma]:=A\circ\sigma
.

When defining the generic contexts à la
[
KL21
]
, we often define them in the internal extensional type theory (ETT) of the topos, as done in
[
Awo18
,
LW15
]
. In particular, given a map
f:B\to A
the endofunctor defined via the adjoint triple

\lx@nobreakspace\sum_{f}\dashv f^{*}\dashv\prod_{f}

as

\lx@nobreakspace P_{f}(X)=\sum_{A\to 1}\Big(\prod_{f}(B^{*}(X))\Big)

in the internal language ETT reads as

P_{f}(X)=\sum_{a:A}X^{B_{a}},

where
B_{a}=f^{*}a
.

Moreover, for
A:\Gamma\to\mathcal{U}
we allow ourselves to abbreviate
A:=\Gamma.A=\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{A}=\pi^{*}A
, as commonly done.

For the universal fibration
\pi:\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}\to\mathcal{U}
, the total object denotes the generic context

\lx@nobreakspace\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}=\sum_{a:\mathcal{U}}A=\llbracket A:\mathcal{U},a:A\rrbracket.

We show how to define a strictly stable choice of extension types à la
[
Awo18
, Proposition 2.4]
. To illustrate the analogy, we give a brief recollection how to define the generic contexts for the formation and introduction rule.

3
Recollection: Strictly stable
\Pi
-types

The generic context for the
\Pi
-formation rule is given, in the internal language, by the type

\mathcal{U}^{\Pi}:=\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{U}^{A}=\llbracket A:\mathcal{U},B:A\to\mathcal{U}\rrbracket,

and the generic context for the introduction rule is given by

\lx@nobreakspace\mathcal{U}^{\lambda\mathrm{@}j}:=\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}}\sum_{B:A\to\mathcal{U}}\prod_{a:A}B(a)=\llbracket A:\mathcal{U},B:A\to\mathcal{U},t:(a:A)\to B(a)\rrbracket,

which captures the
generic dependent term
.

The idea is that a map
\langle A,B\rangle:\Gamma\to\mathcal{U}^{\Pi}
precisely captures the input data for the
\Pi
-type former.

The universe then admits
\Pi
-types (cf.
\Pi
-structure
, cf.
[
KL21
, Definition 1.4.2, Theorem 1.4.15]
) if there exist maps
\Pi:\mathcal{U}^{\Pi}\to\mathcal{U}
,
\lambda:\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}\to\mathcal{U}

making the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}^{\Pi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

commute, and moreover rendering as a pullback.
These maps implement the formation and introduction rule, hence we take the maps

\lx@nobreakspace\Pi:\mathcal{U}^{\Pi}\to\mathcal{U},~\Pi(A,B):=\prod_{A}B,\quad\lx@nobreakspace\lambda:\mathcal{U}^{\Pi}\to\mathcal{U},~\lambda(A,B,t):=\Big\langle\prod_{A}(B),t\Big\rangle

induced from the structure of the ambient category as a type-theoretic model category.
11
11

11

Concretely, for fibrations
p_{A}:\Gamma.A\to\Gamma
,
p_{B}:\Gamma.A.B\to\Gamma.A
we define
\Pi(A,B):=\prod_{p_{A}}(p_{B})
via the pushforward functor
\prod_{p_{A}}\vdash(p_{A})^{*}
which in this setting preserves fibrations.

In particular, for the formation rule, strict stability under pullback follows from strict commutation of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}^{\Pi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle A,B\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{\sigma^{*}A}\sigma^{*}B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\sigma^{*}A,\sigma^{*}B\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{A}B}

as elaborated in
[
Awo18
]
.

We now treat the extension types from
[
RS17
]
in a similar fashion.

4
Strictly stable extension types: Generic contexts

Recall the rules from
[
RS17
, Figure 4]
.

To form an extension type, we start with a context fibered-over-shapes

\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\Phi\rightarrowtail\Xi

and a
separate
12
12

12

The semantic reason for this is explained right before
[
RS17
, Theorem A.17]
. Cf. also the explanation about the rules at the beginning of
[
RS17
, Section 2.2]
shape inclusion

\psi\rightarrowtail\varphi\rightarrowtail I.

The extension type ist then formed for a pair
\langle A,a\rangle
of a type and a partial section

A:\Gamma\times\psi\twoheadrightarrow\Phi\times\psi\rightarrowtail\Xi\times I,\quad a:\Gamma\times\varphi\to_{\Gamma\times\psi}A

(1)

In fact, the defining universal property for the extension types
[
RS17
, Definition A.10]
asks for substitution (pseudo-)stability of
A
and
a
along morphisms
\sigma:\Delta\to\Gamma
. Hence, we in fact consider a
family
of type formers
({\mathrm{Ext@}j})_{j:\varphi\rightarrowtail_{I}\psi}
indexed externally by the shape inclusions
j:\varphi\to\psi
, so that

({\mathrm{Ext@}j})(A,a)=\langle\Pi[\psi][A]|^{\varphi}_{a}\rangle.

Thus, the input data to form the extension type
{\mathrm{Ext@}j}
should be represented using a suitable generic context
\mathcal{U}^{{\mathrm{Ext@}j}}
as a morphism

\langle A,a\rangle:\lx@nobreakspace\Gamma\to\mathcal{U}^{{\mathrm{Ext@}j}}

with
\langle A,a\rangle
as in (
1
).
13
13

13

Note that we are suppress the further structure
\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\Phi\rightarrowtail\Xi
here, in line with
[
RS17
, Deftinition A.10]
.

In analogy to the case of
\Pi
-types, we form the generic contexts by

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{{\mathrm{Ext@}j}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}^{\psi}}j^{*}A,

(2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{{\lambda\mathrm{@}j}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}^{\psi}}\sum_{a:j^{*}A}a^{*}(A^{\psi}).

(3)

5
Strictly stable extension types: Formation and introduction

The formation and introduction rule are, top to bottom, Rule 1 and 2, resp., in
[
RS17
, Figure 4]
.

Then, at stage
\Gamma
the object
\mathcal{U}^{{{\lambda\mathrm{@}j}}}
consists of triples
\langle A,a,b\rangle
as in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma\times\varphi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma\times\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

i.e.
strictly
a=b\circ(\Gamma\times j)
. Considering the projection
\mathcal{U}^{{{\lambda\mathrm{@}j}}}\to\mathcal{U}^{{\mathrm{Ext@}j}}
the fiber at an instance
\langle A,a\rangle
is exactly the semantic extension type

{\mathrm{Ext@}j}(A,a):=\langle\Pi[\psi][A]|^{\varphi}_{a}\rangle

defined by the
split
14
14

14

i.e.

\langle\Pi[\psi][A]|^{\varphi}_{a}\rangle
and its projection to
\Gamma
have been
chosen
cartesian square, after
[
RS17
, Theorem A.16]
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\Pi[\psi][A]|^{\varphi}_{a}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\psi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\varphi}\times_{(\Gamma\times\psi)^{\varphi}}(\Gamma\times\psi)^{\psi}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle a,\eta\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

In particular, the right vertical map is a fibration,
15
15

15

If shapes are taken to be fibrant, this already follows from type-theoretic-ness of the model structure. Otherwise one would have to use that the model structure is cartesian monoidal as in
[
RS17
, Lemma A.4]
.
so the object
{\mathrm{Ext@}j}(A,a)
is fibrant.

Note that in the extensional type theory of the ambient topos one could describe the extension type also as the
\Sigma
-type

\lx@nobreakspace\sum_{b:\psi\to A}(b\circ j\equiv a),

where
(-_{1})\equiv(-_{2})
stands for the extensional identity type.

Now, in analogy to
[
KV18
, Theorem 1.4.15]
, we define the map
{\mathrm{Ext@}j}:\mathcal{U}^{{\lambda\mathrm{@}j}}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}
by the universal property of the extension type. The generic context
\mathcal{U}^{{\lambda\mathrm{@}j}}
consists of
\langle A,a,b\rangle
s.t.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\psi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\varphi}\times_{{\Gamma\times\psi}^{\varphi}}(\Gamma\times\psi)^{\psi}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle a,\eta\rangle}

where
\eta:\Gamma\to(\Gamma\times\psi)^{\psi}
denotes the transpose of the identity of
\Gamma\times\psi
. Now, we define the generic
\lambda
-term of the extension type as the gap map
({{\lambda\mathrm{@}j}})(b):=\widehat{b}
of the pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\Pi[\psi][A]|^{\varphi}_{a}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\psi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\varphi}\times_{{\Gamma\times\psi}^{\varphi}}(\Gamma\times\psi)^{\psi}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle a,\eta\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Then, the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}^{{\lambda\mathrm{@}j}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{Ext@}j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Ext@}j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lambda\mathrm{@}j}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

being a pullback precisely captures the universal property as dicussed in
[
RS17
, Definition A.10, Theorem A.16]
. In the terminology of
[
KL21
, Theorem 2.3.4]
this means that the projection
\mathcal{U}^{{{\lambda\mathrm{@}j}}}\to\mathcal{U}^{{\mathrm{Ext@}j}}
is the
universal dependent extension type
over
\mathcal{U}^{{\mathrm{Ext@}j}}
. In particular, in presence of the splitting it is a
chosen
small fibration. Thus, as illustrated e.g. in
[
Awo18
, Remark 2.6]
we obtain strict pullback stability: everything in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}^{{\mathrm{Ext@}j}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle A,a\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Ext@}j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\mathrm{Ext@}j})(\sigma^{*}A,\sigma^{*}a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\sigma^{*}A,\sigma^{*}a\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\mathrm{Ext@}j})(A,a)}

commutes strictly on the nose because we have split the model structure from the get-go, yielding as desired

{\mathrm{Ext@}j}(A,a)\circ\sigma={\mathrm{Ext@}j}(A\sigma,a\sigma).

Similarly, and using that the generic lifts
b
are given by gap maps of (strict) pullbacks we obtain

{\lambda\mathrm{@}j}(A,a,b)\circ\sigma={\mathrm{Ext@}j}(A\sigma,a\sigma,b\sigma).

6
Strictly stable extension types: Elimination and computation

The elimination and computation rule are Rule 3 and 4, resp., in
[
RS17
, Figure 4]
.

To interpet the elimination rule, note first that the exponential
A^{\psi}
comes with a (chosen) evaluation map:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\psi}\times_{\Gamma}(\Gamma\times\psi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}}

By pulling this back along the (chosen) map from the extension type, we obtain a map

\mathrm{ev}:{\mathrm{Ext@}j}(A,a)\times_{\Gamma}(\Gamma\times\psi)\to_{\Gamma}A,

fibered over
\Gamma
. Now,we want to define application
{\mathrm{app@}j}
of a function
f:\Gamma\to_{\Gamma}{\mathrm{Ext@}j}(A,a)
to a section
s:\Gamma\to_{\Gamma}\Gamma\times\psi
. Analogously to
[
KL21
, Theorem 1.4.15]
, we take the composition:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{Ext@}j}(A,a)\times_{\Gamma}(\Gamma\times\psi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle f,s\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{app@}j}(f,s)}

This validates the rule, since
{\mathrm{app@}j}(f,s):\Gamma\to_{\Gamma}A
, as desired.
For substitution along
\sigma:\Delta\to\Gamma
, consider the following diagram involving chosen fibrations and split cartesian squares:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{Ext@}j}(\sigma^{*}A,\sigma^{*}a)\mathrlap{\times_{\Delta}(\Delta\times\psi)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathrm{Ext@}j}(A,a)\times_{\Gamma}(\Gamma\times\psi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sigma^{*}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{app@}j}(\sigma^{*}f,\sigma^{*}s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\sigma^{*}f,\sigma^{*}a\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{app@}j}(f,s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle f,a\rangle}

This uniquely defines the map
{\mathrm{app@}j}(\sigma^{*}f,\sigma^{*}s)
, and by construction

\sigma^{*}{\mathrm{app@}j}(f,s)={\mathrm{app@}j}(\sigma^{*}f,\sigma^{*}s).\lx@nobreakspace

Furthermore the desired computation rule holds, saying that for a term
s
in the smaller tope
\varphi
, one judgmentally has
{\mathrm{app@}j}(f,s)\equiv a[s]
. This is established by the commutation of the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma\times\varphi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma\times\psi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{app@}j}(f,s)}

The
\beta
- and
\eta
-rule (cf.
[
RS17
, Figure 4]
) can be proven to hold similarl (Rule 5 and 6, resp., in
[
RS17
, Figure 4]
).

Chapter 10
Conclusion and Outlook

1
Conclusion

1
Synthetic cocartesian fibrations

We have developed, also in previous joint work with Ulrik Buchholtz
[
BW21
]
, a theory of co-/cartesian fibrations of synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categories in simplicial HoTT due to
[
RS17
]
. This generalizes concepts and results from
op. cit.
to the non-discrete setting.

Our account follows
[
RV22
, Chapter 5]
and indeed includes characterization theorems for cocartesian arrows, fibrations, and functors, in terms of left adjoint right inverse
aka
Chevalley conditions. These serve to prove several closure properties, resembling the axioms of an
\infty
-cosmos. Thanks to the Chevalley criteria, a portion of these generalizes to Leibniz cotensors w.r.t. to
arbitrary
type maps or shape inclusions.
1
1

1

One should also compare this to the
\infty
-cosmoses of LARI and RARI adjunctions in
[
RV22
]
.

2
Synthetic bicartesian fibrations and Moens’ Theorem

We have given a basic account of Beck–Chevalley bifibrations, leading up to Moens’ Theorem, translating Streicher’s developments and proofs
[
Str21
, Chapter 15]
from the analytical
1
-dimensional to the synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categorical setting. This constitutes an application of our synthetic theory to prove a technically more involved theorem of fibered category theory à la Bénabou. To our knowledge, Moens’ Theorem for
(\infty,1)
-categories has not been considered previously (in the analytical setting).

3
Synthetic two-sided cartesian fibrations

We have introduced synthetic two-sided cartesian fibrations, again in the spirit of
[
RV22
, Chapter 5]
. This includes a systematic development of the notion, proving characterizations and closure properties in a modular way. Along the way, we have considered various (auxiliary) of “fibered” or “sliced fibrations”. Our study leads up to a synthetic version of Riehl–Verity’s two-sided Yoneda Lemma.

One might argue that this treatment is unwieldy at times, and might be more elegantly done in a setting with the appropriate categorical universes at hand, cf.
Section

2
. However, it seems to be a general principle of simplicial HoTT that to be order to be build up the universe inside the theory in the first place, one has to understand the respective fibrations before.

4
Semantics

We have proved a coherence theorem for the extension types of
[
RS17
]
, relative to the interpretation of simplicial HoTT in any
\infty
-topos of simplicial objects,
[
Shu19
, Section 8]
. The method is the well-known “global universe splitting” related to Giraud’s left adjoint splitting, used previously in works on the semantics of HoTT, such as
[
KL21
,
LW15
,
Awo18
]
. This was hinted at but left out in
[
RS17
]
. The coherence construction added on top of the groundwork from
[
RS17
, Appendix A]
and
[
Shu19
, Section 8]
implies that indeed simplicial HoTT has models in simplicial objects internal to an arbitrary Grothendieck–Rezk–Lurie
\infty
-topos.

5
sHoTT as a synthetic language for internal
(\infty,1)
-categories

Consequently, the Rezk types get interpreted as Rezk objects in the respective
(\infty,1)
-topos. In particular, those also form an
\infty
-cosmos, capturing the formal category theory of (internal)
(\infty,1)
-categories. The cosmological notions in
[
RV22
]
are formulated in terms of certain constructions and notions, such as (relative) adjunctions/absolute lifting diagrams, and (fibered) equivalences of modules. Importantly, these notions are invariant under equivalences of
\infty
-cosmoses, hence are suitable to capture the respective internal formal
\infty
-category. Now, by means of the preestablished standard interpretation of sHoTT, one sees that our internal notions translate exactly to the desired analytical counterparts. E.g., cocartesian fibrations are expressed through a LARI condition, and (LARI) adjunctions can be defined as a fibered equivalence of modules/hom objects. This also matches up with previous model-categorical investigations, e.g. in
[
Ras21
]
.

Therefore, sHoTT provides an expressive and convenient tool for reasoning about internal
(\infty,1)
-categories synthetically, at least with regard to the fibrational theory. It notably profits from the relations to
\infty
-cosmos theory, both for the internal and external theory.

However, there are also principle difficulties and obstructions at play. Homotopy-invariance is forced,
i.e.
“discrete” constructions are forbidden. In the face of countermodels, a native development of categorical universes and the ensuing Grothendieck construction does not seem possible. In general, it seems rather tricky in this theory to define concrete Rezk types as opposed to abstract ones, constructed out of anonymous given ones.

Also notably, the elementary feature of
opposite categories
seems subtle to implement.

We present a few of our perspectives to remedy these shortcomings in the next section.

2
Outlook

1
Discrete two-sided fibrations

Natural follow-up work for this thesis includes a dedicated discussion of discrete two-sided fibrations,
aka

\infty
-profunctors or (bi-)modules. We expect to be able to prove an analogous characterization as discrete fibrations of fibrations. This should, in the synthetic setting, imply the expected cosmological closure properties, and also additional operations, known from the
virtual equipment
of modules due to
[
RV22
]
. Finally, we make some considerations on the closure properties of synthetic
\infty
-distributors in particular. Externally, the distributors internal to an arbitrary
\infty
-cosmos form a kind of double-category, encompassing the (formal)
\infty
-category theory of the
\infty
-cosmos.
2
2

2

This even has an associated internal language à la Makkai’s FOLDS
[
RV22
, Section 11.2]
.
This philosophy is a cornerstone of
\infty
-cosmos theory and the Model Independence Theorem. Then, also together with a first consideration of relative adjunctions in this thesis, possible applications could notably include a theory of Kan extensions. It would also be conceivable to develop, a calculus of modules in sHoTT, after
[
RV17a
]
, even though for this purpose working in one categorical dimension up might be more suitable.

2
Categorical universes and modalities

The next step in developing this synthetic theory of fibered
(\infty,1)
-categories is the treatment of categorical
universes
, or
object classifiers
, and the analogue of the
completion operation
introduced by Rezk in
[
Rez01
]
, which completes a general Segal type to a Rezk type. In unpublished joint work with Ulrik Buchholtz we have shown that there are models in which simply restricting the universe to the canonical subtype of small
\infty
-groupoids, or small Segal types, resp., does not yield a Segal type again, cf.
[
BW18
,
BW21
]
. The goal is to establish a hierarchy of categorical universes within simplicial type theory, as previously done in various settings, notably by Ayala–Francis
[
AF20
]
and Rasekh
[
Ras18
]
.

We expect the following construction to work. In the first step, using techniques from
Cubical Homotopy Type Theory

[
LOPS18
,
CCHM18
]
we can, for suitable synthetic notions
F
of fibration, define a type classifying small fibrations of flavor
F
. This requires a further extension of sHoTT, which is however justified by the semantics in simplicial spaces. Mainly, one internalizes the structure of
{\mathrm{sSpace}}
as a
cohesive
(\infty,1)
-topos
over
{\mathrm{Space}}
, by adding new type formers (
modalities
)
[
Shu18
]
for (synthetic) higher versions of Lawvere’s
axiomatic cohesion

[
Law07
]
. It also requires to embed the interpreting
\infty
-topos of simplicial spaces into the
\infty
-topos of
cubical spaces
, as discussed (for the
1
-topos case) in my joint note
[
SW21
]
with Thomas Streicher, building upon work by Sattler
[
Sat18
]
and Kapulkin–Voevodsky
[
KV18
]
.

An analogous method has also been used in
[
WL20
]
ultimately constructing universes satisfying directed univalence, but with a different approach to the structural analysis along the way.

In our setting, we expect connections to a synthetic version of
flat
or
(\infty,1)
-Conduché
fibrations.
3
3

3

I am grateful to David Ayala, Aaron Mazel-Gee, Emily Riehl, and Jay Shah for pointing me to the flat fibrations and discussions revolving around them.

One should note that despite extending sHoTT even further, the additions still preserve the intrinsic character of the theory because the cohesive structure is present in the standard model
{\mathrm{sSpace}}
. Ultimately, we are aiming for a description of the categorical structure of the universes, and a version of the
\infty
-Grothendieck construction (
aka
straightening/unstraightening) in terms of the cohesive structure. This should be contrasted to e.g. the works of Lurie, Riehl–Verity, and Rezk which at times import the machinery of simplicially enriched categories, or Cisinski–Nguyen, who use model structures on
marked
simplicial sets.

This has been joint ongoing work with Ulrik Buchholtz
[
BW18b
,
BW19
]
.

Combining this with the theory of two-sided cartesian fibrations from this thesis, one should also aim for synthetic higher categorical universes of spans.

3
Opposites, twisted arrows, and classical Yoneda Lemma

It is natural to ask for an operation giving for any Rezk type
A
its
opposite
Rezk type
A^{\Op}
. It has turned to be formally very delicate to introduce opposites into a dependent type theory. However, based on
multimodal type theory
due to Licata–Riley–Shulman
[
LSR19
]
and Gratzer–Kavvos–Nuyts–Birkedal
[
GKNB20
,
GKNB20a
]
we have outlined rules and a semantics, more generally, capturing fibered modalities induced by operations on simplices. This in particular includes a modal operator yielding the type
\tw_{A}
of twisted arrows.

Combining the work on fibrations and universes with the new modal extension is expected to yield as a prime application the more “traditional” Yoneda Lemma and Yoneda embedding
A\to(A^{\Op}\to\mathrm{Space})
, using the twisted arrow fibrations, after Kazhdan–Varshavsky
[
KV14
]
. This has been joint ongoing work with Ulrik Buchholtz
[
BW18a
]
.

4
Synthetic higher algebra

In the longer run, with most of the theory described in the previous paragraph established, we can turn to more advanced topics in synthetic fibered
(\infty,1)
-category theory. Specifically, we have in mind symmetric monoidal
(\infty,1)
-categories and
\infty
-operads, both of which can be defined in fibrational language, as done e.g. by Lurie
[
Lur17
]
.

Another direction is to extend our notions of synthetic
\infty
-categories and cocartesian fibrations to an equivariant setting, hence providing an analogue to Barwick
et al.
’s program
[
BDGNS16
]
of equivariant or
parametrized
\infty
-category theory
. The parts of higher group theory already developed in HoTT to this date will be crucial here, notably
[
BvDR18
,
BBDG+21
]
.

5
Synthetic
(\infty,2)
- and
(\infty,n)
-categories and fibrations

In light of the remarkable recent developments of two-dimensional higher category theory, we might hope to extend the syntax and semantics to the
(\infty,2)
-level, working e.g. in
2
-fold complete Segal spaces or
\Theta_{2}
-spaces. Since then objects of our theory would then be
(\infty,2)
-categories we might hope to develop some parts of
\infty
-comos theory inside the theory. However, to get a reasonable grip on this, it seems instructive to achieve the aforementioned constructions of categorical universes first in the
(\infty,1)
-dimensional case. E.g., a two-dimensional theory should support a general pasting principle (both strictly operationally, and internally for the respective
(\infty,2)
-categorical universe types) à la
[
HORR21
]
, and for this purpose directed univalence seems indispensable. Another interesting direction would be “more natively” develop a calculus of modules and other pieces of formal
\infty
-category theory in this two-dimensional setting. Thus, there are several reasons to be interested specifically in a version of sHoTT for synthetic
(\infty,2)
-categories. As shown in the work of the notions of cartesian fibrations also become more intricate. We hope that our “shape-independent” account of LARI fibrations and LARI cells could provide useful here, cf.
[
BW21
]
and
Section

1
.

More generally, as suggested by
[
RS17
]
another direction of study would be a generalization to a type theory of
(\infty,n)
-categories, e.g. inside
\Theta_{n}
-spaces using an appropriate shape theory, cf. also the recent work by Rasekh on fibrations of
(\infty,n)
-categories
[
Ras21a
]
.

In the light of close connections of simplicial type theory with cubical type theory, and the recent analytic results about cubical notions of weak higher categories, one could also hope for mutual new insights and connections between the analytic and type-theoretic side.

6
Higher topos theory

In
[
Str21
,
Str97
]
Streicher lays out an analysis of geometric morphisms in terms of fibered
1
-category theory, recalling and extending work by Bénabou, Moens, and Jibladze. One of the central results is Jibladze’s Theorem which says that locally small, cocomplete fibered toposes over a fixed base (elementary) topos
\mathscr{B}
are given up to equivalence as
Artin gluings

F^{*}\partial_{1}:\mathscr{E}/F\to\mathscr{B}
of some geometric morphism
F:\mathscr{B}\to{\mathscr{E}}
(identified with its inverse image part).

The notion of fibered topos considered here implies that the reindexings are
logical
functors,
i.e.
preserve the subobject classifiers.

It would be intriguing to analyze this and related results for higher toposes. First, generalizing this notion of fibered topos naturally exhibits the homotopy level as a parameter, since in a higher topos we are dealing with
object
classifiers for arbitrary
n
-types, for
-1\leq n\leq\infty
(with
n=-1
recovering the subobject classifier, which is the universe of propositions).

Second, it could be worthwile to consider more general notions than sheaf
(\infty,1)
-toposes, such as the proposed
elementary
(\infty,1)
-toposes
after Shulman
[
Shu18
]
and Rasekh
[
Ras18a
,
Ras22
]
.

A first step in this direction has been taken in this thesis. We have established a form of Moens’ Lemma for synthetic
(\infty,1)
-categories, hence it holds in the intended models as well. This constitutes one of several examples of how the synthetic theory can be a helpful tool for the analytical theory as well, cf. e.g.
[
ABFJ20
]
.

Furthermore, our treatment of BCC and Moens fibrations suggests that some more fibered category theory à la
[
Str21
]
can be developed inside our synthetic setting, including e.g. geometric fibrations. This could nicely complement the analytical treatment.

7
Other type theories and implementations

A different but related overall approach to higher categories in type theory is given by
2-level type theory (2LTT)

[
ACKS19
,
Cap17
]
, based on Voevodsky’s Higher Type System. In contrast, 2LTT seems closer to a (possible) foundational theory, whereas simplicial HoTT could be seen rather as a domain-specific language (DSL). Thus, it would be natural to provide an interpretation of sHoTT in a suitable 2-level type theory, also opening up possibilities of software-implementation.

On the other hand, there is an ongoing development of a new proof-assistant called rzk by Nikolai Kudasov
[
Kud21
]
that supports simplicial type theory. Since we are seeking to extend our type theory by (multi-)modalities, which also (in part) have implementations, it would be most desirable to (eventually) achieve a high degree of modularity on both the theory as well as the practical implementation of the type theories at play.

Another approach, as sketched by Buchholtz
[
Buc21
]
, is to investigate the connections between sHoTT and “HoTT with a simplicial interval” as a less strict, more intensional replacement. One could ask how far along one gets with the latter, probably in the presence of additional induction principles but with no strict extension types.

Chapter 11
Relative adjunctions

Appendix 11.A
Relative adjunctions

Taking up a suggestion by Emily Riehl, we provide here a brief treatment of
relative adjunctions
in the sense of Ulmer
[
Ulm68
]
, cf. also
[
DHLL18
]
. The purpose is to provide a more formal account to cocartesian arrows, or more generally, LARI cells. As a payoff, we will see that the Chevalley condition defining the LARI cells implies the Chevalley condition for LARI fibrations in the sense of
[
BW21
]
, and likewise for LARI functors.

1
Definition and characterization

Definition 11.A.1

(Transposing relative adjunction)
.

Let
A,B,C
be Rezk types and
(g:C\to A\leftarrow B:f)
a cospan. A
(transposing) left relative adjunction
consists of a functor
\ell:C\to B
together with a fibered equivalence

\big(\ell\mathbin{\downarrow}B\simeq_{C\times B}g\mathbin{\downarrow}f\big)\simeq\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}c:C\\
b:B\end{subarray}}\hom_{B}(\ell\,c,b)\simeq\hom_{A}(g\,c,f\,b).

Given such data, we call
\ell
a
(transposing) left adjoint of
f
relative to
g
or
(transposing)
g
-left adjoint of
f
.

In case
C\equiv A
and
g\equiv\id_{A}
one obtains the usual notion of (transposing) adjunction. There also exists a relative analogue of the units. We might occasionally drop the predicate “left” in our discussion since we will only consider the left case. But beware that relative adjunctions are a genuinely asymmetric notion.

Definition 11.A.2

(Relative adjunction via units)
.

Let
A,B,C
be Rezk types and
(g:C\to A\leftarrow B:f)
a cospan. A
(transposing) left relative adjunction
consists of a functor
\ell:C\to B
together with a natural transformation
\eta:g\Rightarrow_{C\to A}f\ell
, called
relative unit
, such that transposition map

\Phi_{\eta}\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda b,c,k.fk\circ\eta_{c}:\ell\mathbin{\downarrow}B\to_{C\times B}g\mathbin{\downarrow}f

is a fiberwise equivalence.

By the characterizations about type-theoretic weak equivalences,
Definition

11.A.2
translates to:

\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}c:C\\
b:B\end{subarray}}\prod_{m:gc\to_{A}fb}\isContr\Big(\sum_{k:\ell\,c\to_{B}b}\Phi_{\eta}(k)=m\Big)

(1)

Diagrammatically, this can be depicted as follows, demonstrating once more the generalization from the usual notion of adjunction:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g\,c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\,b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\,\ell c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell\,c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\forall\,m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\,k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\,k}

It turns out that also in the synthetic setting we recover the equivalence of relative adjunctions with
absolute left lifting diagrams (ALLD)
, whose universal property in terms of pasting diagrams can be (informally or analytically) visualized as:
A lax diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

is an absolute lifting diagram if and only if any given lax square on the left factors uniquely as a pasting diagram as demonstrated below left:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\forall\,\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\,\mu^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

Accordingly we define this type-theoretically

1

1

For a first discussion about lax squares and pasting diagrams in sHoTT cf.
[
BW21

. We do currently not have a systematic account to these. E.g. certainly at some point a pasting theorem à la

as follows:

Definition 11.A.3

(Absolute left lifting diagram)
.

A diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

is an
absolute lifting diagram (ALLD)
if the following proposition is satisfied:

\lx@nobreakspace{\mathrm{isALLD}}_{\ell,f,g}(\eta)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}X:\mathcal{U}\\
\beta:X\to B\\
\gamma:X\to C\end{subarray}}\prod_{\mu:g\gamma\Rightarrow f\beta}\isContr\Big(\sum_{\mu^{\prime}:\ell\gamma\Rightarrow\beta}\prod_{x:X}(f\mu_{x}^{\prime}\circ\eta_{\gamma\,x}=_{g\gamma\,x\to f\beta\,x}\mu_{x})\Big)

Note that one can infer the data
\langle\ell,f,g\rangle
from
\eta
alone. We might also speak of
\eta
as an
absolute left lifting cell
.

Diagrammatically, the demanded identity of morphisms reads:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g(\gamma\,x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\ell(\gamma\,x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(\beta\,x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\gamma\,x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\mu_{x}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{x}}

The above definitions can be dualized to obtain relative
right
adjoints and absolute
right
lifting diagrams. Because of the inherent asymmetry some analogies to the case of ordinary adjunctions are missing (such as the presence of
both
units and counits). However, we can still provide a characterization and some closure results.

Theorem 11.A.4

(Characterizations of relative left adjunctions, cf.
[

, Thm. 3.5.8/3]

[

.

Let
A,B,C
be Rezk types and
(g:C\to A\leftarrow B:f)
a cospan. Then the following types are equivalent propositions:

1.

The type
\sum_{\ell:C\to B}\ell\mathbin{\downarrow}B\simeq_{C\times B}g\mathbin{\downarrow}f
of (transposing)
g
-left adjoints of
f
.

2.

The type
\sum_{\ell:C\to B}\sum_{\eta:g\Rightarrow f\ell}\isEquiv\big(\Phi_{\eta}\big)
with
\Phi
as in
Definition

11.A.2
.

3.

The type
\sum_{\ell:C\to B}\sum_{\eta:g\Rightarrow f\ell}{\mathrm{isALLD}}_{\ell,f,g}(\eta)
of completions of the cospan consisting of
f
and
g
to an ALLD.

Proof.

In parts we can work analogously as in the proof of
[

, Theorem 11.23]
. In particular, an equivalence between the types from
Item

1
and
Item

2
follows
2
2

2

The classical version is due to
[

, Lemma 2.7]
, and it works by the analogous argument.
just as in
loc. cit.
by using the (covariant discrete) Yoneda Lemma
[

, Section 9, and (11.9)]
. Next, analogously as in the proof of
[

, Theorem 11.23]
one also shows that, given
\ell:C\to B
, the type
\sum_{\eta:g\Rightarrow f\ell}\isEquiv\big(\Phi_{\eta}\big)
is a proposition.

We now fix
\ell:C\to B
and
\eta:g\Rightarrow f\ell
. Recall (

). The direction from

follows by setting

. Conversely, we see that we get from

by “reindexing” Condition (

) along any given span

.
3
3

3

More precisely, we use the fact that, given a family of propositions
P:A\to{\mathrm{Prop}}
, there is an equivalence
\Phi:\prod_{a:A}P(a)\simeq\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}X:\mathcal{U}\\
\alpha:X\to A\end{subarray}}\prod_{x:X}P(\alpha,x):\Psi
. We can take
\Phi(\sigma)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda X,\alpha,x.\sigma(\alpha\,x)
and
\Psi(\tau)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\lambda a.\tau(\mathbf{1})(a)({\mathrm{pt}})
.

∎

Corollary 11.A.5
.

Given a cospan
(g:C\to A\leftarrow B:f)
, if both
\ell,\ell^{\prime}:C\to B
are left adjoints to
f
relative to
g
, then there is an identity
\ell=\ell^{\prime}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

We write
{\ell\,{}^{(g)}\dashv f}
if it exists.

Definition 11.A.6

(Relative LARI adjunction)
.

A relative left adjunction is called
relative LARI adjunction
if its relative unit is invertible.

Appendix 11.B
LARI cells, fibrations, and functors

1
LARI cells

Let
j:\Phi\hookrightarrow\Psi
be a shape inclusion. Let
B
be a Rezk type and
P:B\to\mathcal{U}
be an isoinner family. For its unstraightening
\pi:E\twoheadrightarrow B
the diagram induced by expoentiation is given through:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathllap{E^{\Psi}\simeq\sum_{\langle u,f\rangle:\Phi^{E}}}\sum_{v:\left\langle\Psi\to B\middle|^{\Phi}_{u}\right\rangle}\left\langle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{\Psi}}{\textstyle\prod_{\Psi}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{\Psi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{\Psi}}v^{*}P\middle|^{\Phi}_{f}\right\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\Phi}\simeq\sum_{u:\Phi\to B}\mathrlap{\prod_{\Phi}u^{*}P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathllap{B^{\Psi}\simeq\sum_{u:\Phi\to B}}\left\langle\Psi\to B\middle|^{\Phi}_{u}\right\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\Phi}}}

An element
v:\Psi\to B
is to be understood as
\Psi
-shaped cell
(or
diagram
) in the type
B
. A section
g:\prod_{t:\Psi}P(v(t))
is a
dependent
\Psi
-shaped cell (over
v
)
in the family
P
.

Definition 11.B.1

(
j
-LARI cell)
.

Let
g:\Psi\to E
be a
\Psi
-shaped cell in
E
, lying over
\langle u,v,f\rangle
with
u:\Phi\to B
,
v:\Psi\to B
, and
f:\Phi\to E
(both the latter lying over
u
). We call
g
a
j
-LARI cell
if the ensuing canonical commutative diagram

is an absolute left lifting diagram,

\displaystyle{\mathrm{isLariCell}}_{j}^{P}(g)\mathrel{\vcentcolon\equiv}\isEquiv\big(\Phi_{{\mathrm{refl}}}\big)

Unpacking this, after contracting away redundant data, yields

\displaystyle{\mathrm{isLariCell}}_{j}(g)\simeq\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\langle r,w,k,m\rangle:\Psi\to E\\

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle:\hom_{\Phi\to B}(u,r),

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle:\left\langle(\Delta^{1}\times\Psi)\to B\middle|^{b_{1}\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}j}_{[\langle v,w\rangle,\alpha_{1}]}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle:\prod_{\langle t,s\rangle:\Delta^{1}\times\Phi}P\big(\alpha_{1}\,t\,s\big)
